Candidate Dinner at Claud
Confrérie des Chevaliers du Tastevin · Candidate dinner · Claud, New York · 26 October 2026

Candidate Dinner at Claud

A dossier on thirteen wines in two acts — a Meursault investigation in white, and a whole-cluster study in red: the climats, the parcels, the cellars, the vintages, the people.

A · Reception — Leflaive, Meursault 1er Cru Sous le Dos d'Âne 2019

Domaine Leflaive · Sous le Dos d'Âne

Meursault 1er Cru · 2019
Blagny hillside · Puligny house · the domaine's only Meursault
Climat
5.03 ha, entirely 1er cru; below La Pièce sous le Bois, on the "donkey's back" hillock facing Perrières; ~300 m, SE-facing, clay-limestone/marl. Ranked 1ère classe in 1860, level with Perrières. HIGH
Parcel
1.62 ha (Wilson Daniels fact sheets 2021–24) vs 1.18 ha (leflaive.fr 2024) — primary sources disagree; 1.62 is the better-supported total. Single parcel. MED
Vines
Chardonnay planted 1995 and 2004. Pinot Noir here until 2002 (Leflaive bottled a red Blagny 1er Cru SDA through 2001). 15- and 24-year-old vines in 2019. HIGH
Farming
Ecocert 1992; Biodyvin 1998; whole domaine biodynamic from 1997. Horse ploughing documented for GCs and Pucelles, not confirmed here.
Cellar
Slow pneumatic press (new press 2018), 24 h settling; AF + MLF in oak; ~20–22% new, mostly 400-litre barrels — unique in the Leflaive range (Nanson 2018, 2019); bâtonnage only until malo starts; 12 months barrel + 6–10 months steel on fine lees; light fining/filtration if needed; DIAM 30 since the 2014 vintage. HIGH
2019
Harvest from 10/11 Sept; 38 hl/ha (−30%); 14–14.5% alc; pH ~3.1 "like a cold year"; bottled ~Apr–May 2021. Sunburn in this parcel. HIGH
Scores
Burghound 90–93 · Kelley/WA 92 ("Best after 2025") · Neal Martin 92 · Wine Spectator 93 · Nanson: "this wine is BACK!"
JancisRobinson.com
The 2019 has not been reviewed on JancisRobinson.com (the cuvée is covered 2006–2018 and 2020). Her running note on the site, repeated each vintage from Eric Rémy: "Next to Perrières… each year brings more and more minerality"; on the 2008 (Nov 2009): the last of the parcel was planted to Chardonnay in 2004, "their first vintage was 2000, having started the replanting in 1995." Domaine 2018 (Julia Harding, 17): "Crushed rocks and a touch of struck match… Tight, coiled, intense and long. Fabulous premier cru."
The thirty-second version

A Puligny house making Meursault in the Puligny idiom, from a Blagny slope that looks down on Perrières. The parcel was red until 2002. Élevage is the one place Leflaive breaks its own pattern — big 400-litre barrels instead of pièces. The 2019 is the vintage Bill Nanson said the cuvée was "back" after a decade in which the domaine underperformed, and it is a heat-and-sunburn year that still shows pH 3.1 — Pierre Vincent's argument for biodynamic soils. Open it an hour ahead: the house is reductive.

The climat

Seven Blagny climats straddle the Meursault/Puligny line; the four in Meursault (La Jeunelotte, La Pièce sous le Bois, Sous Blagny, Sous le Dos d'Âne) give Meursault 1er Cru when white and Blagny 1er Cru when red. The Cistercians of Maizières — the "Montrachet fathers" — worked the hamlet from the 12th century. The Château de Blagny has belonged to the Montlivault family since 1811, whose heirs (Martelet/de Cherisey, de Bréchard) and Louis Latour are the other names in the climat; Chapelle de Blagny keeps 1.00 ha of Pinot here, the last documented red. Stylistically it is Perrières' cooler, higher, clayier neighbour — Neal Martin on the 2019: "a very Puligny-like bouquet"; a retailer on Olivier Leflaive's version: "a Meursault that's almost a Puligny."

The cellar and the premox story

This bottle sits on the right side of the domaine's worst chapter. Leflaive was "one of the six most reliable white burgundies you could buy" through 2005, then had catastrophic premature oxidation in 2006–08 (Cornwell's Oxidized-Burgundies wiki; Tim Atkin; Raymond Blake). Brice de La Morandière's first big decision on taking over in 2015 was DIAM across the range from the 2014 vintage — announced to the trade via Corney & Barrow in October 2016 — and his second was hiring Pierre Vincent (from Domaine de la Vougeraie, Jan 2017). Vincent's régime: bâtonnage only to the onset of malo, no movement of unfinished wine between buildings, new press in 2018, six months in steel after barrel ("essential for pure, clean flavors"), and — in his own words about his own domaine — "Meursault foie gras is not my style." Nanson (2018): "Pierre has never openly been a proponent of DIAM" — he inherited the closure. Vincent left after the 2024 harvest for his own Domaine Pierre Vincent in Auxey; Amandine Brillanceau (ex-Jadot cellar master) took over in January 2025. The 2019 is squarely a Vincent wine, his third vintage — the trio where Nanson, Jasper Morris and Blake all date the turnaround.

2019 in the domaine's own words

"Not a crazy vintage despite the high level of sugar, because we have fantastic acidity… The one problem is a reduction of 30% in volume… We had 5 frosty nights, oïdium pressure followed by the June heatwave and the July heatwave and hot harvesting conditions… tiny berries, many without pips."Pierre Vincent to Bill Nanson, 7 Oct 2019
"We started our harvesting 11 September and this was a hot period too – the grapes gained 1 degree in 3 days… pH 3.1 like a cold year – despite the alcohol of a hot year. Sugar and acidity – I think they will live long."Pierre Vincent to Nanson, 29 Mar 2021

Kelley (Jan 2022, 92): "a touch reductive out of the gates… crisp green orchard fruit, hazelnuts, dried white flowers and smoke. Medium to full-bodied, ample and satiny, with lively acids and a long, saline finish… Best after 2025." Neal Martin (92): "quite edgy on the opening… a tensile, stem ginger-tinged finish." Burghound (90–93): "linear, youthfully austere and bone-dry… at least a few years of patience." October 2026 is an early but legitimate window.

People and hooks

  • Anne-Claude Leflaive (1956–2015): got François Bouchet's number from Aubert de Villaine in 1989 after Claude Bourguignon's soil seminar; ran Clavoillon trials; the clincher was a blind tasting of two 1996 Clavoillons for journalists where "everyone preferred the biodynamically grown one." Decanter Woman of the Year 2006; IMW Winemakers' Winemaker 2014. Co-founded the École du Vin et des Terroirs with de Villaine — a direct thread to the C3 flight.
  • Pierre Morey: Leflaive's régisseur 1988–July 2008; farms the 0.52 ha of Perrières directly below this parcel; lost his Lafon métayage (Perrières, Charmes, Montrachet) when Dominique took the vines back — so he links A, B3 and the Lafon story. He made the first white SDAs (2002–07).
  • The domaine dates to 1717; Joseph Leflaive (Polytechnicien, naval engineer) assembled 25 ha from 1905 in the phylloxera trough; the 1973 SCE holds ~40 family shareholders together. Brice's line: "we make a living from what we offer rather than what we take."
  • Not found: CellarTracker's 2019 page could not be read (bot-blocked), so the community reception of this vintage is unverified here.
Established
  • Leflaive's only Meursault, from a Blagny slope that looks down on Perrières; the parcel was red until 2002 — the domaine bottled a Blagny 1er Cru from it through 2001.
  • The one cuvée raised in 400-litre barrels (~20–22% new); DIAM 30 from the 2014 vintage, announced to the trade through Corney & Barrow after the 2006–08 premox disaster.
  • 2019: picked from 10/11 Sept at ~38 hl/ha (−30%), 14–14.5% alcohol yet pH 3.1 "like a cold year" — Pierre Vincent's argument for biodynamic soils. Sunburn in this parcel.
  • Anne-Claude Leflaive got François Bouchet's number from Aubert de Villaine in 1989; the biodynamic clincher was a blind tasting of two 1996 Clavoillons where the journalists all preferred the biodynamic one — a thread to the DRC flight.
  • Pierre Morey, Leflaive's régisseur 1988–2008, made the first white SDAs and farms the Perrières directly below this parcel — he ties the reception wine to B3.
Unresolved
  • Parcel size: 1.62 ha (importer sheets) vs 1.18 ha (domaine site) — the primary sources disagree.
  • Whether horse ploughing extends to this parcel (documented for the grands crus and Pucelles only).
  • How the 2019 is drinking now: no JancisRobinson.com review, CellarTracker unread; every critic's window starts at 2025 or later.
  • Who chose the 400-litre format for this cuvée and when — Nanson records it, nobody explains it.

B1 · The Great Village Hillside — Roulot Tessons vs Coche-Dury Rougeots, 2011

Two adjoining lieux-dits on the north slope above the old village, at roughly the altitude of the southern premiers crus, farmed by cousins. Lavalle (1855) ranked Tessons première cuvée — level with the crus that became premiers crus — and Rougeots deuxième. Patrick Essa: "Le classement des parcelles, en dehors des premiers crus n'existent pas, pourtant il est d'usage de considérer certaines d'entre elles comme de véritables seconds crus." This lieu-dit labelling of village wine is, he says, "un fait quasiment unique dans la Côte des blancs" — and Guy Roulot and René Lafon started it in the 1960s.

Domaine Roulot · Les Tessons, Clos de Mon Plaisir

Meursault · 2011
Now labelled "À Mon Plaisir, Clos du Haut Tesson"
Climat
~5.3 ha (Lavalle 5.29), nine owners; full east, ~260 m, directly above the village — Essa: it faces "the village church steeple." Ferruginous red-brown clay-limestone; dotted with stone cabottes. Lavalle 1855 première cuvée; Rodier 1920 deuxième. HIGH
Parcel
0.85 ha walled clos in the upper Tesson; planted 1951 and 1961 (50–60-year-old vines in 2011); assembled by Guy Roulot in the 1950s–60s. Only walled monopole within the climat. HIGH
Farming
Herbicide-free from ~1989; organic from ~1999–2000; Ecocert certified from the 2013 vintage. Jean-Marc on going organic: "I had no choice."
Cellar
Light foulage then pressing at 0.2 bar (75% of juice), last 10% hyperoxidised; overnight settling, turbid; indigenous ferment in barrel; 15–20% new, Damy; light bâtonnage only until malo; ~12 months barrel then 6–7 months stainless on lees; fined casein/bentonite "eight of ten times," 5 µ filtration; bottled Feb–Apr 2013. HIGH
2011
Roulot's full-crop reference year (later losses are quoted "versus our reference of 2011"). Jean-Marc (2024): "I prefer it to my 2010, which had botrytis. 2011 is a great vintage." First vintage with the Clos des Bouchères monopole. HIGH
Scores
Tanzer 91–92 · Burghound 92 · Marsh (2024) "aged better than initially expected… lovely now" · Wilkie 91 · CT ~91
JancisRobinson.com
Three notes on this bottle. Nov 2012, 17+: "Broad and muscular. This has been lifting weights!" Dec 2013, 17: "Nuts and flowers on the nose. Some richness and very obvious class." July 2022 (Bouilland tasting of top 2011s), 16.5, drink 2018–28: "Light nose that's somehow 'molten gold'… A really hedonistic wine without perhaps the crispness and definition of the Lafon and Coche Meursaults already tasted." Her cellar note on the site (2020): "shared with Pierre Morey. Planted in 1959."
The thirty-second version

The most "complete" of Roulot's six village lieux-dits — Jean-Marc calls Tessons "the most representative of the village Meursaults… a synthesis of Tillets and Meix-Chavaux." The house signature is a physical one: crush lightly, press at almost no pressure, then a second winter in steel for "vertical tension." 2011 is the year Roulot himself rates above his 2010, and the critics have come round to. Expect trim, flinty, lime-blossom — serve it before the Coche, not after.

Guy Roulot, the actor, and the interregnum

Roulots in Meursault since 1798; the family were distillers (marc, fine) before Guy (1929–82) started vinifying and bottling separately and — the big idea — bottling the village lieux-dits apart rather than as one blend. He married Geneviève Coche, granddaughter of Léon Coche. Roulot's Meursault-Charmes 1973 placed second among the whites at the 1976 Judgment of Paris (126.5 to Montelena's 132; a recent re-analysis argues a tie). Guy died in 1982 aged 53. Then: Ted Lemon 1982–84, Franck Grux 1984–88, Jean-Marc from the 1988 élevage. Jean-Marc trained at the Conservatoire national supérieur d'art dramatique (Michel Bouquet, Jacques Lassalle), kept acting, and plays Marcel the maître de chai in Klapisch's Ce qui nous lie (2017), shot in Meursault, and the forester in La Passion de Dodin Bouffant (2023). Sarah Marsh: "rather solemn when discussing his wine" but lights up on acting.

Quotable

  • "Excess is the enemy of terroir – too much alcohol or too much oak."
  • "Our wine is lightly colored for three reasons: early harvest dates, not too much oak, and not too much bâtonnage."
  • On bâtonnage: "It does, I admit, create richness, but it also creates heaviness, and one loses purity."
  • "We get microoxygenation in the wood, then reduction in stainless steel. It gives the wine vertical tension."
  • "Charmes is earth, Genevrières is the air, Perrières is the fire." (to Nanson, 2021)
  • On marc: in 2018 he distilled five climats separately — "Tessons has a bit more flesh than Luchets."

Connections: 2011 partition of Domaine René Manuel with Lafon (Roulot took Clos des Bouchères, Lafon Porusots/Bouchères — completing Lafon's set of all six central crus); Jacques Seysses recommended Ted Lemon; Grux went to Olivier Leflaive; Roulot's 0.26 ha Perrières (planted 1964, bought 1976) is in Dessous, in a parcel shared with Matrot. Not found: the reason for the "Clos du Haut Tesson" relabelling (probably INAO rules on "Clos" — inference); exact 2011 harvest dates.

Domaine Coche-Dury · Les Rougeots

Meursault · 2011
Label still reads "J.-F. Coche-Dury"
Climat
Lavalle 3.17 ha; mid-slope, east, triangular sector between Chevalières and Tessons, overlooking the historic village. Name = iron-oxide red-brown soil (Essa rejects the vine-disease etymology). Lavalle deuxième cuvée. HIGH
Parcel
0.65–0.70 ha, planted 1940, 1962, 1973 (38–71-year-old vines in 2011). All massal — "no clones of any kind." Position within the climat and acquisition: not found. HIGH
Farming
Lutte raisonnée since 1973; not certified; all ploughed under Raphaël; short pruning and vine age keep yields low — JFC: "green harvesting is already too late."
Cellar
Bunches firmly crushed/trodden before pressing; old Vaslin ("a Formula One version") plus pneumatic from 2009; abundant lees, regular bâtonnage; barrel ferment, indigenous; new oak moving from ~50% (Nanson 2007) toward 20–25% (Curtis 2022) — ~25–35% plausible for Rougeots 2011 MED; racked July, second winter in cask, hand-bottled cask by cask (chèvre à deux becs, max 8 casks/day) ~April 2013; unfined, unfiltered whites; natural cork. Damy barrels from Coche's own dried wood.
2011
Second vintage under Raphaël's formal control (2010), Jean-François present. First of what Raphaël called "the short vintages 2011–2014." Kelley: "an excellent but comparatively precocious vintage for Coche-Dury." HIGH
Scores
Kelley/WA 94 (Dec 2018) · Neal Martin/WA 93 (2016–2033) · Tanzer 92 · Galloni 91–93 · Burghound 90 · CT 94.3 (28 notes) · Jancis Dec 2024: "Strong struck-match…"
JancisRobinson.com
Nov 2012, 17.5: "Quite smoky and dense. Firm, dry and tense. Electric impact. Super tense. Muscles and vibrato. Long." Re-tasted 14 Dec 2024, 17.5+, drink 2023–38: "Deeper gold than the 2011 Bourgogne Blanc. Strong struck-match aroma and more fruit intensity… a difference that became even more marked after two days… Svelte and super-fresh." At the 2016 Rougeots vertical Raphaël called the site "the Bâtard-Montrachet of Meursault" and "Le Monsieur of village Meursault," in "the most noble line of Meursault: Chevalières, Rougeots, Tesson and Narvaux."
The thirty-second version

The village wine that thinks it is a premier cru, from the domaine Jasper Morris calls "arguably the most sought-after internationally of all white burgundy domaines." Cousin of the Roulot wine beside it and its stylistic opposite: trodden grapes, long pressing, lots of lees, bâtonnage, two winters in cask, hand bottling, no fining or filtration. The struck-match is a lees-and-reduction product, present in every note on the 2011. Decant or open early — one CT note found it much better on day three.

Who made it, and what changed

Léon Coche founded the domaine c. 1923 (Coche-Bouillicaut); son Georges took his share in 1964 and won the gold medals that put the wines on top restaurant lists before scores existed; Jean-François (from 1973) married Odile Dury in 1975 and gave the domaine its name and its fame — Parker's "one of the greatest winemakers on planet earth." Raphaël joined in 1997/99, took formal charge in 2010 with wife Charline; Georges died in 2009 after Raphaël's daily visits. The Raphaël-era programme is "a return to purity": less new wood, less bâtonnage, less trituration since the pneumatic press, less reduction — his self-critique: "once, we produced wine that said more about Coche-Dury than about the terroirs." The 2011s sit at the start of that arc, still very much in the house style.

The reputation for secrecy is better framed as unavailability. Kermit Lynch asked when they could meet: "Only in the evening when I come back from the vines." Jancis: "they closed their visitor's book years ago… wine writers are no longer allowed to taste wines from barrel… Not for the Coches winemaker dinners around the globe." Raphaël's lines: "Tout est sous-classé à Meursault"; "There is no recipe – the élevage depends upon the life the vine has lived during the season." About 30% of production goes to Latour and Jadot under long contracts; the rest is allocated only to buyers with multi-year relationships.

Neal Martin on the 2011 (93): "an intense flinty bouquet with touches of smoke… the oak just loitering in the background… tangerine and citrus peel, hints of blackberry leaf… needs a little coaxing but is worth the wait." Kelley (94, Dec 2018): "lemon oil, green apple, sesame and wheat toast… tangy acids and a long, lingeringly stony finish."

Established
  • Guy Roulot married Léon Coche's granddaughter — cousin domaines, adjoining hillside, opposite élevage.
  • Tessons was Lavalle's première cuvée; Rougeots his deuxième.
  • Roulot: 75% of the juice at 0.2 bar; Coche: trodden grapes, Vaslin.
  • Roulot's 2011 is his reference full crop; Coche's 2011 opened "the short vintages."
  • Rougeots lies just north of Tessons, mid-slope — not near Puligny.
Unresolved
  • Exact 2011 harvest dates at either domaine.
  • Where within Rougeots Coche's rows sit, and when the parcel was bought.
  • Why the Tessons label was changed to "Clos du Haut Tesson."
  • Coche's exact new-oak share for Rougeots in 2011 (25–35% is the best estimate).

B2 · The Heart of Meursault — Lafon vs Ente, Goutte d'Or 2014

Same 5.3 ha climat, same vintage, two parcels replanted a year apart (1991, 1990), two of the three or four most important names in the village — and the cru whose Paulée prize has a walk-on part in the Camus question (see the context section). Goutte d'Or is the northernmost of the six contiguous central premiers crus and the one nearest the village: "the first Premier Cru found when leaving the village in the direction of Puligny" (Latour). It is also, in the 28 June 2014 hail, the worst-placed of the six.

The name, honestly

Three legends: the golden wine (Bouchard still uses it), Latour's gold-coloured flints, and Patrick Essa's, who has farmed the cru for ~25 years and says the wine is not more golden — "la plupart du temps clair comme de l'eau de roche" — but that at dawn in spring "les petites dents de calcaire qui le constellent scintillent de mille feux." He adds the cru could legitimately have been called Perrières, "mais… le nom était déjà pris!" The cadastre pluralised it (Les Gouttes d'Or); Ente uses the historic singular. All three stories agree on one thing: stone.

Was it "the" Meursault of the 19th century? Not by the rankings.

It was the most famous name: Courtépée (1778) lists it among the four best; Thomas Jefferson wrote to his Beaune agent Étienne Parent on 22 January 1789 asking for 250 bottles of "Meursault Goutte d'Or," preferably Bachey's 1784. But Jullien (1816) placed Perrières first; Lavalle (1855) put Perrières in tête de cuvée and Goutte d'Or in première (Bouchard's "tête de cuvée" claim is wrong); the 1861 Beaune committee demoted it to 2e classe while its five neighbours stayed 1re. Essa's explanation: the name was so attractive that for decades Bouchères and other parcels were sold as "Goutte d'Or," diluting its identity. The modern rehabilitation, he says, dates from Lalou Bize-Leroy vinifying two small parcels at d'Auvenay.

The soil argument the two owners are having

Lafon reads it as clay: "Vigorous terroir that must be well-managed. Produces very powerful wines with ripe aromas and explosive minerality"; Dominique on the 2014: "More force (it's the clay), more weight." Essa reads it as a rocky band across the middle — the Comblanchien limestone resurfacing — "sans doute le plus frais… et le plus 'tendu' des crus de la commune," two days later to ripen, "probablement le cru le plus puissant de Côte d'Or avec le vrai Charlemagne." Both are right about their rows: the cru is a rectangle with thin rock in the centre and deeper reworked ground at the bottom; Lafon's and Buisson-Charles' rows run the full height and straddle the band; where Ente's 0.22 ha sits is not found. Burghound's stereotype: "no sense of heaviness present, which Goutte d'Or can sometimes display."

Domaine des Comtes Lafon · Goutte d'Or

Meursault 1er Cru · 2014
Clos de la Barre, Meursault
Parcel
0.39 ha, replanted 1991 (23-year-old vines); rootstock 161-49; massal + clonal (≥5 types); Guyot with some Cordon Royat; east, 5–15% slope. Adjoins Lafon's 0.45 ha village "En Luraule." HIGH
Farming
No synthetics since 1992; organic 1995; whole domaine biodynamic since 1998 (uncertified as biodynamic). Domaine yields ~35 hl/ha.
Cellar
Whole-bunch pneumatic press; since 2009 (Denis Dubourdieu) last ~10% of press juice hyperoxidised separately; 24 h settling; ferment started in tank 2–3 days then barrel; indigenous; 1er crus 25–40% new (≤30% for the top crus); two winters in barrel, 16–22 months, deep cold cellar; bâtonnage essentially abandoned after 1999 (barrels rolled instead); 35 ppm free SO2; nitrogen-rinsed bottles from 2012; DIAM 30 from the 2013 vintage. HIGH
2014
"70% hail losses" (Nanson's note on this wine, 23 Oct 2015). Harvest began Wed 10 Sept (Jasper Morris: "Lafon starts tomorrow"); quick malos, done Jan/Feb. Dominique: "2012 has a bit of strong tannin – probably from the hail – but these 2014s not as the hail was earlier." Order of 600–900 bottles (arithmetic, not a sourced figure). HIGH
Scores
Neal Martin/WA 91–93 · Burghound 90–93 · Vinous/Tanzer 92–93 · Nanson: "More force (it's the clay)… Super yum."
JancisRobinson.com
Nov 2015, 17, drink 2017–25: "Clay of Porusot and the poor soils of Bouchères at the same time. Quite solid. Very winning nose. Lots of pleasure and breadth with good acidity and almost imperceptible tannins. Bright and fleshy. Very long." (Dominique's standing description of the site, which she records every vintage; in 2019 he adds that it is "a wine close to the 'buttery' Meursault stereotype of old.")
The thirty-second version

Lafon's clay wine — the counterweight to Perrières in his range — from young-ish vines and the hail-shredded northern end of the band: seventy percent of the crop gone on 28 June 2014, the rest ripened cleanly because the storm came before véraison. Under DIAM since 2013, with Dubourdieu's anti-oxygen protocol behind it — a 12-year-old Lafon white that can be poured without flinching. And the family that founded La Paulée, whose literary prize was being advertised in November 1941 as Camus finished L'Étranger — a coincidence Alice Kaplan notes without claiming more.

The Lafon story in four generations

Jules Lafon — a Dijon tax official from the Lot, papal count — married Marie Boch on St Vincent's Day 1894; her dowry included the Clos de la Barre house and its 1869 cellar. He bought Montrachet (0.32 ha, plot 37, next to DRC) in 1918/19 and, as mayor, invited 35 friends to his 1923 post-harvest paulée — the origin of La Paulée de Meursault, third of the Trois Glorieuses with the Confrérie's Clos de Vougeot chapitre and the Hospices auction. René (1927–2019) stopped the family selling and bottled everything from 1961. Dominique (b. 1958) — Weinbach 1982, Becky Wasserman's right hand 1983–86, winemaking from 1984/85, full-time August 1986 — told the sharecroppers in 1984 he would not renew, and took Montrachet back from Pierre Morey in 1991. Herbicides out 1992, organic 1995, biodynamic 1998 — "not very popular in the region, except by a few other pioneers, like Anne-Claude Leflaive and Lalou Bize-Leroy." Handed to daughter Léa and nephew Pierre in December 2021. The 2014 is entirely his.

Lafon is the rare top domaine that acknowledged premox and re-engineered: "his first troubled vintage was 1999"; bad bottles 2001, 2004, 2005, 2006; "beginning with his '07s, by most accounts, problems with premox have been increasingly rare." Jancis (2015): some earlier vintages aged "dangerously fast." Daniel Johnnes: "He tirelessly questions his and others' methods."

Domaine Arnaud Ente · La Goutte d'Or

Meursault 1er Cru · 2014
Archer label (from 2009)
Parcel
0.22 ha, planted 1990 (24-year-old vines); from the Thévenot family vines offered by father-in-law Philippe Thévenot in 1990, taken over 1992. Sector within the cru: not found. HIGH
Farming
Certified organic from 2014 — this is the first certified vintage MED; cover crops, fewer tractor passes, Guyot-Poussard pruning; yields "minuscule" — 2013 Goutte d'Or: 27 hl/ha, 12.9% alc. Neighbours "joke that they aren't sure how the Entes put food on the table."
Cellar
Sometimes crushed before gentle pressing; 24 h settling; ≤20% new oak; pièces/demi-muids in 2014 (glass globes only from 2016/18); ~11–12 months barrel + 6 months tank; unfined, unfiltered; closure not found. Reductive, "chiselled" style since ~2000, when he moved from late-picked opulence to early picking — inspired, he says, by Clos Sainte-Hune. MED
2014
Harvest began Thu 11 Sept, one day after Lafon and among the first in Meursault. Hail loss for this parcel not found (the climat lost up to 70% near the village; Ente's Goutte d'Or lost 80–90% again in the 2016 frost). Plausibly one barrel or less.
Scores
Burghound 91–94 · Tanzer reviewed (paywalled) · WA/Morris/Jancis for 2014: not found. Neighbours: 2013 Gilman 94, 2019 Kelley 97 (2027–2060).
JancisRobinson.com
Nov 2015, 17.5, drink 2017–27: "More savoury and mineral than most of his other wines. Rich start and then very creamy and hugely satisfying." Her 2019 note has Ente on the clay: the vines "never suffer water stress… 'stayed in Olympic form til the end'… Truly golden drops."
The thirty-second version

The most closed and coveted address in Meursault, run by a man from the Nord who failed to get into physiotherapy school, took two days to decide whether to accept his father-in-law's 3.5 ha, and was named Bettane & Desseauve's Homme de l'année 2013 — Bettane: the most accomplished Chardonnays "in terms of overall balance" in Burgundy and possibly the world. Early-picked, tiny-yield, unfined, unfiltered, from a parcel replanted the year before Lafon's. Admirers call this his grand cru. The domaine is "totally closed to visits," so almost nothing about this specific bottle is on the record.

Ente in his own words and others'

"Si la qualité et la vérité du raisin sont là, sur le pied de vigne, mon objectif est d'en perdre le moins possible… L'équilibre, toujours l'équilibre."Arnaud Ente, La Route des Blancs

Kermit Lynch: "the baffling paradox of intense old-vine concentration with crystalline weightlessness." Jasper Morris resolved his own question — does Ente like less-ripe grapes, or does his vineyard work ripen them earlier? — in favour of the latter: "his ultra-meticulous work in the vineyards enables him to ripen his grapes that little bit earlier." Inside Burgundy: "a byword for early picking in a way that has unfairly attracted criticism from others in the village." Jean-François Coche was an early mentor by advice ("qui n'aura de cesse de l'encourager à suivre son chemin"). The widely repeated claim that Arnaud worked at Coche-Dury traces to a single Berry Bros. & Rudd sentence ("While working at the legendary Coche Dury, Arnaud married Marie-Odile Thévenot in 1991"), copied by retailers worldwide; it is absent from Jasper Morris's Inside Burgundy profile and Kermit Lynch's sheet, and has been flatly denied twice by people close to the domaine — on La Passion du Vin in 2005 ("il n'a jamais travaillé chez Coche Dury… c'une légende") and on WineBerserkers in 2018 by a "Philip Ente" sitting with Arnaud himself ("Many people in the USA think Arnaud trained under Coche — not true"). The one reconciliation — a placement at Coche's during his 1990–92 work-study course — is not documented anywhere. Son Pierre now makes the reds (40% whole cluster, semi-carbonic). Jeannie Cho Lee named Ente and Roulot as the Meursault successes of 2011 — a bridge back to B1.

Established
  • Jefferson ordered 250 bottles of Goutte d'Or in January 1789.
  • Lavalle 1855: première cuvée, one rung below Perrières (not tête de cuvée, whatever Bouchard's sheet says); 1861: 2e classe.
  • Lafon −70% to hail; both parcels replanted 1990/91 (0.39 ha and 0.22 ha).
  • Lafon began 10 Sept, Ente 11 Sept — Jasper Morris tweeted it.
  • Ente's vines came from his wife's family, the Thévenots.
Unresolved
  • Whether Ente's 1990–92 work-study placement touched Coche-Dury at all; employment there is denied by people close to him (2005, 2018) and unsupported by Morris or Lynch — Jean-François Coche's role was advice and encouragement.
  • Ente's 2014 hail loss and yield — no figure exists.
  • Where within the cru Ente's 0.22 ha sits.
  • Which sector of the climat Lafon's rows occupy.

B3 · The Great Premier Cru Slope — Lafon vs Coche-Dury, Perrières 2014

The grand cru that never was, in the vintage Jasper Morris calls "the most consistently fine white wine vintage for a generation." Two parcels that physically touch: Lafon's small ex-Gauffroy plot is "next to Coche-Dury's Perrières" (Tanzer). Lafon is mostly in Perrières-Dessous, the tier the locals call best; Coche is in Dessus/Aux Perrières, the stony terraces above the old quarry — and makes the most expensive Meursault in the world from it.

The climat

13 ha 21 a 17 ca, 27 owners (Gotti's 2015 map): Aux Perrières 0.80 ha, Dessus 3.76 ha, Dessous 9.60 ha including the 0.95 ha Clos des Perrières (Albert Grivault monopole; bought 1879 from the heirs of the Marquis de La Troche by a 23-year-old distiller). The only one of the three great central crus entirely above the Puligny road; Charmes below, Genevrières north, Puligny Champ Canet south. The name is the quarry, not "little stones": stone from here built the Meursault church tower; Essa says about two hectares of the cru were literally quarried into dust, and the back-filled quarry hole in the middle — separating Dessus from Dessous — is only Bourgogne AOC (Château de Meursault, de Montille have vines in it). Topsoil often 20–30 cm over hard oolite; more clay and Comblanchien higher up; court-noué under the trees at the top.

Essa's sectors: the "Grande Perrière" (Dessous, running on from Genevrières-Dessus — "the most civilised wines of the climat… plus opulents, plus sensuels"; Lafon's 1955 block is here); the "Plat des Perrières" (Dessous, around the Clos — "sans conteste les plus grands Bourgogne blancs par leur intensité… moins visqueux, plus salin et iodé" than Montrachet); and Dessus — "vin de cailloux sur des terres maigres… une sensation quasi coupante sur la langue… Vins de pierres, mais sans doute aussi vin de sang et de labeur" (Coche is here). Nanson's caveat after a 12-wine blind: the tier differences "seem no more marked… versus 50 metres apart in the same sections."

Why no grand cru — the sourced version

Lavalle (1855): "After Montrachet, I know of no other white wine more exquisite" — tête de cuvée. Essa, a Meursault vigneron, gives the only mechanism in print: the communal syndicates in the 1930s "n'ont pas souhaité lui retirer la mention 'Meursault' en des temps où le consacrer grand Cru revenait à le nommer: « Les Perrières »" — a grand cru would have carried no village name, as Montrachet and Corton-Charlemagne do not, and the growers chose the commercial word; rival factions "se sont farouchement querellés." Raphaël Coche's compatible version: the village committees differed — "Tout est sous-classé à Meursault." The Bardet/Grivault dossier for the Clos stalls today for exactly the same reason: Dessous owners would want the whole lower tier in, and Dessus owners — Coche, Bouchard — would be out. Jasper Morris's position: "better perhaps to remain as the outstanding Premier Cru in Meursault." Essa's contrarian aside: Genevrières "aurait le plus mérité" grand cru status for its homogeneity.

Domaine des Comtes Lafon · Perrières

Meursault 1er Cru · 2014
22 ouvrées in three parcels · mostly Dessous
Parcels
~0.91–0.95 ha: 16 ouvrées (~0.68 ha) Dessous on white marl at the northern end beside Genevrières-Dessus, massal, planted 1955; 2 ouvrées (~0.085 ha) very steep, south of the Clos, planted 1982; 4 ouvrées (~0.17 ha) "Perrières Ginette," ex-Gauffroy 2004, reddish soil in lowest Dessus, bordering Coche, vines >50 years. Average vine age ~55. A quarter of the 1955 block was pulled after 2015 — 2014 is the last vintage from the intact 1955 block. (Tanzer, Dec 2019 vertical with Lafon) HIGH
Yield 2014
21 hl/ha (Tanzer: recent years "very low 20 to 30" — court-noué, not hail: southern Meursault "lost hardly anything").
Picking
All Perrières parcels picked the same day; Perrières and Genevrières before Charmes — a deliberate reversal of Pierre Morey, his father's sharecropper, who "believed that Perrières and Genevrières should be picked late." Not acidified since 2003; barely chaptalised in a decade. Lafon and Roulot were seen "in Meursault Perrières, mainly" on Wed 10 Sept 2014.
Cellar
As Goutte d'Or, with ≤30% new oak (Lafon to Tanzer; 100% new until 1989/90, 70% as late as 2000); first racking in summer into 4–5-year-old barrels; second winter in barrel; up to three months in tank; lenticular filtration from 2012; gravity bottling ~spring 2016; DIAM 30. Bladder press since René — "always against Vaslin presses because he found them too harsh." HIGH
Scores
Tanzer 97 (2019–2040) · Galloni 97 · Neal Martin/Vinous 97 (2020–2045) · Martin/WA 96 · Decanter 97 · Jasper Morris 95 · Burghound 94 (2026+) · Gilman 93
JancisRobinson.com
Nov 2015, 17+, drink 2020–30: "1 ha, 10 barrels… Discreet nose. A bit solid and unyielding at the moment. Savoury and dense rather than bedazzling. A bit surly at the moment. But there is massive concentration here." Her 2010 note is the line to keep: "As eloquent an argument for Perrières' elevation to grand cru status as you could find." Matthew Hayes, 2023: only Dessous now — "the vines in Dessus have been grubbed up."
The thirty-second version

Fifty-nine-year-old massal vines on the Grande Perrière, 21 hl/ha, in the last vintage before Lafon pulled a quarter of the block. Tanzer opened his own bottle in 2019 and called it "one of the finest and most complete premier cru white wines I tasted in 2019, even if it was a crime to open it so early." Neal Martin: "dried honey, wet granite and apple blossom… oyster shell lingering." The most engineered anti-premox protocol in Meursault sits behind it: hyperoxidised press fraction, tank-started ferments, nitrogen-rinsed bottles, DIAM 30, 35 ppm free SO2.

Two lines for the table. Dominique on his 2010 Perrières: "My most favourite Perrières – crystal-pure… I don't know if I'll ever make it so good again." And Jasper Morris's confession: despite the ~40% market premium for Perrières, between Lafon's Genevrières and Perrières "if forced to choose, I might very well opt for Genevrières." Denis Dubourdieu — the Bordeaux white-wine professor — consulted from 2009 until his death in 2016: "there's a time for air and there's a time for protection against oxygen."

Domaine Coche-Dury · Perrières

Meursault 1er Cru · 2014
Two plots in the upper tier · Dessus + Aux Perrières
Parcels
0.6 ha (Morris 2023, Curtis 2022; 0.5 Winehog 2011; 0.67 Ficofi) in two plots, Perrières-Dessus and Aux Perrières; bought by Georges Coche (pre-1973); planted 1950, 1970, 2005 MED. Top-10 owner by size. ~1,800–2,700 bottles in a normal year (arithmetic).
Farming
"Neither organic nor biodynamic" (Morris); all ploughed; massal only; yields low "because the vines are old, and not because of green harvesting."
Cellar
Firm crushing before pressing — Vaslin and/or pneumatic (2009), which one for 2014 not found; long lees; bâtonnage (reduced under Raphaël); new oak 25–50%, most likely 30–50% for a top cuvée in 2014 MED; racked July, blended, second winter, bottled cask by cask April 2016; egg-white fined, unfiltered; natural cork. Damy barrels from wood Coche buys and dries itself.
2014
Last of the "short vintages 2011–2014" that pushed Raphaël to buy grapes from 2015 (never for Perrières). Perrières likely spared by the hail (southern Meursault). Harvest date: not found. MED
Scores
Neal Martin/WA 97 (Nov 2016) · Burghound 96 · Morris/Vinous for 2014: not found · sibling 2014 Corton-Charlemagne: WA 99, BH 98 ("wine of the vintage"), Decanter 100
JancisRobinson.com
The 2014 has not been reviewed on JancisRobinson.com (her Coche Perrières notes run 1997–2013). Reference points: 2013, 17.5 ("real attack and richness with massive tension"); 2012, 18 ("Almost painful acidity at first but the undertow is magnificent"); 2009, 18.5 ("absolutely of Grand Cru status"); 1997, 19.
The thirty-second version

Jasper Morris on why Coche's Perrières is the one everyone wants: "Are the yields low? Not exceptionally. Is there some wizardry during the vinification or élevage… specific techniques are not really the issue here: sometimes I think it is just that Jean-François Coche-Dury has the green fingers of a natural gardener." Neal Martin (97): "a shimmering, tensile, wet limestone-scented aroma… wonderful salinity with delicate touches of ginger and grilled walnut — delivering immense depth and power as it fans out in audacious fashion." The reputation gap to the Lafon is a statement about the producer, not the tier — the locals' preferred tier is the one Lafon is in.

Hooks

  • Tastevinage: Neal Martin (Vinous, July 2019) opened a 1979 Coche-Dury Perrières wearing a Tastevinage neck label, approved 1981 — "third-party validation, since this was before the domaine gained its global fame." Four decades before the domaine's fame, Coche's Perrières passed the Confrérie's blind jury. This is the strongest Coche–Confrérie link on record.
  • Coche at the Clos de Vougeot: Jancis Robinson, "Masters of Meursault" (2015): "For Jean-François Coche the 20-mile trip north to the Château du Clos de Vougeot for one of the fancy Chevaliers du Tastevin dinners where I shared a table with him once was a major outing." So Jean-François has sat at a chapitre — and her 2013 Rougeots note records his view on classification: "JF Coche thinks this vineyard should be premier cru. Admits Tessons should be too. But it would result in a coupure du village."
  • Jancis Robinson's 2015 "Masters of Meursault" contrast: Lafon "Burgundy's best-known ambassador," travelling, consulting (Evening Land), making Mâcon; the Coches "completely fixated on their vines," in "decidedly workmanlike cellars below the most ordinary of suburban houses" versus Lafon's cellars under one of the village's grandest houses. She credits Coche with having "instigated the worldwide trend towards the reductive 'struck match' flavours in white wines." Morris calls it "beneficial reduction."
  • Family tree: Léon Coche → Georges → Jean-François → Raphaël; Léon's daughter Marthe → Geneviève → married Guy Roulot. Roulot is Lafon's partner in the 2011 Manuel purchase and a Perrières-Dessous owner. So three of the six Meursault producers at the table are bound by blood or business, and Pierre Morey (Leflaive's régisseur, Lafon's ex-sharecropper) sits under the reception wine.
  • Nanson's rule of thumb: "about 7 years is needed to distinguish a great Perrières." Both are past it. Serve the Coche cool and decant 30–60 minutes for sulfides; the Lafon under DIAM may show a touch of reduction too.
Established
  • The two parcels touch: Lafon's ex-Gauffroy "Perrières Ginette" borders Coche's plot on the Dessus/Dessous line.
  • Lafon: ~0.9 ha in three parcels, mostly Dessous on the 1955 massal block; 21 hl/ha; the last vintage before a quarter of that block was pulled. Coche: ~0.6 ha in the stony upper tier, the last of his "short vintages" 2011–14.
  • Opposite anti-oxidation creeds — Lafon's engineered protocol under DIAM (Denis Dubourdieu consulted 2009–16); Coche's crushed-fruit reduction under natural cork. Jancis credits Coche with starting the world's "struck match" fashion.
  • Coche's Perrières passed the Tastevinage jury in 1981 — Neal Martin opened the 1979 with the neck label still on — and Jean-François has sat at a Clos de Vougeot chapitre (Jancis shared his table).
  • The stone of the climat built the Meursault church tower; Essa says two hectares of the cru were quarried into dust.
  • Jasper Morris, forced to choose between Lafon's Genevrières and Perrières, "might very well opt for Genevrières."
Unresolved
  • Coche's 2014 harvest date, and whether the 2014 went through the Vaslin or the pneumatic press.
  • Coche's new-oak share for the 2014 Perrières (30–50% is the best estimate) and his exact parcel size — 0.5, 0.6 and 0.67 ha all appear in print.
  • No JancisRobinson.com note on the 2014 Coche; no Morris/Vinous score found.
  • Why Perrières was never made grand cru: Essa's "the growers wanted the word Meursault" is the only mechanism in print, not a documented vote.
  • Whether Lafon's 2014 includes fruit from the Dessus rows Matthew Hayes says were later grubbed up.

C · The Importance of Being Stemmed — the whole-cluster table

Six grands crus, three pairs, each pair from one climat and one vintage, each pair a stems-in versus stems-out decision. Every figure below is anchored to a dated statement by the winemaker or a critic who was in the cellar. "Range" is the confidence interval for the specific bottle; the reasoning and the full evidence tables, statement by statement, are in the flight sections and the appendix dossiers.

WineRangeBest pointConf.Anchor evidence
Dujac Clos de la Roche 201080–90%~85%HIGHGalloni, Wine Advocate #198 (Dec 2011), reporting Jeremy Seysses's 2010 regime: "80–90% stems for most wines; ~70% for Charmes and Combottes; 100% for Chambertin and RSV." CdlR is not an exception. Corroborated by Jeremy's "over 80% every year" (SommChat, c. 2012) and by Jancis Robinson's cellar note on the 2013: "63% whole bunch, which is less than usual."
Rousseau Clos de la Roche 20100–10%0%HIGHCoates (Decanter 2011): domaine "normally destems"; only exception 15% in 2009 (Chambertin/Bèze context). Nanson 2005: "90% destemmed." Curtis (Decanter Oct 2024) on CdlR: "grapes are destemmed and aged in used casks." Jancis Robinson on the 2009 CdlR: "the only one with all destemmed grapes… NO new oak." Rousseau moved to ~20% across the range only in 2015.
Mugnier Bonnes-Mares 20100%0%HIGHFrédéric Mugnier to Paul Wasserman, June 2011, with the 2010s in barrel: "De-stemming. Always 100%. Except for experimental purposes." Kolm 2013/2014/2023, Neal Martin 2023, McInerney 2025 all concur. Jancis Robinson's 2020 visit: "an average of 15% new oak… and no whole bunch." One 2010 blog claiming "up to 40%" is an uncorroborated outlier.
Roumier Bonnes-Mares 201030–50%~35–45%MEDNo direct 2010 figure in open sources (Tanzer/Burghound Jan 2012 paywalled). Brackets: 2009 BM = 60% (cellar visit, May 2011); 2013 BM = 35% (Kolm). Christophe's rule (Martin 2018): "the riper the vintage, the more stems"; 2010 was 11.5–12% natural. Kelley 2021: "30–50% stems." Jancis Robinson's cellar notes: 2009 "60% whole bunch, 'even though the stems are green in general but the interior is already transformed into wood'"; 2018 "around 65%"; 2023 "75%."
DRC Échezeaux 201470–80%~70–75%HIGHBertrand de Villaine to Nanson, 14 Nov 2015, tasting the 2014s: "normally there's about 25–30% destemming," whole clusters netted at the bottom of the vat. 2013 = 70–80% by parcel; Cho Lee on RC 2014 "about 70%"; 2015 was the first ~100% year "in many years."
Liger-Belair Échezeaux 20140–15%0% (token ≤10–15% possible)MEDNo Échezeaux-specific figure ever published. Tanzer (Nov 2016): the 2015 crus got "about 20% whole clusters… a bit more than usual" — caps 2014 below 20%. Nanson 2013–16: Chaumes/Suchots 15–20%, Petits Monts "never." Kelley 2017: "He certainly used to destem 100%… very Jayer-iste." Nanson's 2014 Échezeaux barrel note mentions no stems (he flags them when present).

The shape of the act: C1 is maximum distance (85% vs 0%, and 90–100% new oak vs none). C2 is the cleanest control — two neighbours who share a wall in Chambolle, same cru, same year, same cool 11.5–12% natural sugars, 0% vs ~40%. C3 re-stages the old Jayer/DRC Échezeaux argument: Liger-Belair holds 0.33 ha of Jayer's Cruots and learned at Jayer's knee; DRC's Poulaillères, late-picked with three-quarters stems.

One trap to set for the committee: tasters routinely perceive stems in Liger-Belair wines that have none. Nanson (2012): "for ages I always told LM that his wines smell of stems – and he said 'funny, because I don't use them.'" The domaine's own explanation is a week of cold maceration below 15 °C on uncrushed berries.

Established
  • Whole bunches were the historical default; the Burgundian swing to destemming is Henri Jayer's (1922–2006), carried by his pupils — Méo-Camuzet, Rouget, Liger-Belair.
  • Jasper Morris on why the pendulum swung back: "(1) Henri Jayer, who hated stems, is dead. (2) With global warming, stems ripen more often."
  • 2010 is the easy whole-cluster year (small millerandé berries, lignified rachis); 2014 the hard one (under-lignified stems, sour-rot pressure) — the reds were chosen to sit on both sides of it.
  • Lignification is cellular, not colour: "The stems are bright green, even when they are lignified appropriately" (Mahle). Stems raise pH via potassium and give finer-grained tannin than skins.
  • Tasters routinely smell stems in Liger-Belair wines that contain none; the domaine blames a week of cold maceration on uncrushed berries.
  • The only zero-doubt figures at the table: Mugnier 0%, Rousseau ≈0%, DRC ~70–75%.
Unresolved
  • Roumier's 2010 figure — the one number that would close the C2 comparison (Burghound #45 or Tanzer, Jan 2012, would have it).
  • Any Échezeaux-specific stem figure for Liger-Belair; the 0–15% band is inferred from his other crus.
  • Dujac's cuvée-specific share for the 2010 Clos de la Roche (80–90% is the domaine-wide band).
  • Whether a taster can reliably tell stems from cold-soak aromatics blind — the Liger-Belair trap says no, and no controlled test exists.

C1 · Morey-Saint-Denis — Dujac vs Rousseau, Clos de la Roche 2010

Same rock, same year, the two regimes at their maximum distance: Jacques Seysses's 100%-stems inheritance moderated to 80–90% by Jeremy, in 90–100% new oak, against Rousseau's destemmed, zero-new-oak Morey outlier — the domaine's only wine outside Gevrey and, in 2010, its quietest cuvée. Since then the two houses have drifted toward each other (Rousseau to ~20% stems from 2015; Dujac down from 100%), so 2010 is the honest way to stage the argument.

The climat and the "quadrupling"

16.9 ha, largest grand cru in Morey, ~250 m, east-facing, touching Gevrey (Latricières, Combottes) to the north and Clos Saint-Denis to the south; no wall — "Clos" is nominal, "Roche" the limestone blocks that surface in the original lieu-dit. The original Clos de la Roche was 4.57 ha; the 1936 decree annexed Les Mochamps, Froichots, Fremières, Chabiots and lower Monts Luisants (~15.3 ha); 1971 added Genavrières and Chaffots. Curtis (Decanter 2024): the appellation "quadrupled in area," which still "generates debate among connoisseurs about terroir quality." Alexandre Abel (Ponsot): the original lieu-dit "gives substance, while Les Monts Luisants brings" freshness. Jancis Robinson's: Morey is "a sort of St-Julien, if you'll forgive the reference to Bordeaux, of the Côte de Nuits." Curtis's shorthand for the wine: "full-bodied, rich, black-fruit… firm muscular texture, tannic grip, savoury/gamey element… dark and brooding in youth." Owners: Ponsot 3.2–3.5 ha (3 of the original 4), Dujac ~1.95, Rousseau 1.48, G. Lignier 1.0, H. Lignier 0.92, Leroy 0.67, Duband 0.41, Chantal Rémy 0.40.

The point that matters: Dujac's ~0.5 ha and Rousseau's ~0.48 ha in the original lieu-dit are the "true rock"; the rest of both holdings is annexed ground — Monts Luisants and Chabiots (upper/mid) for Dujac, Fremières (lower, toward the road) for Rousseau.

Domaine Dujac · Clos de la Roche

Grand Cru · 2010
Second-largest owner after Ponsot
Parcels
~1.95 ha in 6 parcels: 0.5 original clos, 0.5 Monts Luisants, 0.59 Chabiots + smalls (Curtis 2024). Core from the 1968 Graillet purchase; additions 1977, 1990. Vines "over 50 years old" (Jeremy, 2024); massal from Chambolle Gruenchers historically. HIGH
Farming
Lutte raisonnée 1987; organic from 2001 (a third), 100% by 2008, certified from the 2011 vintage; biodynamic practices from 2002/03 — the trial parcel is Clos de la Roche itself (Diana: "the biodynamic one always has a bit more acidity analytically"). Jacques: "we will certainly never be putting our organic status on the bottles."
Stems 2010
80–90% HIGH — Galloni/WA Dec 2011: "80–90% stems for most wines; ~70% for Charmes and Combottes; 100% for Chambertin and RSV." Whole clusters loaded on top of destemmed fruit, gravity, no crushing.
Cellar
Low-temperature ferment ~2 weeks, indigenous; light punch-downs early then pump-overs; ~90–100% new, light toast Allier bought two years ahead and dried 30 months MED; 12–18 months; one racking after malo; unfined, unfiltered; bottled ~Feb–Mar 2012; 13.5%. Cooper not found.
2010
Finished picking 30 Sept (Nanson diary); crop −30 to −50%; 12–12.5% potential, light chaptalisation; loose bunches, shot berries; 575 cases (UWL). Jeremy (2014): "Since we have been organic… quite a lot more reduction in the wines" — decant.
Scores
Galloni/WA 93–95 (2020–2035) · Tanzer 95.5 · Galloni/Vinous 96.5 (2014: "an infant, but… fabulous") · Burghound 96 (unverified) · BBR 2022–2055
JancisRobinson.com
7 Jan 2012, 18, drink 2017–32: "Bright crimson. Very bright muscular fruit. Very sweet and racy and lovely precision. Dense and dancing. Fragrant." Dec 2011, on the 2009 beside it: "Not as fine as the 2010." Her cellar notes give the stem trend: 2013 "63% whole bunch, which is less than usual"; 2014 "only 80% new oak for the final blend"; 2021 "70% new oak."
The thirty-second version

Jacques Seysses — banker's son, biscuit-factory heir, two harvests with Gérard Potel at Pousse d'Or — bought Marcel Graillet's 4.5 ha in late 1967, named it "du Jacques," and vinified everything whole-cluster in all-new oak for thirty years. Jeremy (joined 1998) kept the faith at 80–90% but stopped treating it as dogma: "Some terroirs don't seem to do so well with whole cluster… I like destemming a little more for the Gevrey vineyards." Tanzer on this bottle: "candied raspberry, flowers, stone and sandalwood… wonderfully expressive and extroverted today for such a dense, even brawny wine." Galloni's counter-intuitive read of the pair: the Dujac is the "decidedly feminine… weightless energy" one; the destemmed Rousseau is "all about power, structure and length."

Jeremy Seysses on stems (the best primary voice on the subject)

"We have the feeling that we get greater complexity and silkier tannins with whole cluster fermentation… In high acid vintages, it helps round things out, and in high ripeness vintages, it brings a freshness."to Jamie Goode, 2015
"It has become currently very fashionable in Burgundy and beyond… There are too many caricatures of whole-cluster wine in the market." On the failure mode: "Bad vegginess. Tomato leaves. Broccoli. Green bean smells. Harsh tannins." On lignification: "Up to the knuckle is what I usually shoot for."2018

Jacques's habitual question at the sorting table, per Jeremy (2024): "Are you sure you don't want to destem that bunch?" Rosalind Seysses (American) joined in 1973; Diana Snowden Seysses (UC Davis 2001, Dujac from 2003, makes Snowden Vineyards on Pritchard Hill) married Jeremy — their sons are Aubert and Blaise; Alec joined 2003. Dujac alumni: Jean-Pierre de Smet (Arlot), Ted Lemon (who then ran Roulot — B1), Gary Farr. The 2005 Thomas-Moillard purchase (with de Montille) is why there are 100%-stem Chambertin and RSV cuvées in Galloni's note. Jacques's friend: Aubert de Villaine. Claude Kolm's line for a 16-year-old: Jacques-era Dujac "hardly ever seem[s] to go into a closed phase."

Domaine Armand Rousseau · Clos de la Roche

Grand Cru · 2010
The domaine's only wine outside Gevrey
Parcels
1.48 ha in two: ~0.48 ha at the southern tip of the original clos + ~1 ha in Fremières (lower); picked and vinified together. Armand bought the first piece c. 1920; Charles enlarged 1965 and 1975 (sources conflict; this reconciles them). Part replanted c. 2008. MED
Farming
No herbicides; insecticides stopped c. 2000; not biodynamic, no organic certification; massal selection; ~11–12,000 vines/ha; green harvest and leaf removal (Eric's introductions).
Stems 2010
0–10%, most likely 0% HIGH — Coates 2011: "normally destems," sole exception 15% in 2009; Nanson 2005: "90% destemmed"; Curtis 2024 on CdlR: "destemmed and aged in used casks."
Cellar
Harvest from 22 Sept 2010; ~4 days cold soak; 15–21 days cuvaison in open steel, pump-over and pigeage, max 31 °C; indigenous; no new oak HIGH (Jancis Dec 2011: "No new oak"; first 10% new on the 2012); 18–22 months, one racking after malo; light filtration; 13.5%.
2010
Frédéric Robert (commercial director — not "Frédéric Rousseau"): "a bit like the 2001s, but with lower yields and more concentration"; crop −30% vs 2009. Charles, then 87, "reluctant to make a comparison."
Scores
Galloni/WA 94–96+ (2030–2050) · Burghound 92–94 ("15 years or so") · Tanzer 91–94 / 92.5 · Jancis 17.5 · Jasper Morris 97, tasted Nov 2025, drink 2022–2032
JancisRobinson.com
8 Dec 2011, 17.5, drink 2018–34: "A little softer than some. Sweet fruit rather than massive Roche expression. Very neat and well behaved but in reserve. A rather wild note on the end. No new oak." On the 2009 (Oct 2010): "the only one with all destemmed grapes… NO new oak." On the 2011: "Quite different from the Gevreys but vinified the same – on purpose." Julia Harding on the 2017: "the freshness I associate with stems (though it is fully destemmed)." Her 1999: 20/20.
The thirty-second version

Rousseau's wine of the range that isn't from Gevrey, made the old way — destemmed, four days cold, three weeks in the vat — and raised entirely in used barrels until 2012 while Chambertin and Bèze got 100% new François Frères. Eric Rousseau: "They should not be too extracted or tannic. They'll keep well enough provided they are balanced." Jancis on release: "Sweet fruit rather than massive Roche expression… A rather wild note on the end. No new oak." Jasper Morris re-tasted it in November 2025 and gave it 97 — it is in its window now, whereas Galloni wanted 2030. Cyrielle Rousseau's nickname for the cuvée: "Jean-Paul, after the robots of JP Gaultier" — the corseted one.

The Rousseau story

Armand Rousseau (1884–1959), from a family of growers, coopers and merchants, inherited his first vines at 18, gained house and vines on marriage in 1909, and on the advice of Raymond Baudoin (founder of La Revue du Vin de France) began domaine-bottling for restaurateurs in the early 1930s — among the first in Burgundy — and exported after Repeal. Killed in a car accident, 1959. Charles (1923–2016), law and oenology at Dijon, took over at 36 with 6.5 ha and built 14+: Clos St-Jacques 1954, Bèze 1961, Clos de la Roche 1965/75, Ruchottes 1977 (brokered with Jean-Marie Roumier and Georges Mugneret — a bridge to C2). "Le Chambertin is male, well-built… Clos de Bèze is more complex, classier and delicate." Eric (b. 1957) joined 1982, vinification from the early 1990s; Cyrielle (b. 1988; geology, viticulture, Dijon DTO; Oregon, Australia, NZ) full-time from 2014 — the 2010 predates her. 15.33 ha, 11 wines, ~63,000 bottles. Jacques Seysses visited Charles Rousseau as a young man (IDTT ep. 470) — so the stems evangelist learned from the destemmer.

Established
  • Dujac ~85% stems / 90–100% new oak; Rousseau 0% stems / 0% new oak. Maximum distance.
  • Both hold ~0.5 ha of the original 4.57 ha; the rest is 1936/1971 annexed ground.
  • Counter-intuition: the stemmed wine is the perfumed, silky one; the destemmed the muscular one (Galloni).
  • Morris, Nov 2025: Rousseau 97, drinking now.
  • The commercial director is Frédéric Robert — not a Rousseau.
Unresolved
  • Dujac's exact stem share for this cuvée (80–90% is the band Galloni reported; no cuvée-specific figure).
  • Dujac's cooper; Rousseau's used-barrel provenance.
  • Rousseau's 2010 chaptalisation and bottling date.
  • Whether Rousseau's 2008 replant contributed to the 2010 (young vines are usually excluded).

C2 · Chambolle-Musigny — Mugnier vs Roumier, Bonnes-Mares 2010

The cleanest control in the dinner. The two domaines share a wall on the bend of the Rue de Vergy — Roumier "sits on the sharp bend in the road, next-door to Mugnier's Château de Chambolle-Musigny" (Nanson). Same cru, same year, same cool millerandé fruit at 11.5–12% natural, and two diametrically opposed decisions at the destemmer.

The climat

15.06 ha (13.54 in Chambolle, 1.52 in Morey), 25 exploitants (2023), bounded by Clos de Tart to the north and Les Fuées to the south. The structural fact: "a diagonal path leading from the lower part of the vineyard at the southern end to the top at the Morey end. Broadly speaking the soil below this line is terres rouges; above it terres blanches" (Jasper Morris). Terres blanches = white marl with crushed Ostrea acuminata oyster fossils, upper slope; terres rouges = crinoidal limestone and red clay over Prémeaux-type Bathonian, lower slope and Morey end. Roumier: terres rouges gives "richesse, onctuosité, fruits rouges voire noirs"; terres blanches "l'épice, les fleurs blanches et même la pivoine rouge." François Millet (de Vogüé) on Bonnes-Mares in the Chambolle family: "perhaps a brother-in-law." Etymology: bonnes mères (the nuns of Tart), or marer (to cultivate), or a statue of three fertility goddesses — none proven. A detail worth having: the 8 Dec 1936 decree covered only the 1.85 ha in Morey; the Chambolle 13.7 ha were added by a Vichy decree of 3 July 1944. Owners: de Vogüé 2.67–2.70 (all terres rouges; destems), Roumier 1.39 (+0.50 métayage since 2016), Clair 1.64, Drouhin-Laroze 1.5, Bart 1.03, Groffier 0.97, Dujac 0.58, Mugnier 0.36, d'Auvenay 0.26 (all stems).

Domaine Jacques-Frédéric Mugnier · Bonnes-Mares

Grand Cru · 2010
"Une bande étroite de 36 ares"
Parcel
0.36 ha, a narrow strip "stretching the full height of the hillside" — ~1/3 terres rouges, ~2/3 terres blanches (Burghound). Planted half in 1988, the rest 1961 and 1980; Fred was "never completely satisfied" with his Bonnes-Mares and replanted half to fix the clonal mix — by 2006 "the wine quality [was] reaching his expectations." Part of the 1863 Marey-Monge purchase. 900–1,500 bottles a year. HIGH
Farming
Not organic, deliberately: last fertiliser 1986, herbicide 1990, insecticide 1995; published sceptic of biodynamics ("Regard sur la biodynamie"); no green harvest ("a cover for earlier mistakes"); ~5 clusters per vine; a vibrating sorting table bought in 2002 famously shook "a hundred kilos of ladybugs" out of the 2004 crop.
Stems 2010
0% HIGH — "De-stemming. Always 100%. Except for experimental purposes… I'd like to do some whole cluster. But there are often reasons not to." (to Paul Wasserman, June 2011)
Cellar
Pied de cuve from cut grapes ~10 days before harvest; 3–5 days cold soak; century-old open wooden vats + temperature-controlled steel; ~3 weeks; pigeage "less and less… at the end of fermentation"; 17–20% new oak ("it no longer has a determining influence"); ~12 months barrel then ~6 months steel; no fining, no filtration — "Zero." HIGH
2010
Fred's vintage text: "all the qualities of late September vintages… But they also have a fullness of texture that one would expect to find only in warmer vintages… The secret is called 'millerandage.'" Yields ~20% below 2009. He rates 2009 higher and says drink the 2010s first. Looking back (2019): "a lovely template but we see wines like that much more rarely." Harvest date not found (his norm: last week of Sept).
Scores
Tanzer 94 ("surprisingly smooth tannins") · Burghound 94 · Galloni/WA 91–94 (2020–2040) · UWL "Outstanding Plus"
JancisRobinson.com
6 Dec 2011, 18.5, drink 2018–32: "Even this is pale. Quite autumnal. So light! A less confident winemaker would worry about this. Sheer lift off! Lots going on on the end." Her standing cellar note: "In the middle of the vineyard. Terres rouges and blanches. Small parcel so assembled from the start"; 2019: "0.35 ha, 25 hl/ha… Four barrels, sometimes five." And Fred's view: "inside each climat the magic is to have big diversity… Separating the soils seems erroneous to him." 2020 visit: "an average of 15% new oak… and no whole bunch."
The thirty-second version

Petroleum engineer on North Sea rigs, then a TAT regional-airline pilot flying "half the week" into the 1990s because four hectares couldn't feed a family; first vintage 1985 after a six-month crash course at the Lycée Viticole. Fred destems everything, always, for a reason that is more philosophical than technical: "Since I could never make up my mind, I decided I would vinify all the wines the same. Then that became a philosophy." Adding stems by cru would break the rule. Becky Wasserman: "In winemaking, Fred is seeking the absence of Fred." He thinks of this wine as "a more powerful Fuées."

Mugnier, quotable

  • "You cannot reconstruct balance. You cannot, through winemaking techniques, achieve balance in what was not balanced beforehand."
  • "Every time skins are torn they release green tannins, herbal flavors, coarse stuff." — his case against stems, in effect.
  • "I don't want to interfere in the expression of the year; cold, dry, wet, hot… it's why we put the years on the label!" (2019)
  • "I have some nostalgia for those thinner more fragile wines — ethereal and of emotion." (2021) and, having finished the 2020 harvest in August: "I intend to move the business to Finland if the warming increases."
  • From his essay "Le Pianiste et l'Oenologue," quoting Glenn Gould: beauty is "quand on savoure dans le vin le goût du raisin plutôt que la science de l'oenologue."
  • "I say if the wine isn't ready to drink after ten years then perhaps I have failed." (2025)

The domaine exists as a business only because the Clos de la Maréchale (9.5–10 ha, Nuits) came back from a 53-year Faiveley lease on 1 Nov 2003, taking the domaine "d'un coup de 4 à 14 hectares" — so the Confrérie's founding family was this producer's tenant. The 19th-century fortune was Absinthe Mugnier (the Lucien Lefèvre "Zouave" poster). Bruno/Bernard Clair was régisseur 1978–84. "I learned more about Burgundy from [Becky Wasserman] than from anyone else."

Domaine Georges Roumier · Bonnes-Mares

Grand Cru · 2010
Four plots, both soils, blended
Parcels
1.3919 ha owned (2010; +0.50 ha métayage from 2016): four plots — three terres blanches, one terres rouges at the Morey end from the 1952 Belorgey purchase (Nanson/Martin; Morris counts eight cadastral plots). Split ~50:50 by surface in 2010 (domaine, Morris) or red-leaning 0.9/0.6 (Burghound). Origins: 1924 Quanquin dowry + 1952. Average vine age ~40; oldest 1902. On repique — vines replaced individually, never blocks. HIGH
Farming
Herbicides stopped 1989; failed organic trial 1993; gradual conversion from 1998; uncertified by choice — "The organic label is like locking yourself into prohibitions that have only a marketing objective." On biodynamics: "It seems quite mysterious to me." Clonal diversity (667/777/778/828 on 161-49), not massal. Yield 30 hl/ha.
Stems 2010
30–50%, most likely ~35–45% MED — 2009 BM = 60% (cellar, May 2011); 2013 BM = 35% (Kolm); rule: "the riper the vintage, the more stems"; Kelley 2021: "30–50%." Stems "normally come from the older rather than younger vines." Vinified as two cuvées by soil, blended for élevage.
Cellar
Vibrating sorting table (2003); 5–6 days cold soak ~15 °C; open concrete and steel; indigenous; 18–25 days; pigeage early then remontage "to soften the tannins"; 30–40% new oak, Vosges MED; 16 months; malo deliberately late; one racking; unfined, unfiltered; bottled ~spring 2012. Natural 11.5–12%, label 13.5% — chaptalised ~1–1.5° (inference).
2010
Christophe (2011): "prefers his 2010s to his 2009s for their purity of fruit and expression of terroir… a similarity between the 2010s and the 2008s… could be a richer version of 2004"; yields −30 to −35% vs 2009. Harvest date not found (he picks late).
Scores
Burghound 94–97 → 96 ("overtly cool and restrained… even spicier nose… explosive length") · Galloni/WA 95–97 (2025–2050) · Tanzer 95 · Wine Spectator 95 · Jancis: "Very solid, almost animal" · UWL "Exceptional"
JancisRobinson.com
6 Dec 2011, 18, drink 2020–32: "Very solid, almost animal. Great weight and solidity. This one has its feet firmly on the ground. Savoury finish. The driest of the range on the end. Solid, and a tiny bit of a carthorse." Stem figures from her visits: 2009 "60% whole bunch, 'even though the stems are green in general but the interior is already transformed into wood'"; 2018 "around 65%"; 2023 "75%." On the 2011 she tasted the two soils apart — terres rouges "45% of the blend"; the assemblage "so much greater than the elements" — and gave that wine 19.5 at Bouilland in 2022: "The sort of wine every burgundy drinker wants to find."
The thirty-second version

Georges Roumier came from the Morvan, not a vigneron's son, and married into Chambolle in 1924 while simultaneously running de Vogüé's vineyards; Christophe (b. 1958, first vintage 1982) blends four plots across both soil types — "arguably the most representative Bonnes Mares available" (Morris) — and has used stems since the late 1980s: "the freshness of flavor brought by whole cluster fermentation interests me." Meadows found the 2010 "partially reduced… overtly cool and restrained yet… spicier"; a Berserker in March 2026: "not ready quite yet." Kolm's rule: 15–20 years to show. Decant, and expect it to be the muscular, pungently mineral one beside Mugnier's floral, Fuées-like glass.

The 1988 parable — use this

In 1988 Christophe made two Bonnes-Mares: the regular cuvée, fully destemmed, and a Vieilles Vignes with ~50% stems. Six of the eleven VV barrels went back into the regular blend. Neal Martin has tasted the pair blind four times; his verdict after thirty years: "the right decision was to pick earlier and use less stems… the regular cuvée seems to be better, though both are stupendous" — and then, in a 2022 re-match, the VV "showed more mineralité and complexity." That is the whole stems argument, unresolved, in one domaine's cellar. Martin also calls the very stemmy 2008 Roumier Musigny "Christophe Roumier's homage to Jacques Seysses" — Seysses is one of the four mentors on the Roumier website (with Jayer, d'Angerville, Potel), so Roumier is the bridge between C1's stems camp and C2's destemmer.

Also: Christophe on driving a fermentation (IDTT ep. 476): "you need an accelerator and you need a steering wheel. There is nothing like that… The only way is to use the temperature control." On the 2004 ladybugs: "at least you always know the vintage in a blind tasting!" On flipping: Musigny prices "perplex and embarrass" him; every bottle now carries an NFC chip. Dominique Lafon was his organic-farming interlocutor in the 1980s "at a time when everyone was criticizing it."

Confrérie hook for this flight

Both domaines donated to the April 2022 Sotheby's/Château du Clos de Vougeot charity auction for Cîteaux Abbey — the mother house of the monks who built the Clos — run with the Confrérie in the Grand Cellier — a Roumier Bonnes-Mares 2015 magnum, a Mugnier Musigny 2007 jeroboam. Leroy, DRC, Rousseau, Dujac, Lafon and Leflaive also gave — eight of the dinner's producers in one Confrérie-hosted cause. Neither domaine owns Clos de Vougeot today (Roumier's 1952 parcels went to the Hervé Roumier branch; Mugnier sold theirs after 1949).

Established
  • Neighbours across a wall on the Rue de Vergy; same cru, same cool millerandé 2010 at 11.5–12% natural sugar.
  • Mugnier: one 0.36 ha strip running the full slope; 100% destemmed, always — "Except for experimental purposes." Roumier: ~1.39 ha in four plots across both soils, two cuvées by soil then blended; 60% stems in 2009, 35% in 2013.
  • Fred Mugnier flew regional airliners half the week into the 1990s because four hectares couldn't feed a family; the domaine became viable only when the Clos de la Maréchale came back from a 53-year Faiveley lease in 2003 — the Confrérie's founding family was his tenant.
  • Georges Roumier came from the Morvan and married into Chambolle in 1924 while running de Vogüé's vineyards; the oldest vine in the Bonnes-Mares dates from 1902. Christophe and Dominique Lafon were both born in 1958.
  • Mugnier's vibrating sorting table shook "a hundred kilos of ladybugs" out of the 2004 crop; Roumier: "at least you always know the vintage in a blind tasting!"
  • Roumier's 1988 two-cuvée Bonnes-Mares is the domaine's own controlled stems trial — and Neal Martin's blind verdicts on it have gone both ways over thirty years.
Unresolved
  • Roumier's stem share for the 2010 Bonnes-Mares specifically: 30–50%, most likely 35–45% — no dated figure for this vintage.
  • Harvest dates at either domaine in 2010.
  • Roumier's new-oak share for the 2010 (30–40% is the best estimate) and the terres rouges/blanches split of his parcels (50:50 vs 0.9/0.6).
  • Whether the 2010 was chaptalised at Roumier — the 13.5% label against 11.5–12% natural is an inference.
  • Which soil dominates Mugnier's strip in the glass — Fred thinks separating the soils is "erroneous," so no one has tasted them apart.

C3 · Vosne-Romanée (Flagey) — DRC vs Liger-Belair, Échezeaux 2014

The Jayer/DRC Échezeaux argument, re-run in 2014. Herwig Janssen's summary of the 1990 pair: "Henri Jayer = early picking, cold maceration, chaptalization, complete destemming… mostly from the Cruots part of Echezeaux. DRC = later picking, cold maceration, less chaptalization, whole cluster, 18 months and more aging… mostly from the Poulaillères part." Liger-Belair holds 0.33 ha of Cruots, learned at Jayer's knee, and picked nine days before DRC. The two parcels are ~400 m apart across Échezeaux du Dessus. Curtis's line: "Flagey is doing a lot of the work, and Vosne is getting all the credit."

The climat

36.27 ha in production (BIVB 2022), ~80 owners, 230–300+ m, east-facing at the mouth of the Combe d'Orveau — an alluvial fan that drains fast, channels cold air from the Hautes-Côtes and ripens later than Vosne's core. Name from chesaux/casa, "little huts"; first recorded 1287; Cîteaux from 1353 — the monks' vineyard outside the Clos de Vougeot wall. "Échezeaux" proper was the 3.5–4.5 ha lieu-dit Échezeaux du Dessus; a 1925 court case (Étienne Camuzet v. the Gros/Mongeard families) let neighbours use the name, the 1937 AOC swept in all eleven lieux-dits, and a judge added 1.27 ha of Beaux Monts Bas in 1984 — hence the reputation for variability. The hierarchy most critics agree on: Les Poulaillères (5.2 ha, ~90% DRC, above Grands Échezeaux) and Les Cruots ou Vignes Blanches (3.2 ha, Jayer's — he called it "the best terroir of the appellation") at the top, then Champs Traversins and Échezeaux du Dessus; Treux, Quartiers de Nuits, Clos St-Denis on the damp low ground below. These two bottles are, arguably, the two best addresses in the cru.

Domaine de la Romanée-Conti · Échezeaux

Grand Cru · 2014
Largest owner · Bernard Noblet's cellar
Parcel
4.67 ha — ~90% Les Poulaillères "lying just above Grands Échezeaux" (C&B 2017) + ~10% of Clos Saint-Denis. Champs Traversins/Cruots for DRC: not found — a breakdown sometimes repeated in the trade, unsupported by any source. Average vine age 30 in 2014 — youngest red vineyard in the domaine. Since 1988 farmed under a 30-year sale-and-leaseback that financed the RSV purchase MED.
Farming
Fully biodynamic from 2007/08 after trials on La Tâche/Grands Échezeaux; horses first in RC and Montrachet; massal restocking from 50–60 genotypes identified in Romanée-Conti; ~25 hl/ha average — "three vines to produce one bottle." Chef de culture Nicolas Jacob.
Stems 2014
~70–75%, up to 80% by parcel HIGH — Bertrand de Villaine, 14 Nov 2015: "normally there's about 25–30% destemming, the whole clusters at the bottom of the tank but with a net to catch them just above the base… to help drainage." 2013: "between 70 and 80% depending on the parcel." 2015: "almost 100%… for the first time in many years."
Cellar
Triage tables; 3–6 days cold soak (Noblet); indigenous; ~3 weeks; more pigeage than the post-2018 "delicacy" régime; 100% new François Frères (a few older barrels exist) MED; 16–18 months; one racking; fine filtration only where needed. Noblet on oak: "un support, comme un soutien-gorge pour une belle poitrine." The often-repeated claim that DRC buys and dries its own Tronçais wood: not verified.
2014
Picked 24, 25, 26 Sept — last of the domaine's vineyards; 27.8 hl/ha; 1,443 dozen; bottled 25/29 Feb and 3 Mar 2016; 13%. Hail 28 June cost 15–20% — "more like a green harvest for us" (Bertrand). No Duvault-Blochet in 2014. HIGH
Scores
Jancis 18.5 (2029–2050) · Burghound 94 · Tanzer 93 · Galloni 93+ (2024: "shockingly primary… whole cluster savoriness") · Neal Martin/WA 92 (2019–2035: "one of the most approachable 2014s… just as likely to be drunk too young") · C&B 17.5+ ("very 'D'Artagnan'… 'Costaud'")
JancisRobinson.com
9 Nov 2016, 18, drink 2028–48: "Heady, almost gamey nose – immediately much richer and denser than the Corton… Imagine the subtlest redcurrant liqueur… Very communicative!" Julia Harding, Feb 2017, 18.5: "A clear stemmy (but fully ripe) freshness… leaving an imprint rather than drawing you along by the fingertips." Aubert, at the 2015 tasting: Échezeaux "has been très en forme for the last few years."
The thirty-second version

Three-quarters whole cluster, netted at the bottom of the vat, picked last on 24–26 September at 27.8 hl/ha, in new François Frères for sixteen months — Bernard Noblet's next-to-last vintage as chef de cave. Aubert de Villaine on 2014: "phenolic ripeness came much later. The wines have purity, but they are going to take a long time to reach their full maturity… About 15 years." His epigraph for the vintage: risk, and luck — "peut-être tout simplement le sourire de la vigne au vigneron qui a su la respecter… et l'aimer!" Galloni in 2024 found it "dark, brooding and introspective… needs another five years at a minimum." At twelve years the whole-cluster savoury register should be front and centre — which is the point.

Aubert de Villaine and the Climats — the strongest Tastevin thread

De Villaine (b. 1939; joined 1965; co-gérant 1974–2021, with Lalou Bize-Leroy until 1992, then Henry-Frédéric Roch; nephew Bertrand co-director; Perrine Fenal — Lalou's daughter — replaced Roch in Jan 2019; advisory role from 2022) led the Association pour l'inscription des Climats du vignoble de Bourgogne from its founding to the UNESCO inscription on 4 July 2015 (1,247 climats, 13,219 ha) and is its Honorary President. The Association's headquarters is the Château du Clos de Vougeot — the Confrérie's own seat. Échezeaux is the Cistercians' vineyard outside the Clos wall. He was also a juror at the 1976 Judgment of Paris (where Roulot's Charmes came second), gave Anne-Claude Leflaive her biodynamics introduction, is Jacques Seysses's close friend, and co-founded Leflaive's École du Vin et des Terroirs. DRC's 2014 release letter also carried a provenance warning ("buy wines only through our official distributor") — Kurniawan-era context a committee of collectors will recognise.

Domaine du Comte Liger-Belair · Échezeaux

Grand Cru · 2014
"Iron fist, velvet glove" · 9th vintage of the cuvée
Parcels
0.6157 ha in six plots across three lieux-dits: Cruots ou Vignes Blanches 0.3299 (two plots running top to bottom), Champs Traversins 0.2546, Clos Saint-Denis 0.0312. Leased 2006 from the Lamadon family (ex-sharecroppers), with Suchots, Petits Monts, Brûlées — first vintage 2006. Vines in 2014: ~40% c. 79 years old, 50% c. 59, 10% 20–40. ~1,950 bottles average. HIGH
Farming
Horse-ploughed since 2002 — "initially he used the same horses as Domaine de la Romanée-Conti"; whole domaine biodynamic 2008; Ecocert 2011, Biodyvin 2012; "No anti-rot treatment… since it is an admission of failure."
Stems 2014
0–15%, most probably fully destemmed MED — no Échezeaux figure ever published; 2015 crus "about 20%… a bit more than usual" (Tanzer) caps 2014 below that; Brûlées is the "test-bed"; Petits Monts "never."
Cellar
"When the grapes are on the sorting table… 95% of the work is done." 14 kg crates, 8 sorters; conveyor, never pumped; cold soak below 15 °C for a week on uncrushed berries; indigenous; ~10 days; pump-overs each morning, "only two punchdowns at the beginning… 'and that was it'"; small vertical press; 100% new oak from three coopers/three forests (incl. Seguin-Moreau) — "even with one-year-old barrels, my wines are too reduced"; no racking, no SO2 until the racking before bottling (10–18 months); "2 to 3 moons" in tank under gas; no fining, no filtration; gravity bottling. HIGH
2014
Harvest from 15 Sept — "serious phenolic ripeness – 12.4–12.9° and there was super deep colour right from the beginning. The first part of aging was difficult, the malos being long and late." Domaine ~30 hl/ha. "A little hail that affected quantity but not quality." "I'm super happy with 2014 vs expectation."
Scores
Neal Martin/WA 95 ("almost Suchots-like… Everything is in its right place") · Burghound 95 · Tanzer 91 ("quite backward and hard to judge") · Öhman 93–94 (2023, from 2027) · CT 94
JancisRobinson.com
10 Nov 2015, 18, drink 2025–42: "Deep crimson. A little more diffuse than the Reignots on the nose. Racy and polished. Refined tannins. Great undertow. Blackberries and tension." Same visit, on the Clos du Château: "This climat always naturally tastes as though it has whole bunch." Her 2025 vertical: the 2009 Échezeaux 19 ("An utter charm-pot"), the 2010 17 ("Big and bold rather than the delicacy that characterises younger vintages").
The thirty-second version

The aristocratic Vosne house rebuilt from almost nothing: General Louis Liger-Belair bought the Château de Vosne in 1815 and the family assembled La Romanée, La Tâche, La Grande Rue and 8 ha of Clos de Vougeot; a forced judicial auction on 31 August 1933 (ten children, two minors) scattered it — La Tâche went, DRC bought it the same year — and Canon Just and Comte Michel bought back only La Romanée, Reignots and Chaumes.
