Essa's summary: "Petit cru proche du village, découpé en bande il est possédé directement par seulement quelques propriétés locales... À côté de ce grand carré les entités n'excèdent jamais plus du demi hectare." Lafon at 0.39 ha and Bouchard at 0.55 ha are therefore among the larger holders after the ~1.2 ha block.

WINE (a): DOMAINE DES COMTES LAFON, MEURSAULT 1ER CRU GOUTTE D'OR 2014

1. The vineyard / climat

See Part A. Lafon-specific framing (from the domaine via Grand Cru Selections): Goutte d'Or is a "vigorous terroir that must be well-managed" on "clay-rich soil on pebbly limestone sub-soil," giving "very powerful wines with ripe aromas and explosive minerality." Dominique treats it as the clay wine of his Meursault range — the counterpoint to Perrières (fine white marl on flaky limestone) and Genevrières-Dessus ("shallow and very light... incredible finesse"). HIGH

Lafon also owns 0.45 ha of village "En Luraule" immediately north of Goutte d'Or (bought 1996, planted 1975), which goes into the villages Meursault and which the domaine describes as giving "very little wine but of extreme minerality and tension" — useful context: the Lafons know this corner of the slope intimately. HIGH

2. Lafon's plot

Item Value Source
Size 0.39 ha (0.96 ac) Wasserman; GCS; Inside Burgundy HIGH
Planting Replanted 1991 (so 23-year-old vines in 2014) Wasserman "Planted in 1991"; GCS "replanted in 1991"; Sokolin HIGH
Position within climat "5–15% slope, facing east" — i.e. the parcel runs up the slope through more than one gradient. Exact sector (north/centre/south): NOT FOUND Wasserman MED
Rootstock 161-49 Wasserman HIGH
Plant material "combined replantation method, with a blend of massal selection and clonal selection (at least 5 different types)" Wasserman HIGH
Pruning "mostly Guyot, with some Cordon Royat" Wasserman HIGH
Wasserman's odd line "This is a small parcel with generous yields and a high proportion of old vines" — contradicts "planted 1991"; likely boilerplate or refers to pre-1991 survivors. Treat with caution LOW
Acquired NOT FOUND. Goutte d'Or is not among the parcels attributed to Jules Lafon's founding purchases in any source I found; Nanson's 2010 profile lists it among the domaine's 1ers crus without a date. Possibly one of the sharecropped parcels Dominique took back in-house after 1984 —
Farming No synthetics since 1992, ploughing generalised 1992; organic conversion from 1995; biodynamic across the whole domaine after 3-year trials; "certified biodynamic in 1998" (GCS) vs. "Organic (certified), biodynamic (since 1998, not certified)" (Wasserman) vs. "certified organic since 1998" (iDealwine). Best reading: organic certification 1998, biodynamic practice since 1998 without Demeter/Biodyvin certification GCS; Wasserman; Nanson HIGH on dates; MED on certification wording
Yields (domaine-wide) "kept low, averaging 35hl/ha over the past decade" GCS HIGH
Horse-ploughing Only documented for the 0.15 ha Monthélie Blanc (28% slope) — not Goutte d'Or GCS HIGH

Talking point: Lafon (1991) and Ente (1990) replanted their Goutte d'Or within a year of each other, so in 2014 both wines came from ~23–24-year-old vines — a rare like-for-like.

3. Winemaking for this cuvée (and 2014 specifics)

General white regime at Comtes Lafon (statements 2010–2022): - Pressing/settling: whole-bunch pressed; settled 24 h in tank; "in recent vintages the fermentation has been started in tank before sending the wine to barrel" (Inside Burgundy, updated ~2022). Indigenous yeasts (Wasserman). HIGH - New oak: Village wines none; 1ers crus "a proportion that varies from 25% to 40%" (GCS, domaine-supplied); Wasserman "up to 33% new"; Inside Burgundy says "up to 70 per cent for Charmes and Perrières; less for Genevrières and 100 per cent for Le Montrachet, though these are subsequently racked into older wood." For Goutte d'Or specifically: NOT FOUND, but it sits in the 25–40% band — I would say "roughly a third new" and cite GCS/Wasserman. The brief's "~30% new" is consistent. MED - Cooper/forest/toast: NOT FOUND for the Lafon whites in any source fetched. - Élevage: Wasserman (Goutte d'Or sheet): "Racked after malolactic. Aged for 18–22 months on fine lees, blended in tank, fining if necessary." GCS: "the wine stays in wood for 16 months." Inside Burgundy: "The wines spend a second winter in barrel, for bottling the following spring or early summer" (Montrachet 18–21 months total). Consensus: two winters in barrel, bottling in spring/early summer of the second year — so the 2014 was bottled roughly spring–summer 2016 MED, inferred. Note the domaine cellars are among the deepest/coldest in Burgundy (Cult Wines: "believed to be some of the deepest and coolest in Burgundy"), which is why fermentations and malos are slow and élevage long. HIGH - Bâtonnage: "Lees-stirring is used sparingly – 'usually when the fermentation is slow to finish or when the wine is reduced', he says" (GCS). Nanson (2010): after the oxidation problems "instead of batonnage they began rolling the barrels to avoid introducing more air but still getting the fine lees into suspension." HIGH - Malolactic: full malo. 2014: "All had quick malos and were often done in Jan/Feb" [2015] — Dominique to Nanson, Oct 2015. Contrast 2013: "Plenty of long and sometimes troublesome malos." HIGH - Racking: at Nanson's visit on 23 Oct 2015, "No racking yet for these wines [the 2014s], except the Monthelie." HIGH - Fining/filtration: "no fining, reds are unfiltered, whites are filtered if necessary" (Wasserman domaine page) vs. "fining if necessary" (Wasserman Goutte d'Or page) vs. GCS "long aging before being racked and fined." Take: light fining/filtration only as needed. MED - SO2: NOT FOUND as a number. René Lafon-era wines were famously low-sulphur/long-élevage; Dominique's answers to premox were physical (see below) rather than a big SO2 increase. LOW - Closure / premox: The domaine had documented premature-oxidation problems (Inside Burgundy: "Dominique's approach to white wine making has at times been controversial and the domaine has had its share of premature oxidation, though the issue appears now to be sorted here"). Responses, in order: barrel-rolling instead of bâtonnage; "since 2004 moved to corks that were only coated in paraffin rather than silicone" (Nanson 2010); and DIAM closures "from the 2013 vintage onwards" (Inside Burgundy). Therefore the 2014 Goutte d'Or should be under DIAM HIGH on the policy; verify on the bottle — MED that the specific bottling used DIAM. This is a significant reassurance to give the table on a 12-year-old Lafon white. - Style: Nanson's 2010 profile noted a "style-shift" from the powerful 1990s toward elegance and "minerality that was quite spellbinding." Inside Burgundy: "Dominique Lafon is aiming for purity in his whites." HIGH

2014 specifics for this wine: - Hail: Nanson's note on the 2014 Goutte d'Or, verbatim: "70% hail losses." (visit 23 Oct 2015). HIGH This is the key fact. The 28 June 2014 storm cut across Beaune, Meursault (village-adjacent and northern 1ers crus), Volnay and Pommard; Nanson's vintage summary: "The southern stretches of Meursault lost hardly anything but closer to the village and to its north losses were up to 70%." Goutte d'Or is the 1er cru closest to the village, at the northern end of the band — the worst-placed of Lafon's six. Gilman on the 2014 Bouchères: "the vineyard of Bouchères was right in the path of the hail." Lafon's Charmes (southern end, by Puligny Combettes) was essentially spared. HIGH - Effect on the wine: Dominique to Nanson: 2012 (also hailed) "has a bit of strong tannin – probably from the hail – but these 2014s not as the hail was earlier" [i.e., late June, before véraison, so the vines re-set leaves and the surviving fruit ripened cleanly]. Sarah Marsh (WFW): the hail "dramatically reduc[ed] yields without affecting quality since it struck before veraison." HIGH - Harvest: Jasper Morris tweeted on 9 Sept 2014: "Lafon starts tomorrow, Ente on thursday" → Lafon began Wednesday 10 September 2014; Nanson saw "Lafon and Roulot... in the vines – Meursault Perrières, mainly" that Wednesday afternoon. Goutte d'Or's own picking date: NOT FOUND (Essa says the cru runs ~2 days later than its neighbours; Lafon's Porusots "still one of last to be harvested"). Reasonable inference: 11–16 Sept 2014. HIGH on domaine start date; LOW on Goutte d'Or day - Production: at 70% loss on 0.39 ha, the 2014 is a very small bottling — order of magnitude 2–3 barrels / 600–900 bottles vs. a normal ~8–9 barrels. My arithmetic, not a sourced figure. LOW

4. The vintage as it applied to this wine

Season (Côte de Beaune, 2014): mild winter; warm March and early budbreak; cool May with frost scares in Chablis 11–12 May; heat in late May gave a quick, successful flowering; 28 June hail (Saturday afternoon, ~16:30, ~5 minutes) hitting Beaune (Clos des Mouches up to 90%), Volnay, Pommard (Rugiens 60–80%), Meursault ("at least 40%" overall; up to 70% nearest the village), plus Echézeaux/Clos de Vougeot in the Nuits; then a wet, cool July–mid August; a dry, sunny, north-wind late August/September saved the whites. Harvest in the Côte de Beaune began tentatively ~8–10 Sept, "full commencement" from 13 Sept, mostly finished by 22 Sept (Inside Burgundy). Drosophila suzukii/sour rot was a red-grape problem; the whites were largely clean. Third consecutive year of hail for Volnay/Pommard/Meursault. HIGH

Critics on 2014 whites in general: Nanson (Nov 2015): "2014 is the greatest white wine vintage that I've ever tasted... If consistency is your only yardstick, 2014 is way out in front... very fine textures, a largely citrus fruit style, and many, many wines deliver their flavour in waves." Jasper Morris: "the most consistent for decades... concentrated, fresh fruit with high acidity." Decanter: Côte d'Or whites 5/5. Sarah Marsh's 2021 retrospective (WFW) was more cautious: "stylish," "haute couture," but most wines faded within 2–3 days of opening, 4 of 14 Côte de Beaune 1ers crus showed oxidative characters early, and for some Meursault "even the premiers crus need drinking up." HIGH

Lafon's 2014s at the domaine: Nanson, 23 Oct 2015: "Reds and whites were really showing brilliantly. Bravo!" Yields: Volnay Clos des Chênes 2 barrels (normal 6); Porusots "only 16 hl/ha from these 45 year-old vines"; Goutte d'Or 70% loss; Dominique's joke: "Good ripeness but not too much. Good acidity but not too much ('all 14s are like that'). Concentrated but not heavy." Dominique on the reds (2015 vs 2005 comparison is for 2015). HIGH

Reviews of THIS wine (2014 Comtes Lafon Goutte d'Or): - Bill Nanson, burgundy-report, from barrel 23 Oct 2015: "70% hail losses. The nose has a grain more of ripeness and is more open, but this is fresh and has a little ginger. More force (it's the clay), more weight, but with lovely waves of flavour. Super yum." HIGH, verbatim - Neal Martin, The Wine Advocate, 30 Dec 2015 (barrel): 91–93. "expressive nose" of "citrus fruit, white peach and a touch of honeysuckle" with "fine mineral tones"; palate "fresh and vibrant with a keen thread of acidity, slightly honeyed" with "marzipan or coconut" on the finish; advises cellaring "3–4 years before welcoming them back home, preferably to your dinner table." HIGH — quoted via Cult Wines - Allen Meadows, Burghound: 90–93 (via Thatcher's Wine). Note text NOT FOUND. For comparison Meadows gave the 2014 Bouchères 91–93 ("sufficiently reduced such that the nose is presently unreadable... racy middle weight flavors... fine bead of minerality"). MED on score - Vinous (Tanzer), 92–93 (via Thatcher's): "Pale, bright green-tinged yellow. Very pure aromas of lemon, lime and honey. Rich and lively on the palate, conveying a strong impression of saline dry extract but also a distinctly fine-grained texture." The vinous.com page for this wine now lists Neal Martin as reviewer (he may have re-tasted it in bottle later; text paywalled). MED - John Gilman (View from the Cellar, Nov 2015): reviewed the Lafon 2014s; Goutte d'Or score NOT FOUND (his Bouchères note says it "does not quite have the same vibrancy as the other Meursault premier crus in the cellar this year" — implying the Goutte d'Or showed more vibrancy). MED - Jasper Morris / Decanter / Jancis: scores NOT FOUND for this cuvée (Decanter gave the 2014 Charmes 93, 2016–2028, and 2014 Bouchères 92). - CellarTracker: community average 93 (Thatcher's citation); page itself blocked (403). LOW

Drinking window opinions: Martin (2015): 3–4 years then drink; Gilman's Bouchères window 2015–2023 suggests the hail-affected wines were seen as forward; Nanson's general 2014 view is of long-lived, acid-driven wines. At 12 years in 2026 and under DIAM, expect a mature-but-fresh wine with Goutte d'Or's weight; Marsh's warning about 2014 Meursault 1ers crus "needing drinking up" applies to conventional-cork wines more than to a DIAM-sealed Lafon. Premox/bottle-variation reports for this specific wine: NOT FOUND. MED

Market: Cult Wines listed the 2014 at US$4,870/12 (~$405/bt) and S$6,660/12; Thatcher's Wine $395/bt; comparison — K&L 2015 $259.99, 2017 $299.99 (both sold out); iDealwine cote 2019 €313; Bourgogne-level 2014 Lafon Goutte d'Or auction hammer: NOT FOUND. Expect US$350–450 retail if found. MED

5. Stories, people, connections

  • Origins. Jules Lafon — Dijon tax official/lawyer, born in the Lot — married Marie Boch on St Vincent's Day 1894; her dowry included the Clos de la Barre house and cellar (built 1869) and a small négoce. Jules bought Meursault and Volnay parcels and the 0.32 ha of Le Montrachet in 1918/1919 (plot 37, south-east corner, next to DRC's holding). "Comte" was a papal title. First domaine bottling 1935. Sons Pierre and Henri, then René (1927–2019) from 1956, who stopped the family from selling, replanted with the sharecroppers' help, and bottled everything from 1961. Dominique (b. 1958) took over in 1984/85 with brother Bruno (who "has since faded into the background" — Nanson), recovered the métayage contracts and took back Montrachet from sharecropper Pierre Morey in 1991. Handed over formally in December 2021 to daughter Léa and nephew Pierre. HIGH
  • La Paulée de Meursault. Jules Lafon, as mayor, revived/organised the end-of-harvest paulée as a formal banquet (English-Wikipedia: "formally organized in the mid-1920s by the Count Lafon and Jacques Prieur"; most sources say 1923; fr.wikipedia says 1932 — go with "1920s, 1923 by most accounts" MED). It is the third of the Trois Glorieuses — with the Hospices de Beaune auction and the Confrérie's Clos de Vougeot dinner — and Dominique Lafon has helped run it. Direct Tastevin link: the Lafons are the founding family of one leg of the Confrérie-anchored November weekend. Whether Dominique is himself intronised as a Chevalier/Commandeur: NOT FOUND — a question worth asking the table rather than asserting.
  • Biodynamic pioneer. Herbicides out 1992; organic 1995; 3-year biodynamic trials; whole domaine biodynamic from 1998 — "not very popular in the region, except by a few other pioneers, like Anne-Claude Leflaive and Lalou Bize-Leroy" (GCS). Approach is pragmatic not dogmatic — La Route des Blancs: "pragmatism, sincere humility and capacity to observe." The Charmes is the wine he says improved most under biodynamics (from "very opulent" to "considerably more tension"). HIGH
  • Premox and honesty. Lafon is one of the few top domaines to have openly acknowledged premox and changed practices (rolling barrels, paraffin corks 2004, DIAM from 2013). Good quote for the table (Inside Burgundy): "Dominique's approach to white wine making has at times been controversial." HIGH
  • Quotes. On 2010 Perrières: "My most favourite Perrières – crystal-pure... I don't know if I'll ever make it so good again." On Genevrières: "Not the best wine of Meursault but often the tastiest." On the 2016 whites: "The whites make me think of my 2000s – and I think I made very good 2000s." (all to Nanson.) Daniel Johnnes: "There are few growers anywhere in the world more committed to improving his vineyards... He tirelessly questions his and others' methods." HIGH
  • Other hats. Les Héritiers du Comte Lafon (Mâcon, from 1999); Dominique Lafon négoce label (~4.5 ha, incl. a Puligny 1er Champ-Gains "once worked by Comtes Lafon"), made from 2015 at Château de Bligny with Pierre Murgey; consultant to Evening Land (Oregon) — from Nanson 2015. HIGH
  • Levi Dalton, I'll Drink to That: an episode with Dominique Lafon exists (linked from Becky Wasserman & Co. and Grand Cru Selections pages); episode number/content NOT FETCHED. MED
  • Connections to Ente: same village; both hold Volnay 1er Santenots-du-Milieu (Lafon 3.78 ha, Ente 0.39–0.49 ha) and Puligny Champ-Gains (Ente 0.24 ha; Lafon's négoce); BBR positions Ente "between the generous character of Dominique Lafon and the mineral-driven precision of Coche Dury"; Vinovest: Ente's wines combine "the precision and citrus of Coche-Dury with the richness of the wines by Dominique Lafon." Burgundy Cave sells Lafon as the value alternative "compared to the other top white producers like Coche Dury, Arnaud Ente and Roulot." Both replanted Goutte d'Or 1990/91. HIGH
  • Importers: Lafon — Becky Wasserman & Co. (export agent) / Grand Cru Selections (US importer, NY–CA–IL). Ente — Kermit Lynch (US). No shared importer. HIGH

WINE (b): DOMAINE ARNAUD ENTE, MEURSAULT 1ER CRU LA GOUTTE D'OR 2014

1. The vineyard / climat

See Part A. Ente labels it "La Goutte d'Or" (singular, historic form). Kermit Lynch's sheet: "Eastern and western sun exposure, mid-slope" (their boilerplate for every Ente parcel — treat "mid-slope" as LOW). La Route des Blancs: the Goutte d'Or is what admirers consider Ente's "grand cru" ("son Grand Cru pour ses admirateurs" — paraphrase). MED

2. Ente's plot

Item Value Source
Size 0.22 ha Kermit Lynch sheet; Inside Burgundy (Sep 2022); bestofwines HIGH
Planting 1990 (24-year-old vines in 2014) Kermit Lynch; FINE+RARE ("planted in 1990") HIGH
Origin Part of the ~3.5 ha of Thévenot family vines Arnaud was offered by his father-in-law in 1990 and took over in 1992 (La Route des Blancs; BBR names Philippe Thévenot; Inside Burgundy: "vineyards farmed from the Thévenot family"; Kermit Lynch: domaine "Founded: 1992") HIGH — not an Ente-family parcel
Sector within climat NOT FOUND
Rootstock/clones NOT FOUND
Farming Kermit Lynch (older sheet): "Lutte Raisonnée." Inside Burgundy: "The domaine has been certified organic since 2014" — so 2014 is the first certified-organic vintage HIGH on the statement; MED on whether certification applied to the 2014 harvest. Burgundy Cave claims "now operates biodynamically" LOW. Practices (La Route des Blancs, iDealwine): no synthetic fertilisers; cover crops (féverole, épeautre, pois, vesce, prairie); fewer tractor passes to preserve soil porosity; Guyot-Poussard pruning to protect sap flow; yields "measured"/"minuscule"
Yields Domaine 2013 Goutte d'Or: 27 hl/ha, 12.9% alc. (Tanzer/IWC via K&L). Kermit Lynch: neighbours "joke that they aren't sure how the Entes put food on the table when they observe such minuscule yields." Total production ~1,250 cases/yr for ~4 ha HIGH
Team Arnaud, Marie-Odile, two employees; sons Pierre (now full-time, reds) and Louis HIGH

Tight canopy/leaf-thinning/sorting specifics: NOT FOUND in a quotable source. What is documented is "meticulous husbandry" (Morris) and the claim, from Morris, that Ente's early picking is a result of superior vineyard work: "his ultra-meticulous work in the vineyards enables him to ripen his grapes that little bit earlier." HIGH

3. Winemaking for this cuvée (and 2014 specifics)

Sources disagree on details and the regime changed over time; give the 2014-era picture:

  • Harvest timing: Ente became "a byword for early picking in a way that has unfairly attracted criticism from others in the village" (Inside Burgundy). Switch from late-picking opulence (1990s) to earlier picking "since 2000" (Morris) / "early-2000s" (LRDB), inspired, he says, by great Alsace Riesling, "comme le Clos Sainte-Hune qu'il cite volontiers." Jasper Morris's tweet of 9 Sept 2014: "Ente on thursday" → Ente began harvest Thursday 11 September 2014, one day after Lafon and among the first in Meursault. HIGH
  • Pressing/settling: BBR: "White grapes sometimes crushed before pressing; 24-hour must settlement." Sommpicks: "Grapes crushed before gentle pressing; juice settles 24 hours pre-racking." Vinovest: "crushing and hydraulic pressing." LRDB (his own evolution): "les pressurages sont plus doux, les débourbages sont plus serrés" — softer pressing, tighter settling for aromatic precision. iDealwine: "long, gentle pressing." MED-HIGH
  • Fermentation vessel / oak: Kermit Lynch: "20% maximum new oak for all wines"; BBR: "New oak reduced from 35% to 20% for top cuvées"; "Large 600-liter barrels [demi-muids] used for entry-level whites"; Sommpicks: "aging in barrels and demi-muids." Vessel evolution per LRDB: "Des pièces, il passe au demi-muids, plus grands, puis aux foudres, avant d'expérimenter depuis quelques années déjà les globes de verre" (glass "Wine Globes" first used for tiny 2016 lots, bought in size for 2018+; Brasserie du Théâtre 2024: barrels now eliminated entirely). In 2014 the Goutte d'Or would still have been in pièces/demi-muids with ≤20% new oak MED. Cooper: NOT FOUND.
  • Élevage: Kermit Lynch: "twelve months in barrel and six months in stainless"; BBR/bestofwines/Sommpicks: "11 months in barrel, then 6 months in tank." → ~17–18 months total; 2014 bottled ~spring 2016 MED, inferred.
  • Malolactic / bâtonnage: NOT FOUND (no bâtonnage figure anywhere; assume full malo).
  • Fining/filtration: "Bottled without fining or filtration" (BBR, bestofwines, Vinovest, Sommpicks). HIGH
  • SO2: NOT FOUND as a number.
  • Closure/premox: NOT FOUND (no DIAM statement found for Ente). Morris says the 1990s wines were "fleshier but less stable than today." Reductive style noted: Sommpicks — shift toward "a leaner, more focused, and reductive style" using "foudres, glass, and steel vessels"; Brasserie du Théâtre on the Wine Globe: "eliminating oxidation and wood influence while reducing evaporation to a minimum." MED
  • Style statement (his own words, LRDB): "Si la qualité et la vérité du raisin sont là, sur le pied de vigne, mon objectif est d'en perdre le moins possible." And: "L'équilibre, toujours l'équilibre." Kermit Lynch: "Precision, purity, elegance, and finesse are the best descriptors of the Ente style... the baffling paradox of intense old-vine concentration with crystalline weightlessness." HIGH
  • Label: new archer label from 2009 — Morris: "reflects the purity and precision perfectly." The 2014 wears the archer label. HIGH

2014 specifics for this wine: Ente-specific yield/hail figures for the 2014 Goutte d'Or: NOT FOUND. Given the climat-wide picture (Lafon −70%; "closer to the village... losses were up to 70%"), assume Ente's 0.22 ha was also heavily hailed — plausibly only one barrel or less (a normal year at ~27–30 hl/ha on 0.22 ha is ~6 hl ≈ 2.5 barrels; 2014 likely ≤1 barrel). Note that in the 2016 frost Decanter reported "The Goutte d'Or suffered losses of 80–90%" at Ente, so this parcel has been serially unlucky. LOW on 2014 numbers; HIGH on 2016

4. The vintage as it applied to this wine

Season narrative as above (Part A / Lafon §4). Ente-specific comment on 2014: NOT FOUND (Ente does not receive critics at the domaine in the way Lafon does; Nanson has no Ente report; the domaine is "totally closed to visits").

Reviews of THIS wine (2014 Arnaud Ente Meursault 1er Cru La Goutte d'Or): - Allen Meadows, Burghound: 91–94 (listed by Pleasure Wine, Belgium). Note text NOT FOUND. MED - Stephen Tanzer, Vinous: a review exists (vinous.com wine page lists Tanzer as reviewer for the 2014); score and text paywalled — NOT FOUND. For calibration, Tanzer's 2013: 90–92, "Dense and sweet... good salty minerality," 27 hl/ha, 12.9%. MED that review exists - Wine Advocate: NOT FOUND for 2014 (Neal Martin's Dec 2015 Burgundy report covered Lafon; whether he tasted Ente's 2014s is unclear). 2015: WA 94. [—] - Jasper Morris: 2014 note NOT FOUND (his 2015 and 2017 notes exist behind the paywall; 2019 92–95). - Jancis Robinson: 2014 NOT FOUND; 2013 = 17/20 "Vibrant... Pure and tense." - John Gilman: 2014 NOT FOUND; 2013 = 94, "passion fruit, apple, pear, almonds, chalky soil tones, citrus zest and vanillin oak," drink 2020–2045. - CellarTracker/Vivino: no 2014 entry found on Vivino's vintage list (which shows 2013 at 4.6 with 30+ ratings); CellarTracker blocked. This suggests the 2014 is genuinely scarce in circulation. MED

Neighbouring-vintage benchmarks for the same wine (useful to frame expectations): 2013 Burghound 90–92, Gilman 94, Tanzer 90–92, Jancis 17; 2015 WA 94, Gilman 94, Burghound 90–92 ("Exotic characters on ripe white fruit... hints of viognier"); 2016 Decanter "one of the highlights of the Ente range" despite 80–90% loss; 2018 Morris 95, Gilman 94+; 2019 Kelley 97 ("a remarkable wine... pear, freshly baked bread, clear honey, white flowers and crisp stone fruit," 2027–2060), Morris 92–95; 2020 Gilman 96+, Morris 95; 2022 WA 95. HIGH

Drinking window / premox: No bottle-variation or premox reports for the 2014 found. Ente's post-2000 wines are consistently described as long-lived and tense; Gilman's windows for the 2013 (2020–2045) and Kelley's for the 2019 (2027–2060) suggest a 2014 in 2026 is early-plateau. MED

Market: iDealwine cote (75 cl): 2013 €996, 2015 €981, 2012 €1,083, 2016 €956, 2017 €969, 2018 €881 — 2014 not listed (rare at auction). Sommpicks range for other vintages $1,399–2,266; BBR 2013 £1,024; Vivino 2022 $2,743; Vinovest: 2006 at $1,950. Expect the 2014 at €900–1,100 / US$1,200–1,800 if it surfaces. Direct-from-domaine price for 1ers crus in 2012 was ~€130 (La Passion du Vin forum) — a ~8–10x secondary-market multiple, which is the whole "cult label" story in one number. MED

5. Stories, people, connections

  • Biography. Arnaud Ente, born 1966; family from the Nord (Armentières); father married the daughter of Puligny vigneron Camille David; brother Benoît was the one expected to take over the family tradition (Benoît Ente has his own Puligny domaine). Arnaud studied biology, failed to get into physiotherapy school, did military service, met Marie-Odile Thévenot, married her in 1991; in 1990 his father-in-law offered him 3.5 ha of family vines in Meursault; he accepted after two days' thought, enrolled in a two-year alternating viticulture/oenology course, and took over the vines in 1992 (La Route des Blancs; Inside Burgundy; Vinovest). Address: 12 rue de Mazeray, Meursault. HIGH
  • Coche-Dury — verify status: Retailer/importer sources (BBR: "After working at the legendary Coche Dury"; bestofwines; Sommpicks; Vinovest; wineonp) say he worked at Coche-Dury. Jasper Morris's Inside Burgundy overview and Kermit Lynch's sheet do not mention it. La Route des Blancs (long French profile with domaine input) says only: "Il n'hésite pas non plus à demander conseil, à ses débuts, à un vigneron qu'il admire, Jean-François Coche, qui n'aura de cesse de l'encourager à suivre son chemin." — i.e., Coche as mentor/adviser, not employer. Verdict: MED that he did a stage/period at Coche-Dury; HIGH that Jean-François Coche was an early mentor. Safe phrasing: "mentored by, and by some accounts briefly worked with, Jean-François Coche."
  • The style arc. 1990s: late-picked, opulent wines that first drew attention (Morris first tasted them mid-1990s on Jean-Philippe Fichet's recommendation); from ~2000: earlier picking, "a more chiselled approach." Morris's two hypotheses — does he like less-ripe grapes, or does his vineyard work ripen them earlier? — resolved in favour of the latter. HIGH
  • Bettane & Desseauve "Homme de l'année" (announced Sept 2012, for the 2013 guide): Michel Bettane: his Chardonnays are "the most accomplished wines in terms of overall balance" in Burgundy and possibly the world; LRDB renders it as "le plus grand vinificateur de chardonnay sur la planète." Arnaud's reaction: "C'est une vraie surprise, je ne pensais pas que le domaine pouvait être hissé à ce rang." Thierry Desseauve: the aim was partly to "showcase vignerons that most enthusiasts don't know." HIGH
  • Cult status / secrecy. Kermit Lynch: "rarely seen outside of his vines or cellar"; the domaine is "totally closed to visits" (Brasserie du Théâtre); French forum members in 2012 debated whether Ente was a "discovery" given that serious collectors had "been discussed for a decade"; demand strongest in France and Japan (vinsdebourgogne.nl). LRDB: "faisant l'objet d'un véritable culte aux quatre coins du monde." HIGH
  • Innovation. Glass jars for the tiny 2016 lots ("because there was not enough volume for even a single barrel of certain vineyards"), then purpose-bought glass globes from 2018; Morris: "Arnaud does not wish them to become the story." HIGH
  • Succession. Son Pierre full-time (reds: 40% whole-cluster, semi-carbonic 3–4 days, no pigeage, stainless élevage — Kermit Lynch 2016 data); son Louis presented wines in London 2018. HIGH
  • Connections to Lafon / the dinner: see Lafon §5. Additionally, Ente's other Meursault parcels (En l'Ormeau, Petits Charrons, Casse-Têtes) are village sites below and behind the village on the same north side as Goutte d'Or; Ente's Puligny Referts abuts Meursault Charmes-Dessous, close to Lafon's Charmes. Ente's Volnay Santenots-du-Milieu (planted 1992) and Lafon's (3.78 ha) are the same climat. HIGH
  • Confrérie des Chevaliers du Tastevin / Tastevinage: nothing found linking Ente to the Confrérie or Tastevinage; he is, by reputation, the anti-institutional figure of Meursault. NOT FOUND.

HEAD-TO-HEAD CHEAT SHEET (2014, same climat)

Comtes Lafon Arnaud Ente
Parcel 0.39 ha, replanted 1991, rootstock 161-49, massal+clonal 0.22 ha, planted 1990, Thévenot family vines
Farming in 2014 Organic (cert. 1998) + biodynamic since 1998 First certified-organic vintage (2014); Guyot-Poussard
Harvest start 2014 Wed 10 Sept (Perrières first) Thu 11 Sept
Hail 28 June 2014 −70% on Goutte d'Or (Lafon to Nanson) NOT FOUND; climat lost up to 70% near village
Oak ~25–40% new (1er cru band), 16–22 months, two winters, DIAM from 2013 ≤20% new, pièces/demi-muids, ~11–12 mo + 6 mo tank, unfined/unfiltered
House reading of the site "It's the clay" — power, weight, "explosive minerality" Precision/tension; early-picked; ~27 hl/ha, ~12.9% (2013 data)
2014 scores Martin 91–93; Burghound 90–93; Vinous 92–93; Nanson "Super yum" Burghound 91–94; Tanzer (paywalled)
Market ~US$400/bt ~€900–1,100/bt (2014 rare)

B3 — Lafon & Coche-Dury Perrières 2014 10,278 words

Meursault 1er Cru Les Perrières 2014 — Domaine des Comtes Lafon & Domaine Coche-Dury

Dossier for the Claud dinner, 26 Oct 2026. Companion to the Lafon Goutte d'Or 2014 and Coche-Dury Rougeots 2011 files, which carry the full domaine biographies; this file concentrates on the Perrières climat, the two parcels, and the two 2014 bottles.

Confidence key: HIGH = stated by a primary source (domaine, importer) or by two independent expert sources; MED = single expert/critic source, or sources mildly disagree; LOW = retailer/community/secondary source only, or inference.

PART A — THE CLIMAT: MEURSAULT 1ER CRU LES PERRIÈRES

A1. Location, size, exposure, altitude

  • Position. Southernmost of Meursault's great central band of premiers crus, on the Puligny side of the commune. It is "the only one of these three 1ers [Perrières, Charmes, Genevrières] that sits entirely above the road to Puligny" (Bill Nanson, burgundy-report.com, 2015, updated 2022) HIGH. Charmes (c. 31 ha) lies directly below it across the road; Genevrières (c. 16 ha) is its neighbour to the north; to the south "everything south of Meursault-Perrières is indeed in Puligny-Montrachet" — chiefly Puligny 1er Champ Canet, with Chalumaux and Blagny higher up (Nanson) HIGH. Stephen Tanzer describes Lafon's main parcel as being "at the northern end of the appellation, adjacent to Genevrières-Dessus" (Vinous, Dec 2019) HIGH.
  • Size. 13 ha 21 a 17 ca (Patrick Essa, vigneron at Buisson-Charles, degustateurs.pro, citing the cadastre) HIGH; Nanson: "a little over 13 hectares" with 27 owners on Laurent Gotti's 2015 producer map HIGH; Nanson's 2019 Grivault article gives 13.21 ha total HIGH. (Older secondary sources say 13.71 ha — Jockovino 2010 LOW.)
  • Sub-lieux-dits (cadastral, per Essa): Aux Perrières 0 ha 79 a 90 ca; Les Perrières Dessus 3 ha 76 a 29 ca; Les Perrières Dessous 9 ha 59 a 50 ca (of which the Clos des Perrières = 95 a, leaving 8 ha 64 a 98 ca of "open" Dessous) HIGH.
  • Exposure/altitude. "Perfect east–southeast exposure" (Jasper Morris MW, Sotheby's Epicurean's Atlas, 2023) HIGH. Lafon's parcels: "East/southeast facing with slopes between 10-16%" (Becky Wasserman & Co. tech sheet) HIGH. The Clos des Perrières sits at 260 m (Pascal Ribéreau-Gayon, quoted by Sommeliers International) MED. Winewithseth gives 280–310 m for the climat as a whole LOW. Essa notes Dessous lies roughly twenty metres below Dessus, separated by the old quarry HIGH.

A2. Name etymology — the quarry

  • The name is not "little stones." Jasper Morris: "the derivation of Perrières is not 'little stones' but a reference to a former quarry here, dating back centuries to a time when limestone was needed to build the houses in the village" (Sotheby's, 2023) HIGH. iDealwine derives it from Latin petrarium (stone quarry) MED.
  • Nanson: "Perrières is not an uncommon name in Burgundy … all take their names from the ancient extraction of stone to build the local villages – quarries. Among other places and houses, the quarried stone from here was used for the bell-tower of the church in the centre of Meursault" HIGH.
  • Essa: the higher block (Dessus + Aux Perrières) "surplombe des carrières qui ont fortement entaillé le coteau" — the quarries cut deeply into the slope; what was extracted was "le marbre de Bourgogne ou pierre de Chassagne," used for the walls and houses of the bourg. He adds that over time "le Cru a ainsi perdu deux hectares disparus en poussière" — roughly two hectares of the climat were literally quarried away into dust HIGH, single but expert local source.
  • The quarry is still visible in the appellation map: right in the middle of the vineyard there are parcels (Château de Meursault, de Montille) "actually only rated as 'Bourgogne' or 'Villages Meursault' – due to being a quarry hole that was back-filled with soil and rubble" (Nanson) HIGH. Essa confirms: the partially filled quarry "porte encore des Vignes d'appellation « Bourgogne blanc »," and it "separates clearly the Dessus from the Dessous" HIGH. William Kelley, quoted on Domaine Pierre Morey's site: "the composition of the soils suggests that this was the location where rejected stones from the quarry that lends the climat its name were discarded" MED. Anne Morey: it is essentially "a heap of stones with vines growing straight out of the stones" MED.
  • Jockovino's claim that "no stone was actually extracted there" is contradicted by every local source and should be disregarded HIGH.

A3. Soils and geology

  • Nanson (2015): "Geologically, the base stone is quite homogenous on a Chassagne stone lower down – a hard oolite limestone. There's more argilles and clay higher up with some Comblanchien limestone. Often there's no more than 20-30 centimetres of soil before the mother-rock." The upper sections are "a mix of the very stony and sometimes a little more sandy soil. Towards the top, under the trees there is often some degeneration of the vines – virus issues – mainly court-noué" HIGH.
  • Lafon's own description (via Grand Cru Selections/Kellogg importer sheets): "topsoil quite fine and white due to the high amount of limestone and marl, subsoils are flaked limestone" HIGH. Becky Wasserman: "Shallow white limestone and marl soil on crumbly limestone subsoil" HIGH. Tanzer: Lafon's main parcel is on "mostly whitish marl soil," the steep 1982 parcel on "slightly darker soil," the ex-Gauffroy parcel on "more reddish soil" HIGH.
  • Clos des Perrières: "Bathonian limestone at higher elevations; stony, red colluvium soil below" (Sommeliers International, on Grivault) MED.
  • Louis Latour: "a soil that is thinner and stonier than many of its immediate neighbours" HIGH.
  • Essa's sector-by-sector reading (the single most detailed published description) HIGH as a vigneron's testimony, but interpretive:
  • "Grande Perrière" — in Dessous, running on from Genevrières-Dessus "jusqu'à la grotte … de la 'Porre et Piarde'"; gives "the most civilised wines of the climat," closest to the great upper Genevrières facing it: "plus opulents, plus sensuels et plus immédiatement accessibles que le reste du Cru." Fairly steep; "terres blondes et caillouteuses" with good water retention and frost protection; rows run east–west; parcels from 25 ares to over 2 ha. This is where Lafon's main 1955 parcel sits (Tanzer places it at the northern end adjacent to Genevrières-Dessus).
  • "Perrières Dessus" — more fragmented, high and low parcels with variable orientation; gives a "côté rocailleux" that thickens the grain of the texture and "finit par imprimer une sensation quasi coupante sur la langue. Vin de cailloux sur des terres maigres" — evoking upper Chevalier or the rigidity of Goutte d'Or, but denser. Many walls and terraces "gagnées sur le calcaire dur à force de ténacité … Vins de pierres, mais sans doute aussi vin de sang et de labeur." This is where Coche-Dury's vines are.
  • "Plat des Perrières" — in Dessous, just above the "grande Charmes" (Charmes-Dessus), with the Clos as its epicentre, extending right to the Grande Perrière and left as a tongue that butts against Puligny Champ Canet. Nearly flat, slightly inclined east, stony. Essa: the Perrières from this sector "sont sans conteste les plus grands Bourgogne blancs par leur intensité," at Montrachet's level of complexity "mais sur un registre moins visqueux, plus salin et iodé." Lafon's steep 2-ouvrée 1982 parcel is just south of the Clos and just above his Charmes-Dessus (Tanzer).
  • Character consensus: Nanson — "Good Perrières is often described as having a more linear, direct and mineral intensity – like the wines of Puligny"; "Perrières is a relatively early ripening terroir, similar to Charmes below" HIGH. Jasper Morris: "Perrières is certainly the weightiest of the Meursault vineyards and it should be the longest-lived … all the minerality of Genevrières, while the rounded appeal of Charmes shows here with greater power" HIGH. Charles Curtis MW (Decanter 2022): "Perrières is a warm terroir that ripens early" HIGH. Essa: "vin tellurique, énergique, violent, à la sauvagerie quasi dérangeante, qui déroute souvent lorsqu'il est jeune" HIGH.

A4. Perrières-Dessus vs Perrières-Dessous vs Clos des Perrières — which is best?

  • Nanson: unlike Charmes and Genevrières (where the upper sections are better regarded), "in Perrières this is not the case." Producers "agree [Perrières-Dessous] to be the best part of this cru"; "amongst others, Coche-Dury and Bouchard Père might have another view, as most of their vines lie in the higher Perrières-Dessus section!" HIGH.
  • Essa (above) also ranks the flat Dessous/Clos sector highest, then the Grande Perrière, with Dessus giving the most "cutting," stony wines HIGH.
  • Yet Nanson's own tasting conclusion is that the Perrières-ness of the wines transcends the tiers: "these differences seem no more marked in the different tiers versus 50 metres apart in the same sections" HIGH. And the market's verdict is unambiguous: the most expensive Perrières in the world (Coche-Dury) comes from Dessus.
  • Clos des Perrières. 94 a 52 ca (Grivault, via Sommeliers International; Nanson says 0.95 ha; Essa "un peu moins d'un hectare") HIGH. "A square Clos of vines that follows the road for 100 metres or-so … perfectly encompassed by a wall and sitting next to the road" (Nanson) HIGH. Created "by the Marquis de la Roche" at the beginning of the 19th century (Essa) — the Grivault website spells it "Marquis de La Troche"; Nanson writes "Marquess de la Roche who died with no heirs in 1879" MED on the name; the family and date are consistent. Albert Grivault (b. Meursault 1856, a distiller with a second distillery in Béziers) bought it from the heirs in 1879, aged 23 (Grivault website; Nanson) HIGH. Essa: buying "ce pré-carré en 1879 est assurément un coup de génie"; Grivault also created the Clos du Murger by his house and bought Clos Blanc in Pommard (planted in red) and Clos de Vougeot. In 1904 Albert Grivault bequeathed his Meursault Charmes to the Hospices de Beaune — the Cuvée Albert Grivault still sold every November (Grivault website) HIGH. The Clos is a monopole, now run by the fourth generation (Claire Bardet, manager; Henri-Marc Bardet, vinification; Michel Bardet still involved) HIGH. The domaine holds 1.5 ha of Perrières in total and is "the largest owner of the iconic Meursault 1er cru 'Perrières'" (Nanson 2019) HIGH. The 1893 Clos won a gold medal at the 1895 Paris show and was served at President Félix Faure's table (Sommeliers International) MED.
  • Nanson on the Clos: "excellent, and indeed a little different as it usually shows a more floral aromatic (heat of the Clos?) but no-one except the Bardets would say that it is the best" HIGH.

A5. The grand-cru-that-never-was

  • 19th-century standing. Jasper Morris: "Dr Lavalle, the expert 19th-century author of the first book about Burgundy to make a concerted effort to classify the vineyards, picked out Perrières as being the best white wine vineyard after the Montrachets, classing it as a rare tête de cuvée" (Sotheby's 2023) HIGH. Doctorwine: "From early nineteenth-century maps onward, it held the designation 'tête de cuvée, the highest quality accolade of the time'" MED. Essa: "sa réputation ancestrale l'a toujours positionné comme second après le 'Vrai Montrachet'" HIGH. Grivault's site cites Lavalle (1855) and Danguy & Aubertin (1892) as praising the Clos HIGH. The intermediate classifications (Beaune Comité d'agriculture 1860, Camille Rodier 1920) are known to have followed Lavalle's hierarchy with adjustments (monocepage.com) — I could NOT retrieve the specific Perrières line in Rodier; treat "tête de cuvée in Lavalle, top rank retained by Rodier" as MED.
  • The 1936–37 decision. Meursault "was not blessed with any Grands Crus when those jewels were handed out in the 1930s" (Jasper Morris) HIGH. The most specific published explanation comes from Patrick Essa (a Meursault vigneron): at the time of the AOC classification in the 1930s, "les décideurs des syndicats communaux … n'ont pas souhaité lui retirer la mention 'Meursault' en des temps où le consacrer grand Cru revenait à le nommer: « Les Perrières »." In other words, a grand cru would have been labelled simply "Les Perrières" (as Montrachet, Corton-Charlemagne etc. carry no village name), and the local syndicate preferred to keep the commercially valuable word Meursault on the label. He adds: "On sait aussi que des groupes de producteurs d'obédiences différentes se sont farouchement querellés pour générer ces décisions" — rival producer factions fought bitterly over it HIGH as testimony; the archival paper trail is not online. Raphaël Coche gives a compatible version: "when the time came to codify the appellations, the committees in each village were different — an explanation for the fact that there are no grands crus in Meursault," summarised in his line "Tout est sous-classé à Meursault" (Decanter, Charles Curtis MW, 2022) HIGH.
  • Essa's critique of the outcome: the general classification "le 'minore' et fait fi de son incomparable caractère de vin de pierres, taillé pour la très grande garde," leaving it "assez loin de son vrai rang de meilleur grand Cru après le Montrachet." He also raises the sceptic's question: with yields now roughly 2.5× the 15 hl/ha of the 1850s and planting density halved, does modern Perrières still dominate as it once did? HIGH
  • The Grivault dossier. Nanson: "For many years, the Bardet family of Clos des Perrières have built a dossier with the aim of proposing their 'Clos des Perrières' for grand cru status." Other producers "will tell you that an AOC change only for the Clos des Perrières would always be opposed by other owners in the lower Perrières-Dessous section … but that if that whole lower section was considered, there might be some consensus" — while Coche-Dury and Bouchard, mostly in Dessus, "might have another view" HIGH. Winewithseth claims lobbying "stalled at INAO since early 2000s" LOW. Jasper Morris's own position: "There might indeed be a case for now promoting Les Perrières, but better perhaps to remain as the outstanding Premier Cru in Meursault." He adds the grand cru-style argument that Perrières "has the additional merit … of being good every year, not just in the great vintages" HIGH.
  • Useful dinner line: Nanson — "I don't find the weight of the grand crus of Puligny and Chassagne to the south, but weight alone is not really interesting, Perrières has energy and intensity – as good as any grand cru – and that's very interesting!"

A6. Ownership map (27 owners; figures as found)
