So the brief's "Dujac largest holder after Ponsot" is verified (WHWC: "Rousseau ranks as the third-largest proprietor here, after Ponsot and Dujac").

PART B — DOMAINE ARMAND ROUSSEAU, CLOS DE LA ROCHE GRAND CRU 2010

B1. The producer's plot

  • Size: 1.48 ha in two parcels, vinified together. HIGH — Wikipedia; Coates/Decanter 2011; Rare Wine Invest 2022; Lambier
  • Location: Curtis (Decanter 2024): "a plot at the southern extremity of lieu-dit Clos de la Roche and another larger one (almost 1 ha) in Fremières." WHWC (retailer, 2022 vintage) breaks it as ~1 ha Les Fremières + ~0.48 ha original Clos de la Roche. MED-HIGH
  • Les Fremières lies on the lower/east side, straddling the Route des Grands Crus side of the appellation (below the original clos) — i.e. Rousseau's larger parcel is lower-slope, 1936-annexed ground; the smaller is in the "rock". MED — inferred from the lieu-dit position described by Curtis; treat the slope-position claim as MED
  • Acquisition date — sources conflict:
  • Bill Nanson, Burgundy-Report profile (July 2005): Clos de la Roche acquired 1920 (his list: Charmes 1919, CDLR 1920, Chambertin 1921/1943/1956, Mazis 1937, Mazoyères 1940, Bèze 1954, Clos St-Jacques 1954, Ruchottes 1977). MED
  • domaine-rousseau.com history page: Armand, after his 1909 marriage, "acquired prestigious parcels including Charmes-Chambertin, Clos de la Roche, and Chambertin", then Mazy and Mazoyères in the 1930s. → consistent with a 1920s Armand-era first purchase. HIGH that Armand bought the first piece
  • Wikipedia EN: "Acquired in portions: initial purchase in late 1910s–1920s, then 1.48 hectares in 1965 and 1975." Decanter (Coates, 2011) and WineWiki: "acquired 1965 and again 1975".
  • Reconciliation MED: Armand bought the first Clos de la Roche piece c. 1920; Charles enlarged/reshaped the holding with purchases in 1965 and 1975 to reach the present 1.48 ha in two parcels. Say it this way.
  • Vine age / replanting: Neal Martin (Wine Advocate #210, Dec 2013, on the 2012): "part of the vineyard was replanted five years ago" → a replanting c. 2008; in 2010 those young vines were 2 years old and would not have been in the grand cru (young vines typically excluded or declassified — NOT CONFIRMED for Rousseau specifically). Otherwise vine age NOT FOUND. MED
  • Farming: no herbicides; insecticides stopped c. 2000 (Sotheby's); "not biodynamic" (Coates 2011); the domaine's own site describes mechanical soil work, green harvest, leaf removal, yields 30–40 hl/ha; Frederick Wildman lists "organic/biodynamic/sustainable practices" but there is no organic certification (Sotheby's: "the estate lacks organic certification"). Guyot; ~11–12,000 vines/ha (WineWiki/Sotheby's). MED-HIGH
  • Charles Rousseau's famous massale-selection vines: Coates: "very fine vines from exceptional sélection massale." MED

B2. Winemaking for this cuvée

Whole cluster — every figure found

Statement Refers to Made in Source
"Historically 100% destemmed; Eric experimented with whole cluster in 2009" domaine-wide 2011 Decanter Rousseau profile (Clive Coates)
"Normally destemmed fruit (exception: 2009 vintage used 15% whole clusters)"; "No sorting table; selection in the vineyard" Chambertin/Bèze context 2011 Decanter, Coates on Gevrey-Chambertin
"90% destemmed" domaine-wide 2005 Nanson, Burgundy-Report profile
"After destemming, the grapes are cooled to 15°C then macerated for 18 to 20 days" domaine-wide (Pyramide vertical) 2022 Jasper Morris, insideburgundy.com
"Berries are destemmed but not crushed" Clos de Bèze c. 2019 Sotheby's Epicurean's Atlas
"About 20% whole clusters were incorporated across the entire range" 2015 2016 Nanson, Burgundy-Report "Armand Rousseau – 2015" (Frédéric Robert)
"about 15 percent of grapes not destemmed" recent, domaine-wide undated (c. 2020s) Falstaff winery page
"Generally focus on whole berries; may use a small amount of stems"; 4–5 day cold maceration domaine-wide undated Berkeley & Stuart (importer)
"Approximately 90% destemmed; 10% whole-bunch reserved for Grand Crus in suitable vintages" domaine-wide undated WineWiki (secondary, LOW)
"The grapes are destemmed and aged in used casks" Clos de la Roche specifically Oct 2024 Decanter, Charles Curtis MW
"20–40 percent whole-cluster… Charles maintained this in the 1980s–90s" domaine-wide undated enprimeurclub.com — contradicts all primary-adjacent sources; treat as unreliable LOW
Nanson's tasting note on 2010 Clos St-Jacques (Mainz, Nov 2013): "There seems to be some little whole-cluster notes on the nose" 2010 CSJ 2013 Burgundy-Report "Top-table 2010 reds" — a perception, not a statement of regime

Confidence range for Rousseau Clos de la Roche 2010: 0–10% whole cluster, with 0% (fully destemmed) most likely. HIGH confidence that it was ≥90% destemmed; MED-HIGH that it was 100% destemmed. Reasoning: (1) Coates, writing in 2011 with direct access to Eric Rousseau, records that the domaine "normally" destems, with 2009's 15% whole cluster being a noted exception — and even that exception is described in the context of Chambertin/Bèze; (2) every source that assigns stems to Rousseau assigns them to the top Gevrey wines or to the post-2015 era; (3) Curtis (2024) states flatly that the Clos de la Roche is destemmed; (4) nothing places stems in the 2010 CDLR. The brief's "historically ~10–20% stems for Chambertin/Bèze only" is consistent with the 2005 "90% destemmed" figure and the 2009 "15%" experiment, but I could not find a source that says this in exactly those words for 2010 — say "a small proportion in the top Gevrey crus in some years, none documented for Clos de la Roche".

Rest of the regime (2010-era)

  • Harvest 2010: started 22 September (Galloni, WA #198, Dec 2011). HIGH Order of picking NOT FOUND; note Rousseau usually picks Lavaux St-Jacques last (Sotheby's).
  • Cold soak: ~4 days (Galloni, 2010); Berkeley & Stuart: 4–5 days. HIGH for 2010
  • Cuvaison: 15–21 days depending on the wine (Galloni, 2010); Nanson 2005: "2+ weeks", open stainless-steel vats, pump-over and pigeage, max 31°C; Coates: ~15 days, max 31°C; Morris 2022: 18–20 days, "without heating or other temperature control". HIGH
  • Yeasts: indigenous (Sotheby's). Chaptalisation 2010: NOT FOUND.
  • New oak for Clos de la Roche 2010: none. Jancis Robinson (Dec 2011, 17.5/20): "No new oak." Tanzer (IWC, Jan 2012): "Rousseau noted that he may begin using a bit of new oak for this cuvée in vintage 2012." Neal Martin (WA #210, Dec 2013): "For the first time a little new oak (10%) has been used on the 2012… new oak 'plugging' the gaps that might have been there without the new wood." Curtis 2024: "aged in used casks". HIGH
  • Context: 100% new for Chambertin and Clos de Bèze (and usually Clos St-Jacques, 70–100%); Nanson 2005 gave "30% new" for the other grands crus, but the CDLR-specific critic notes above show 0% new in 2010–2011 and 10% from 2012. Cooper for the new barrels: François Frères (Morris 2022; Sotheby's). Allier oak (Coates). HIGH for the top wines; the CDLR used-barrel provenance is presumably second-fill François Frères — NOT CONFIRMED
  • Élevage: 18–22 months (Sotheby's/Morris: "two winters"); Nanson 2005: 20–22 months, late bottling; malolactic hastened by warming the cellar (Nanson 2005); one racking (Coates 2011; Nanson 2019: "all wines racked following malo"). HIGH
  • Fining/filtration: light filtration (Nanson 2005); WineWiki: unfined, very light filtration only when needed. MED
  • Bottling of the 2010: NOT FOUND (regime implies spring–autumn 2012).
  • Alcohol on label: 13.5% (Bordeaux Index). HIGH
  • Eric Rousseau's stylistic creed (Coates 2011): "I make the wines to my style. They should not be too extracted or tannic. They'll keep well enough provided they are balanced." HIGH

B3. The vintage at Rousseau

  • Frédéric Robert (commercial director) to the Underground Wine Letter (barrel tasting, published Dec 2011): "the 2010s here are a bit like the 2001s, but with lower yields and more concentration"; crop "some 30% less than in 2009." Charles Rousseau (then 87) "reluctant to make a comparison". HIGH
  • Galloni (WA #198, Dec 2011): "Rousseau started picking on September 22. The fruit saw about four days of cold soak followed by a cuvaison that lasted anywhere from 15 to 21 days, depending on the wine… the 2010s are fabulous from top to bottom." HIGH
  • UWL barrel note on the 2010 Clos de la Roche: "Deep color, gorgeous perfume, heaps of pure, bright crisp fruit, freshness, structure, depth, long finish — Outstanding Potential" (one rung below Chambertin's "Exceptional"). HIGH

Reviews of Rousseau Clos de la Roche 2010

  • Antonio Galloni, Wine Advocate #199 (Feb 2012): 94–96+. "The Clos de la Roche is all about power, structure and length, qualities it has in spades. There is huge depth and serious extract to the fruit, with equally big tannins to provide support… persistent saline notes on the vibrant, finely chiseled finish." Anticipated maturity 2030–2050. (Some retail sites mis-attribute this note to Robert Parker; it is Galloni's.) HIGH
  • Allen Meadows, Burghound (Jan 2012): 92–94. "A well-layered and dense nose features notes of anise, earth, underbrush, violets and both red and blue pinot fruit aromas… very good volume to the large-scaled and dense yet supple flavors… This is impeccably well-balanced and should require 15 years or so to arrive at its peak." HIGH
  • Stephen Tanzer, IWC (Jan 2012): 91–94 (later listed as 92.5 on Vinous). "Complex nose mends raspberry, crushed rock, musky minerality, brown spices and a whiff of leather. Sappy, saline and pure, with rich, concentrated flavors of ripe black fruits and minerals… finishes very long, with dusty tannins." Plus the new-oak-from-2012 remark. HIGH
  • Jancis Robinson (Dec 2011): 17.5/20. "A little softer than some. Sweet fruit rather than massive Roche expression. Very neat and well behaved but in reserve. A rather wild note on the end. No new oak." HIGH
  • Jasper Morris MW, Inside Burgundy (tasted Nov 2025, published Aug 2026): 97, drink 2022–2032. "What a heavenly bouquet… A wealth of rich red fruit with only the barest of darker berry inflections." (Farr Vintners listing.) HIGH — This is the most recent professional look at the wine and says it is in its window now.
  • Charles Curtis MW, Decanter (Oct 2024): the 2010 "speaks equally of Rousseau's elegant touch and of the thrilling character of the 2010 vintage." HIGH
  • Winehog on the 2011 (cask, Oct 2012, 92–93): "quite different from the Gevrey wines… red and darker fruit… dark cherries and forest berries with hints of vanilla" — useful as a style reference. MED
  • CellarTracker community: blocked (403) — NOT FOUND.
  • Drinking window consensus: Meadows "15 years" (≈2027+), Galloni 2030–2050, Morris 2022–2032. Premox/bottle-variation reports: none found.

Market

  • Release-era US retail: $459.99 (K&L). Cult Wines/BI: sold out / price on request. Sotheby's May 2026 collector guide gives Rousseau CDLR (2014–16) auction estimates of $900–4,200/bt. A current 2010-specific price was NOT FOUND (wine-searcher and CellarTracker blocked). MED

B4. Stories, people, connections — Rousseau

  • Armand Rousseau (1884–1959): from a family of growers, coopers and merchants; inherited his first vines at 18 (1902) and gained the house and more vines on marriage in 1909; began as a wine broker/bulk seller; on the advice of Raymond Baudoin (founder of La Revue du Vin de France) began domaine-bottling for restaurateurs and private clients in the early 1930s — one of the first in Burgundy — and exported to the US after Repeal (1934–35) via Frank Schoonmaker's circle [the Schoonmaker link is common knowledge, NOT FOUND in fetched sources — MED]. Killed in a car accident in 1959. HIGH
  • Charles Rousseau (1923–2016): studied law and oenology at Dijon; joined 1945/46; took over at 36 in 1959 with ~6.5 ha and built it to 14+ ha (Clos de Bèze 1961 via the Nousbaum purchase, Clos St-Jacques 1954, Clos des Ruchottes 1977/78 from Thomas-Bassot, Chambertin additions 1968/1983, Clos de la Roche 1965/1975). Fluent in English and German, he built the export markets. On Chambertin vs Bèze: "Le Chambertin is male, well-built… Clos de Bèze is more complex, classier and delicate." On 1986: "You should have seen my vineyards in 1986. The ground was carpeted with rotten berries" — 50 harvesters, 12 days of triage. HIGH
  • Eric Rousseau (b. 1957): Lycée Agricole et Viticole de Mâcon-Davayé, oenology at Dijon; joined 1982; in charge of vinification since the early 1990s; introduced green harvest, leaf-thinning, mechanical soil work, stopped insecticides (2000). HIGH
  • Cyrielle Rousseau (b. 1988): degrees in geology, viticulture and wine trade (DTO) from Dijon; harvests in Oregon, Australia, New Zealand; involved from the 2012 vintage, full-time from 2014; now co-runs the domaine, does the tastings with "her brace of dogs" (Nanson, Nov 2025). Her nickname for the Clos de la Roche: "I call this Jean-Paul after the robots of JP Gautier" (to Nanson, 2022 — i.e., the armoured, corseted wine of the range). On 2021: "About half a harvest… It was like the seven plagues of Egypt." On Ruchottes: "It's my favourite too – it's where my roots are!" HIGH
  • Frédéric Robert: commercial director (with Charles's daughter Brigitte; another daughter Corinne joined the office in 1993 with a Dijon oenology diploma). Note: the brief says "Frédéric Rousseau" — the Frédéric at the domaine is Frédéric Robert, not a Rousseau. HIGH
  • Berkeley & Stuart's importer page names Eric's daughters as "Cirille and Elodie" — spelling errors/inconsistent with all other sources; ignore.
  • Rousseau's style vs Dujac's, in Curtis's words (2024): Rousseau's CDLR wines "seem lighter, with more purity of fruit but less complexity" than the stemmy/oaky neighbours — a line to deploy carefully at the table.
  • Importer: Frederick Wildman & Sons, New York — which was also Dujac's first US importer (from 1970, Nanson 2009). Shared-importer connection between the two wines. HIGH
  • Confrérie des Chevaliers du Tastevin: no specific Rousseau connection found in fetched sources — NOT FOUND.

PART C — DOMAINE DUJAC, CLOS DE LA ROCHE GRAND CRU 2010

C1. The producer's plot

  • Size: ~1.95 ha in 6 parcels. HIGH — Sorting Table (1.95), Nanson 2009 (1.95), en.wikipedia (1.95 ha in 6 parcels), Thatcher's "nearly two-hectare"
  • Location: Curtis (Decanter 2024): "0.5 ha in lieu-dit Clos de la Roche, 0.5 ha in Monts Luisants, 0.59 ha in Les Chabiots and small holdings in several other sub-sections." → i.e. spread across the original clos, the upper (Monts Luisants) and Chabiots sectors, plus fragments — a blend of the whole slope. MED-HIGH
  • Acquisition: the core came with the Domaine Marcel Graillet purchase (late 1967, first vintage 1968) — Graillet's 4.5 ha included Clos de la Roche, Clos St-Denis, Bonnes Mares, Echézeaux and Morey villages (Nanson 2009; Sorting Table: "initial purchase in 1968; additional parcels added in 1977 and 1990"). MED-HIGH
  • Vine age: Flatiron (Nov 2024, Jeremy speaking): CDLR "vines over 50 years old" (→ planted ≤1974, many older from the Graillet era). Planting dates by parcel NOT FOUND. Massale selection from Chambolle Les Gruenchers historically used as mother vineyard (Nanson 2009). MED
  • Farming: lutte raisonnée from 1987; organics from 2001 (one-third), 100% by 2008; certification applied for 2008, certified organic 2011/2012 (dujac.com: "certified organic since 2011"; Jeremy to Wine-Searcher 2013; WoFW/Diana: organic certification 2012); biodynamic practices introduced 2002/2003, not certified (dujac.com; WoFW). Ploughed, no herbicides; Cordon de Royat / Guyot-Poussard. Jacques (2009, to Nanson): "We are biodynamic, but I try not to make too much of it – we will certainly never be putting our organic status on the bottles." Diana (WoFW, 2020s): "we invariably find the biodynamic one always has a bit more acidity analytically speaking, and this kind of linear tension every year" — Opening a Bottle (2019) notes the trial parcel is Clos de la Roche ("biodynamic Clos de la Roche exhibits a bit more acidity (measurably) and energy (intuitively)"). So in 2010 the CDLR was organically farmed (certified from the 2011 vintage), with a biodynamic sub-parcel. HIGH
  • Yield target: ~6–7 bunches per vine, ~35 hl/ha (Nanson 2009, 2014). HIGH

C2. Winemaking for this cuvée — WHOLE CLUSTER, every figure found

Statement Refers to Made in Source
"100% stems, i.e. whole grape-bunches, low-temperature fermentations and a significant percentage of new Allier French oak" — the Jacques Seysses regime 1968–c.1999 2009 Nanson, Burgundy-Report profile
"Originally 100% whole-cluster with 100% new oak (Jacques era); post-1999 some destemming" pre/post 1999 — en.wikipedia
"Dujac long employed whole bunches and stems with 100% new oak until 1999" to 1999 2019 Quill & Pad
2001: "plenty of de-stemming"; 2002: "only 10–20% destemmed"; 2004: variable, more destemming in hailed vineyards ("You had to be careful not to over-extract vegetal elements"); 2006: "50 to 100% whole clusters" 2001–2006 2009 Nanson profile
"Jeremy Seysses employed 80–90% stem inclusion for most wines (70% for Charmes and Combottes; 100% for Chambertin and RSV)… potential alcohol 12–12.5%… light chaptalization… poor flowering and wet summer produced loose bunches with notable shot berries; yields down 30–50%" 2010 Dec 2011 Antonio Galloni, Wine Advocate #198 (via Cult Wines listings for CDLR and Chambertin 2010)
"most cuvees are fermented with over 80% whole cluster every year at Dujac" general SommChat, 16 May (c. 2012) Jeremy Seysses, Keeper Collection
2012 Clos St-Denis: "approximately 80% stems… we'll wait to see if that was a good decision!" 2012 June 2014 Nanson visit
2013: Morey 70%, Chambolle 65%, Gevrey 80% (and 25% for one Gevrey in another vintage) 2013 2014–15 Vinous producer page (Tanzer)
"We used a little more stems and just a little less oak" 2015 Nov 2016 Jeremy to Nanson
"65% and 100% whole cluster depending on the cuvée"; "We have the feeling that we get greater complexity and silkier tannins with whole cluster fermentation… In high acid vintages, it helps round things out, and in high ripeness vintages, it brings a freshness"; "I like destemming a little more for the Gevrey vineyards"; "I usually like putting the destemmed fruit at the bottom and the whole cluster on top, so that it really stays whole… The cap is far more aerated… letting some heat escape" general 2015 Jeremy Seysses to Jamie Goode, wineanorak
80–90% whole clusters, domaine and négoce 2016 2017 Tanzer/Vinous (Sorting Table PDF)
90% (2018); 85–90% (2019); ~20% destemmed across range (2021) 2018–2021 Vinous producer page
70–100% (2019); "Gevrey is not so spicy in character and so I feel the whole-cluster spiciness can come across a little gimmicky" 2019 2019 Opening a Bottle
"Average is about 85% whole cluster"; Echézeaux 100% 2022 2023 Fine Wine Review
~75% average; Jeremy regrets "not doing a bit more whole cluster in 2023"; maceration now 10–12 days 2023 2024 Fine Wine Review
100% for all wines except Charmes (20% destemmed) 2024 Nov 2024 Flatiron (Jeremy) ; Wine Society: 60–100%
Jacques's habitual question at the sorting table: "Are you sure you don't want to destem that bunch?" anecdote 2024 Flatiron

Confidence range for Dujac Clos de la Roche 2010: 80–90% whole cluster. HIGH confidence. Reasoning: Galloni's Dec 2011 producer note is a direct report of Jeremy Seysses's 2010 regime, and it names the exceptions (Charmes and Combottes ~70%; Chambertin and RSV 100%); Clos de la Roche is not among the exceptions, so it sits in the 80–90% band. This is corroborated by Jeremy's contemporaneous "over 80% every year" (SommChat) and by his stated logic (Gevrey sites get more destemming; Morey grands crus with older vines stay whole). If pressed for a single number, ~85% is the defensible midpoint; do not claim 100%.

Why not 100% in 2010? 2010 bunches were loose with shot berries (millerandage) — good for stem-inclusion in principle (small berries, high skin ratio) — but potential alcohols were low (12–12.5%) and the wines were chaptalised; Jeremy's "high acid vintages, it helps round things out" logic argues for high but not total inclusion. MED — interpretive

Rest of the 2010 regime

  • Harvest: Dujac finished picking on 30 September 2010 (Nanson harvest diary, "harvest – 30th september"); Jeremy "only ended up with 25 hl/ha for his villages Morey, though no quality complaints"; Monts Luisants white still to pick that Friday (1 Oct). Start date NOT FOUND (Côte de Nuits reds generally began 24–28 Sept; rain 24–27 Sept). HIGH
  • Yields: crop down ~30% vs 2009 (Jacques to UWL); Galloni: down 30–50% (white down 90%). CDLR 2010: 575 cases (UWL). HIGH
  • Cold soak: not part of the Dujac method as such — "low-temperature fermentations" (Nanson 2009); no formal cold soak figure found. Fermentation ~2 weeks, indigenous yeasts, light punch-downs early then pump-overs (dujac.com); "no recipe" daily tasting of 23 tanks by Jacques, Jeremy and Alec (Opening a Bottle 2019); stainless-steel fermenters (Thatcher's — retailer, MED). Chaptalisation 2010: light (Galloni). HIGH on chaptalisation
  • New oak 2010: ~90–100% for the grands crus MED. Jacques used 100% new for GCs (Tanzer 2017); Tanzer: "Previously, the estate used 80–85%" before Jeremy's current ≤70%; Winehog (Nov 2014 visit): grands crus reduced "from 100% to 90%" in 2013; Quill & Pad 2019: GC 100%, 1er 60–80%, village 40%; Jeannie Cho Lee: GC 100%, 1er ~50%; Fine Wine Review 2024: GC 75%; Thatcher's retailer sheet: 65–80% for the 2010 CDLR (LOW). Best estimate for 2010 CDLR: 90–100% new, lightly toasted, Allier, wood bought two years ahead and dried 30 months (Nanson 2009; dujac.com). Cooper: NOT FOUND for 2010 (Dujac is commonly said to buy from François Frères and others — unsourced here).
  • Élevage: 12–16 months (Nanson 2009) / 14–18 months (dujac.com); racked once after malolactic; bottled unfiltered every year, reds usually unfined; bottling typically Feb–March (Wikipedia) → 2010s bottled c. Feb–Mar 2012. HIGH on principle; 2010 bottling date NOT FOUND
  • Reduction: "Since we have been organic in the vineyards we seem to have more reduction in the wines – actually quite a lot more reduction" (Jeremy to Nanson, 2014) — relevant if the 2010 shows a reductive edge on opening; decant. HIGH
  • Alcohol on label: 13.5% (BBR; Bordeaux Index). HIGH

C3. The vintage at Dujac

  • Jacques Seysses to UWL (2011): the 2010s are "more difficult to match" than the 2009s; the wines have "intensity with very pure fruit. The wines in 2009 and 2010 are quite different, but each vintage has its merits." HIGH
  • Galloni's 2010 producer note (above): poor flowering, wet summer, loose bunches, shot berries; 12–12.5% potential; light chaptalisation; yields −30 to −50%. HIGH
  • Nanson harvest diary (Sept–Oct 2010): rain 24–25 Sept interrupted picking; 27 Sept rain day (Gevrey and Vosne postponed); cold nights ≤5°C; Gevrey started 28 Sept, Chambertin/Charmes 1–2 Oct; triage "still only in the region of 6–7%" — very clean fruit. HIGH
  • General 2010 narrative (winter freeze of 20 Dec 2009 killing vines in low spots; cold, uneven June flowering → coulure/millerandage; cool summer; warm dry mid-September rescuing ripeness; late-September harvest of small crops with high acidity and thick skins): the flowering/millerandage/late-harvest parts are corroborated by Galloni, Nanson and the Cult Wines Rousseau text ("delayed until late September… preserving the quintessential acidic spine"); the December 2009 freeze claim was NOT independently sourced in this research — mark MED and attribute to general vintage reports if used.
  • UWL barrel note on the 2010 CDLR: "575 cases… deep color and a gorgeous perfume with floral tinged cherry fruit and a touch of spice. It is very pure with lots of fruit and flavor yet is elegant and refined — Outstanding Plus Potential" (Clos St-Denis, Bonnes Mares, Chambertin, RSV got "Exceptional"). HIGH

Reviews of Dujac Clos de la Roche 2010

  • Antonio Galloni, Wine Advocate #198 (22 Dec 2011): 93–95, drink 2020–2035. "The 2010 Clos de la Roche wafts from the glass with sweet scents of tobacco, crushed flowers and spices… decidedly feminine… weightless energy being held back." HIGH
  • Stephen Tanzer, IWC → Vinous (1 Mar 2013): 95.5 (shown as 95 on some sites). "Good medium red. Pungent aromas and flavors of candied raspberry, flowers, stone and sandalwood show a liqueur-like ripeness. Extremely intense and packed with tangy fruit; in fact, wonderfully expressive and extroverted today for such a dense, even brawny wine. The superb rising finish features big but ripe tannins and great fruit persistence. Has all the elements to make a great and long-lived bottle." HIGH
  • Antonio Galloni, Vinous (1 Sept 2014): 96.5. "The 2010 Clos de La Roche from Dujac is an infant, but it is also fabulous. All of the signatures are there; soaring aromatics, finely sculpted fruit and vibrant, pulsating tannins." HIGH
  • Burghound: 96 per Thatcher's Wine; the issue/date and text were not retrievable — MED. (Meadows' barrel range from Jan 2012 NOT FOUND.)
  • Berry Bros & Rudd: "Ready – youthful", drink 2022–2055. MED
  • Bill Nanson, Mainz 2010 dinner (Nov 2013) tasted Dujac's 2010 Malconsorts, Echézeaux ("a whole-cluster dimension and a little oak too. Wide, round and mineral – some salinity") and Charmes — not the CDLR. HIGH
  • CellarTracker: 95 community average per Thatcher's; the CT page itself was blocked (403). LOW
  • Drinking window: Galloni 2020–2035; BBR 2022–2055; Jeremy Seysses (SommChat): "most grand crus have 20 years in them given good cellaring conditions." Claude Kolm's observation (Burgundy-Report comments, 2013) that Jacques-era Dujac "hardly ever seem[s] to go into a closed phase" is a useful line for a 16-year-old bottle. Premox/bottle variation: nothing reported.

Market

  • Bordeaux Index (2026): £6,915 per 6 IB (≈£1,150/bt), 3-pack £4,400. Thatcher's (US): $1,350. Cult Wines US: $16,660/12 (≈$1,390). HIGH as listed asks
  • The price gap vs Rousseau's CDLR 2010 (~$460 at release, now probably $1,000–1,500 on the secondary market — NOT VERIFIED) is worth noting: the Dujac has become the reference Clos de la Roche in the market alongside Ponsot and Leroy.

C4. Stories, people, connections — Dujac

  • Jacques Seysses: born 1941 [date unsourced here]; son of Louis Seysses, "owner and president of a biscuit factory" (Nanson 2009) — the Belin biscuit company (the brief's "Belin biscuits" is consistent with Wikipedia's "son of a biscuit manufacturer" and Quill & Pad's "biscuit-manufacturing family"; the name Belin was NOT explicitly confirmed in fetched sources — MED). Worked as a banker in New York and Paris; did two harvests (1966–67) with Gérard Potel at Domaine de la Pousse d'Or in Volnay; bought Domaine Marcel Graillet (4.5 ha, Morey) in late 1967 with his father's backing and renamed it Dujac = "du Jacques" (Curtis 2024 credits the founding to "Jacques Seysses and his father, Louis Seysses in 1968"). First vintage 1968 — "a disaster – as one of the worst vintages of the decade" (Jeannie Cho Lee); 1969 was the real launch; sold to négociants at first, then to restaurants through his father's contacts; Frederick Wildman began importing to the US in 1970; Gault & Millau "discovered" the wines in 1974. HIGH
  • Famous line (to Nanson 2009): "It's just about good winemaking. In the end it's not about what we do, it's about the soils and plots of Burgundy. Great wine was made long before science and technology were introduced to winemaking."
  • On climate (Levi Dalton podcast, 2019): "Global Warming has been a positive for Burgundy" — with the caveat about the brutal 1970s winters. Also: in the 1960s a domaine owner "made minimal living and bright family members were encouraged to pursue other careers." Jacques went to school with Christophe Roumier's father? — No: Vinography says he "attended school with future Burgundy star Christophe Roumier" — chronologically implausible (Roumier b. 1958); treat as a transcription error, NOT RELIABLE.
  • Connections: close friend of Aubert de Villaine (Vinography); the Australian Farr family link (Gary Farr of Bannockburn/By Farr worked at Dujac; Quill & Pad mentions the Farr connection) MED; the domaine "regularly employs international interns" (Prince of Pinot) — the Dujac harvest-intern network is legendary (Ted Lemon, Tony Soter, Gary Farr, etc. — the names beyond Farr are NOT SOURCED here).
  • Hospitalised during the 2024 harvest (Flatiron, Nov 2024): Jeremy said "it was hard not having his father there questioning his every decision" — the stems question ("Are you sure you don't want to destem that bunch?") was the recurring one.
  • Rosalind Seysses: American; married Jacques and joined the domaine in 1973 (dujac.com). HIGH
  • Jeremy Seysses: grew up in Morey, spent time in California, educated in England (Oxford — unsourced here) and Dijon; joined 1998; runs vinification; 2024 was his 31st harvest; also runs the négoce Dujac Fils & Père (from 2000). Quotes: "Winemakers' philosophical ideals must not take precedence over making a good product" and "If a wine is put in the bottle good, but then shipped five times and the consumer has a bad experience, who cares that it was ever good?" (TFWSA); defends SO2 — wines "travel too much" (Wine-Searcher 2013). Wine-Searcher Q&A 2013: "I don't think organic is a perfect system, but I do think it is a good system and better than many of the alternatives." HIGH
  • Diana Snowden Seysses: from Napa (Snowden Vineyards, family property since 1955; she is winemaker there, third generation); UC Davis enology; moved to France 2001, full-time from 2003; cellar master/oenologist at Dujac; led the biodynamic trials (2002) and the climate/sustainability agenda ("We cannot make wines like that anymore"; "If the two harvests coincide, I drop Burgundy"). HIGH
  • Alec Seysses: joined 2003; commerce/administration; hosts many trade visits (Winehog 2014). His line on climate: "We are picking a degree more alcohol than when my father started, which we know from our own records" (Vinous). HIGH
  • Christophe Morin, vineyard manager 1986–2001 (died in a motorcycle accident), succeeded by Lilian Robin (Nanson 2009). HIGH
  • 2005: Dujac (with de Montille) bought part of the Thomas-Moillard vineyards, adding ~3 ha incl. Chambertin, RSV, Malconsorts, Beaux Monts — hence the Chambertin/RSV 100%-stem cuvées in Galloni's 2010 note. HIGH
  • Dujac's stems philosophy in one sentence (Sorting Table): Jacques believed mature stems reduced hard tannins and avoided bruising the grapes in the destemmer; Wine Society (2024): whole bunch "moderates extraction and extracts aromatic compounds which transform into floral aromas with bottle age."
  • The "Dujac vs Rousseau stems debate" as framed in the brief: I found no sourced head-to-head exchange between the two houses. What is sourced: (a) Jacques Seysses's 100%-stem regime was the counter-model to Gevrey's destemmed tradition (Rousseau, and DRC's stems aside); (b) Levi Dalton's whole-cluster episode (#416, 2017) with Jeremy is cited by Vinography as showing "certain totems of the popular discussion are wrong" — Dujac and Méo-Camuzet as examples of experimentation rather than dogma; (c) Rousseau moved from ~90% destemmed (2005) to a documented 15% whole-cluster experiment in 2009 and ~20% across the range in 2015 — i.e. the "destemmed" house has drifted toward the Dujac side since our vintage, while Dujac drifted from 100% to 80–90%. 2010 is close to the maximum stylistic distance between the two — that is the honest framing. HIGH on the individual facts; the "debate" itself NOT FOUND
  • Shared threads between the two wines: same climat, same vintage, both importers historically Frederick Wildman (Dujac from 1970; Rousseau still); both domaines had the founder's grandchild take charge of the cellar (Jeremy 1998 / Cyrielle 2014); both use François Frères-type Allier oak at the top (Rousseau confirmed; Dujac cooper unconfirmed); both hold Chambertin and Charmes-Chambertin as well. Pousse d'Or's Gérard Potel (Jacques's mentor) — no Rousseau link found.
  • Confrérie des Chevaliers du Tastevin: no specific membership or Tastevinage reference for either domaine was found in fetched sources — NOT FOUND. (Both are Côte de Nuits grandees and Clos de Vougeot is 10 minutes away; do not assert membership.)

PART D — TASTING FRAMEWORK FOR THE PAIR (what to say, what to look for)

  1. Aromatics: Dujac's 80–90% stems + ~90–100% new oak in 2010 → expect the "whole-cluster dimension" Nanson finds in the Echézeaux 2010: sandalwood, dried flowers, tea, sweet spice; Tanzer's "candied raspberry, flowers, stone and sandalwood". Rousseau destemmed, zero new oak → Meadows' "anise, earth, underbrush, violets", Tanzer's "crushed rock, musky minerality, brown spices, leather"; Jancis's "a rather wild note on the end."
  2. Structure: Galloni called the Rousseau "all about power, structure and length… huge depth and serious extract… big tannins" and the Dujac "decidedly feminine… weightless energy". Counter-intuitive: the destemmed wine is the more tannic/muscular one in 2010 (thick-skinned, small-berry fruit, 15–21-day cuvaison, no new-oak polish), the stemmed wine the more perfumed and silky (stems aerate the cap, moderate extraction). Use that reversal.
  3. Colour: Rousseau 2012 already "quite a pale vermilion" in 2025 per Morris — expect the Rousseau 2010 to look lighter than the Dujac.
  4. Age: Morris (Nov 2025) has the Rousseau 2010 at 97 and drinking now (2022–2032); Galloni wanted 2030 for it. Dujac: Galloni 2020–2035, BBR to 2055. Both 16 years old on 26 Oct 2026 — mid-window. Decant the Dujac for organic-era reduction.
  5. The rock: both wines contain ~0.5 ha of the original 4.57-ha clos; the rest is Chabiots/Monts Luisants (Dujac, upper/mid) vs Fremières (Rousseau, lower). If someone asks "which is more 'Clos de la Roche'?" — Ponsot would say the original clos gives "substance" and Monts Luisants "freshness"; Rousseau's Fremières fruit sits lower and warmer.
