C3 — DRC & Liger-Belair Échezeaux 2014 10,139 words

Échezeaux Grand Cru 2014 — Domaine de la Romanée-Conti vs. Domaine du Comte Liger-Belair

Dinner theme: "The Importance of Being Stemmed." This pair is the theme in a single flight: two Échezeaux from the same commune, the same vintage, harvested nine days apart, from parcels roughly 400 m from each other — one (DRC) fermented with ~70–75 % whole clusters, the other (Liger-Belair) from a "Jayer-iste" house that historically destemmed 100 % and was only just beginning cautious whole-cluster experiments in its crus.

Headline whole-cluster verdict

Wine Whole cluster in 2014 Confidence Key sources
DRC Échezeaux 2014 ~70–75 % (i.e. "normally about 25–30 % destemming") HIGH Bertrand de Villaine to Bill Nanson, 14 Nov 2015 (tasting the 2014s); Jeannie Cho Lee on RC 2014 "about 70 % whole bunches"; 2013 = 70–80 % by parcel (Nanson, Nov 2014); 2017 = 75 % all crus except RC 100 % (Nanson, Dec 2018)
Liger-Belair Échezeaux 2014 0–15 %, most probably fully or nearly fully destemmed MEDIUM Domaine website ("destemmed in whole or in part, depending on the cuvées and vintages"); Tanzer/Vinous Nov 2016: 2015 crus "about 20 % whole clusters… a bit more than usual"; Nanson 2014–2016: Chaumes 15–20 %, Suchots 15 %, Petits Monts "never", villages all destemmed; William Kelley 2017: "He certainly used to destem 100 %"

WINE A — Domaine de la Romanée-Conti, Échezeaux Grand Cru 2014

1. The vineyard / climat: Échezeaux

Location, size, exposure, altitude

  • Commune: Flagey-Échezeaux, the "plain" village between Vougeot and Vosne-Romanée whose vineyards sit up on the slope and whose two grands crus (Échezeaux and Grands Échezeaux) are the only wines to carry the commune's name — the village-level and premier-cru wines of Flagey are sold as Vosne-Romanée. HIGH — BIVB
  • Official BIVB figures: Échezeaux 36.27 ha in production (2022), Grands Échezeaux 9.06 ha; 5-year average yield (2017–21) 1,177 hl ≈ 156,541 bottles for Échezeaux. HIGH — bourgogne-wines.com Other published totals: 34.79 ha (Wikipedia, 2008 production data), 35.77–37.69 ha (Jefford/Decanter), ~37 ha (Winehog), 38.14 ha (Charles Curtis MW, Decanter 2025, sum of lieux-dits). The spread reflects cadastral vs. planted area. HIGH that the answer is "36–38 ha"
  • AOC Grand Cru created 31 July 1937 (same decree as Grands Échezeaux). HIGH — BIVB; Liger-Belair site says "recognized as Grand Cru since July 1937"
  • Exposure east; altitude 230 m to a little over 300 m, gradient about 13 % at mid-slope (BIVB). Jockovino/Coates-derived description: "faces east from an elevation that reaches 300 meters at the top and 260 meters down below. The slope varies from 10 % at the base to 13 % at the top." HIGH
  • Geology (BIVB): "The Echezeaux Climats have more diverse soils (largely bajocien marls with pebbly overlay)… Up-slope, the soil is deep (70–80 cm). Gravels, red alluvium, yellowish marl, etc., make up quite a complex mosaic." Jefford (Decanter) and Curtis (Decanter 2022) both emphasise that the vineyard sits at the mouth of the Combe d'Orveau, a dry valley through the escarpment whose ancient alluvial fan gives quick-draining soils, gentler slopes, roots farther from bedrock, and a channel for cold air from the Hautes-Côtes — hence a cooler, later-ripening, more "complex and intriguing" site than Vosne's core grands crus. HIGH

Name etymology

  • BIVB: the name "derives from chesaux, a word of Gallo-Roman origin meaning a group of dwellings, presumably referring to an ancient hamlet." Jefford: "Echézeaux is derived from casa — cottage or hut in classical Latin." Jockovino/Coates: from Latin casalis, a rural house. All agree: "little houses/huts." HIGH
  • History: first mention in records 1287; acquired by the Abbot of Cîteaux 1353 (Jockovino/Coates). BIVB: like Clos de Vougeot, "founded by the monks of the abbey of Cîteaux and date from the 12th and 13th centuries." The Cistercians farmed this as land outside the Clos de Vougeot wall. HIGH

Classification history and the "dilution" controversy

  • Historically, "Échezeaux" proper was the small lieu-dit Échezeaux du Dessus (3.55–4.48 ha depending on source). A 1925 court case (Étienne Camuzet vs. local families including the Gros and Mongeard families — Curtis; Jefford describes it as Camuzet v. Mongeard-Mugneret) established that grapes from the surrounding lieux-dits could also be sold as "Échezeaux"; the 1937 AOC then swept all of today's lieux-dits into the grand cru. In 1984 a judge added 1.27 ha of Les Beaux Monts Bas on the basis of historical sales records. HIGH — Curtis, Decanter 13 Jun 2022 Winehog summarises: "expanded from 3.5 ha in 1937" to ~37 ha. MED on the "3.5 ha" figure
  • Consequence: Échezeaux "has a reputation for being somewhat variable in quality" (Wikipedia) — ~80 owners, most of whom blend across lieux-dits and decline to say where their vines are (Winehog). Steen Öhman (Winehog, 2011/2017) is blunt: "Echézeaux vineyard cannot merit an extraordinary wine; it lacks the complexity and finesse of grand crus like La Romanée, Romanée Conti, Richebourg and Romanée Saint-Vivant" and "A good Echézeaux should at least have some mineral balance from lighter soil, otherwise it's just a big Suchots without freshness and complexity." Charles Curtis's line for the dinner: "Flagey is doing a lot of the work, and Vosne is getting all the credit." HIGH as quotes

The 11 lieux-dits (sizes from Winehog 2017 / Curtis 2022 / Curtis 2025 — figures vary by source)

Lieu-dit Winehog (ha) Curtis 2022 / 2025 (ha) Character / notes
Échezeaux du Dessus 3.55 3.55 / 4.48 The historic core, mid-slope; "deep and chalky soil" (Winehog); Jefford: higher quality
Les Poulaillères 5.21 — / 5.24 "Heart of the vineyard"; DRC owns ~90 % (Winehog); "almost exclusively DRC property" (Curtis 2025); rocky alluvial soil, lies just above Grands Échezeaux
Les Champs Traversins 3.58 3.59 / 3.58 Hillside, shallow stony soil; "vines planted across the others" (Liger-Belair); nicknamed "Little Cîteaux" (Winehog)
Les Rouges du Bas 3.99 4 / 3.09 Upper slope, highest point of the cru; thin marl; steep
En Orveaux (GC part) 5.04 5 / 8.03 North-facing, coldest, extends into the Combe d'Orveau; late-ripening
Les Loächausses 2.48 2.49 / 2.22 Effectively a Gros-family monopole
Les Treux 4.89 ~5 / 4.76 Low, adjacent to Grands Échezeaux; damp, heavy soil (Jefford)
Les Cruots ou Vignes Blanches 3.28 — / 3.16 Southern sector adjoining Les Suchots; stony ("cruots") + former white-vine site; Henri Jayer called it "the best terroir of the appellation" (Curtis 2025)
Clos Saint-Denis 1.80 1.8 / 1.66 Southern limit along Suchots; low, heavier
Les Quartiers de Nuits (GC part) 1.12 1.13 / 1.15 Eastern edge against the Clos de Vougeot wall
Les Beaux Monts Bas (GC part) 1.25 1.27 / 0.77 Added 1984
  • Quality hierarchy (Winehog, 2011, updated 2017): "The plots placed above Grands Echézeaux and towards Vosne Romanée yield the highest quality. Les Poulaillères, Les Cruots or Vignes Blanches could very well be the premier spots, followed by Les Champs-Traversins, Echézeaux du dessus." Lower tier: Quartiers de Nuits, Rouges du Bas, Beaux Monts Bas, En Orveaux. Jefford's grouping: stony (Cruots, Vignes Blanches); damp/heavy (Treux, Quartiers de Nuits, Clos St-Denis); higher quality (Poulaillères, En Orveaux, Échezeaux du Dessus). Curtis 2025: the three southern lieux-dits "were regarded in the 19th century… as a step lower in quality." MED — critics disagree on En Orveaux
  • Winehog on the two producers at this dinner: "The best Echézeauxs were made by producers Liger-Belair and Henri Jayer, both partly from 'Les Cruots or Vignes Blanches'. Domaine de la Romanée Conti Echézeaux, mainly from 'Les Poulaillères', is also very fine but lacks complexity and intensity." HIGH as an opinion, dated 2011/2017

Neighbours and how Échezeaux differs

  • North: Chambolle-Musigny (Musigny/Combe d'Orveau). East: Grands Échezeaux and the Clos de Vougeot wall. South/west: Vosne-Romanée premiers crus (Les Suchots, Les Beaux Monts, Les Brûlées, Les Rouges). Grands Échezeaux (BIVB): homogeneous, gradient 3–4 %, clay-limestone over Bajocian limestone, 250 m — the calm, structured wine; Échezeaux is the diverse, higher, cooler, gravel-and-marl mosaic that produces (per BIVB) wines of "heightened attack… supple tannins," "rose, violet and fresh cherry" when young, "animal, spice, underbrush, prune" with age. HIGH

Ownership map (roughly 80 owners; hectares only where sourced)

  • DRC 4.67 ha — the largest owner (Wikipedia/Wilson Daniels/C&B "a seventh of Échezeaux" [in fact 4.67/36.27 ≈ 12.9 %, nearer an eighth]); ~90 % of Poulaillères plus a share (~10 %, Winehog) of Clos Saint-Denis. HIGH on total; MED on breakdown
  • Comte Liger-Belair 0.6157 ha (Cruots 0.3299 / Champs Traversins 0.2546 / Clos St-Denis 0.0312) HIGH — domaine site; plus a plot of Échezeaux du Dessus from 2020 (Winehog) and additional Échezeaux from Nathalie Pacareau (ex-Lamarche) from the 2022 harvest (Winehog; Curtis).
  • Others by lieu-dit (Winehog 2017; sizes NOT FOUND unless noted): En Orveaux — Domaine d'Eugénie (ex-René Engel), Bouchard Père & Fils, Dominique Mugneret, Joseph Drouhin, Faiveley, Dominique Laurent, Christian Clerget. Champs Traversins — Liger-Belair, Jacques Prieur, A.-F. Gros, Lamarche, Dujac, Louis Jadot, Clos Frantin (Bichot). Poulaillères — DRC (~90 %), Frédéric Magnien, Jean-Marc Millot, Capitain-Gagnerot, Richard Manière (0.25 ha "right next to DRC's large parcel," per a WineBerserkers contributor; Jacques Cacheux also said to have some). Rouges du Bas — Méo-Camuzet, Mongeard-Mugneret, Jadot, Mugneret-Gibourg. Échezeaux du Dessus — Jayer-Gilles, Cécile Tremblay, Millot, Mongeard-Mugneret, (ex-)Alex Gambal. Loächausses — Gros Frère et Sœur, Anne Gros. Treux — Georges/Lucien Jayer, Emmanuel Rouget, Magnien, Mongeard-Mugneret, Régis Forey, Jean Tardy. Cruots/Vignes Blanches — Henri Jayer/Rouget, Liger-Belair, Jean Grivot, Lamarche, Georges Noëllat. Clos St-Denis — DRC, Lamarche, Millot, Forey. MED — Winehog's list, 2017

2. DRC's plot within Échezeaux

  • Size: 4.67 ha (Wikipedia; Wilson Daniels tech page "Surface area: 4.67 hectares"). HIGH
  • Location: overwhelmingly Les Poulaillères, the block lying immediately above (west of) Grands Échezeaux in the "heart" of the cru. Corney & Barrow's 2014 release brochure (Adam Brett-Smith, Feb 2017): "the Domaine's Échézeaux has never been in a more beautiful condition with 90 % of the vineyard comprising 'Les Poulaillères' lying just above Grands Échézeaux." Winehog: DRC ~90 % of Poulaillères (5.21 ha ⇒ ~4.7 ha, which squares with the 4.67 ha total) plus ~10 % of Clos Saint-Denis. HIGH on Poulaillères dominance; the brief's "Poulaillères, Champs Traversins, Clos St Denis, Cruots" breakdown: Champs Traversins and Cruots were <strong>NOT FOUND</strong> in any source consulted — Winehog lists DRC only in Poulaillères and Clos St-Denis.
  • Vine age: Corney & Barrow 2014 vineyard table — average age 30 years for Échezeaux in 2014 (vs. 35 GE, 40 RSV, 45 Richebourg/La Tâche, 56 RC); Wikipedia 32 years. This is the youngest red-wine vineyard in the domaine — consistent with the replanting Nanson recorded in 2005 ("The resurgence of this bottling continues") and the "recently replanting part of Echezeaux holdings" noted by Curtis (2022). Precise planting dates NOT FOUND. HIGH on average age
  • Ownership structure: In 1988, to finance the outright purchase of the Marey-Monge share of Romanée-Saint-Vivant (leased since 1966), "the Domaine de la Romanée-Conti sold their vines in Echézeaux and a smaller amount in Grands Echézeaux, though retained the use of the land on a renewable 30 year lease" (Nanson, 2005 profile); Wikipedia: "financed by the sale and leaseback of the estate's holdings in Échezeaux and some in Grands Échezeaux." So DRC's Échezeaux is farmed under a long lease/leaseback rather than owned outright — a point to verify with the domaine before repeating at table; whether the 2018 renewal changed anything was NOT FOUND. MED
  • Plant material: DRC restocks from its own massal palette: "some 50–60 genetically differentiated vines were identified in Romanée-Conti's 1.8 hectares, and today, the clones of this palette is still used to restock the domaine's vineyards"; three routes — annual replacement of dead vines, uprooting whole sections, and "surgreffage" grafting of in-house selections onto adult vines (Nanson 2005). Bertrand de Villaine (Nov 2014): experiments with 14,000 vines/ha "inconclusive… they would replant at the standard 10K." HIGH
  • Farming: organic through the 1980s–90s; biodynamic trials on ~5–8 ha (La Tâche, Grands Échezeaux) then fully biodynamic from 2007/2008 ("DRC has been fully biodynamic since 2008 after experiments on 8 hectares proved that this made the wines more expressive" — Bernard Noblet to a visitor, Jan 2010; C&B 2015 brochure refers to copper "permitted under biodynamic regulations"). Horses reintroduced first in Romanée-Conti and Le Montrachet (C&B). Compost from crushed vine roots, grape skins and fermentation residues. Yields "an average of 25 hl/ha… it takes three vines to produce one bottle" (C&B). Chef de culture: Nicolas Jacob. HIGH

3. Winemaking for the Échezeaux — and in 2014

WHOLE CLUSTER — every figure found, with source and date

Vintage referred to Figure Who / where / when said
General (1990s) "whole cluster" (contrasted with Jayer's "complete destemming") Herwig Janssen, WineBerserkers, 2013, comparing 1990 Échezeaux
2008 (general policy) "The stems are systematically included. M. Noblet refers to them as the 'skeleton' of the grape bunches and says that… these stems must be treated very gently to release just the right amount of desired components." Bernard Noblet, cellar visit reported by Alex Rychlewski, WineBerserkers, 17 Jan 2010
2013 "We used between 70 and 80 % whole clusters during the fermentation, depending on the parcel." Bertrand de Villaine to Bill Nanson, 18 Nov 2014
2014 (the wine in question) "Most people assume that the domaine only uses whole cluster fermentations, but normally there's about 25–30 % destemming, the whole clusters at the bottom of the tank but with a net to catch them just above the base of the tank to help drainage a little." ⇒ ~70–75 % whole cluster Bertrand de Villaine to Bill Nanson, 14 Nov 2015, while tasting the 2014s; the destemmer "sitting on top of a vat – rather than the end of a triage table"
2014 Romanée-Conti "In this vintage about 70 % whole bunches were included" Jeannie Cho Lee MW, tasting note on RC 2014 (Tastingbook)
2015 "For the first time on many years, the domaine was almost 100 % whole-cluster in 2015"; C&B: "vinifications… effected with whole clusters. Fermentations were powerful, rich and extremely long (between 21 and 23 days)"; Nanson Dec 2016: "There was no destemming in 2015" Bertrand de Villaine to Nanson, Nov 2015; C&B 2015 brochure (Jan 2018)
2017 "About 75 % whole-clusters were used in the fermentation for all wines except the Romanée-Conti, which was 100 %." Nanson, 6 Dec 2018 visit
2020 "It wasn't a vintage for using lots of stems as they were not in great shape." Bertrand de Villaine to Nanson, Nov 2021
2021/2022 Strong stem aromatics noted; "Of all the domaines that used whole clusters in 2022, the domaine here is towards the top of the list of those whose wines show the aromatics quite strongly." Nanson, Dec 2022 / Dec 2023

Confidence range for DRC Échezeaux 2014: 70–75 % whole cluster (HIGH), possibly up to 80 % for this parcel (the 2013 "70–80 % depending on the parcel" and Nanson's 2014 note that the Échezeaux showed "Whole-cluster flavour… some whole-clusters in the finishing flavour too"). The myth of "100 % whole cluster at DRC" is explicitly corrected by Bertrand de Villaine for the pre-2015 era; 2015 was the exception, not the rule.

Technique detail (Nanson 2015): whole clusters go in first, at the bottom of the vat, held on a net above the base to aid drainage; the destemmed fraction is added on top from a destemmer mounted on the vat. This "stems at the bottom" layering is exactly the technique Louis-Michel Liger-Belair described to Craig Williams in 2011 for his own small whole-cluster additions — a nice symmetry for the theme.

The rest of the regime

  • Sorting: grapes "sorted into small baskets and individually examined for health on triage tables" (C&B). In 2014, "Nicolas Jacob and his team effected meticulous selection, in particular eliminating the raisined berries from the heat spike in June and the dried up rot berries of August, as well as the bigger, less ripe grapes" (Brett-Smith, who "ate some of them in the La Tâche vineyard" in Dec 2016). HIGH
  • Cold pre-fermentation maceration 3–6 days (Noblet, 2010). Indigenous yeasts (Noblet; general). Fermentation in open wooden vats MED — widely reported; not confirmed in a source fetched here, length ~3 weeks (2015: 21–23 days). Extraction: by 2018 "a little foulage, more remontage, less pigeage – 'we're looking more for delicacy'" (Bertrand de Villaine to Nanson, Dec 2018) — earlier vintages including 2014 used more pigeage MED. Chaptalisation: historically "less chaptalization (none in ripe years)" (Janssen 2013); 2014 NOT FOUND.
  • Oak: 100 % new François Frères barrels ("All the following wines were tasted from new François Frères barrels" — Nanson, Nov 2014, DRC 2013s; "We always taste from new barrels, but there are some older barrels in the mix too" — Dec 2018). HIGH on François Frères; MED on strictly 100 % new for Échezeaux in 2014 — the 2018 comment implies a few older barrels exist in the cellar. The brief's memory that DRC buys its own Tronçais/Fontainebleau wood and dries it before FF coopers it was NOT VERIFIED in any source consulted — do not state it as fact. Noblet's line on oak (2010): "un support, comme un soutien-gorge pour une belle poitrine."
  • Élevage: "the standard 16–18 month élevage" (Nanson Nov 2014); racked once ("The wines have already been racked for the one and only time prior to bottling" — Nanson 2005, of the 2004s). The 2014 brochure: "the wines were put to cask for the long secondary fermentation with final bottling in spring 2016."
  • Fining/filtration: in 2018 "we will make a very fine filtration where needed" (Bertrand). 2014 NOT FOUND (assume unfined, light filtration only if needed).
  • 2014 Échezeaux specifics (Corney & Barrow vineyard table, 2014 vintage): average vine age 30; average production 1,340 doz; 2014 production 1,443 doz (17,316 bottles); yield 27.8 hl/ha; harvest 24, 25, 26 September 2014 — the very last of the domaine's vineyards to be picked; bottled 25 & 29 February and 3 March 2016; 13 % abv (Tribeca label data). "Vinifications under Bernard Noblet's serene and stoic eye were naturally easy." HIGH
  • Chef de cave: Bernard Noblet (son of André Noblet, who preceded him) through the 2014 vintage; Alexandre Bernier — "with the domaine for 16 years" as of Nov 2025 — succeeded him (Nanson, Nov 2025; exact hand-over date paywalled — the brief's "2018" is plausible but NOT VERIFIED). MED

4. The 2014 vintage at DRC — and this wine's reviews

Aubert de Villaine / Adam Brett-Smith's "2014: A Play in Three Acts" (C&B brochure, Feb 2017)

  • Epigraph from Aubert: "Jamais plus qu'en ce millésime 2014 ne se sont vérifiées les deux grandes règles du jeu… la prise de risque, obligatoire, et ce qu'on peut appeler la chance, mais qui est peut-être tout simplement le sourire de la vigne au vigneron qui a su la respecter… et l'aimer !" ("Never more than in this 2014 vintage have the two great rules of the game revealed themselves… the requirement for risk and what some might call luck, but which is perhaps simply the vines' way of smiling at the winemaker as a sign of gratitude for the respect he showed… and love!")
  • Act I: "one of the loveliest – and driest – springs in memory" after "a wet, dark and exceptionally mild winter"; early flowering, "hardly any coulure… but some millerandage."
  • Act II: 28 June — "Spectacular storms erupted along the whole of the Côte, with hail hitting Beaune, Pommard and Volnay with particular savagery and side-swiping Vosne-Romanée," followed by "a canicular spike of heat that literally roasted some of the young berries whose protective leaf canopy had been shredded." July/August "cool, even cold, with very little sun and… over 100 mm of rain in July alone"; rot appeared early August; grass growth between the vines "curiously… had a benevolent influence… as it metered the amount of water available." Nanson's on-the-day report (30 Jun 2014): "hailstones of 2–4 cm diameter in Vosne-Romanée – the Clos de Vougeot and Romanée St.Vivant lost up to 25 %, maybe more in Echézeaux. Much of the rest of Vosne lost more like 15 %." Bertrand de Villaine (Nov 2014): "The hail was not such a great problem in this part of the Côte de Nuits; fortunately it was the grapes that were damaged, not the plants – we lost 15–20 %, which was more like a green harvest for us."
  • Act III: "From the beginning of September the blessed North wind arrived… By the end of the second week of September the Pinot Noir was fully ripe and harvesting began on the hill of Corton on the 16th September with La Tâche… on the 17th"; storm on the afternoon of the 19th (no hail) halted picking; RC picked 19th, Montrachet 22nd, GE 23–24th, Échezeaux 24–26th; "Harvesting was completed on the 26th September… dry, warm and bright." Grapes "magnificent, thick skinned, deep coloured and sweet to taste."
  • Yields: Corton 25, Échezeaux 27.8, Richebourg 29.75, RSV 31.6, La Tâche 32, GE 32.5, RC 32.6 hl/ha — "After four consecutively short and/or difficult vintages, 2014 marks the intermittent turn of fortune's wheel." Bertrand: "2014 is a more regular volume than 2013 – double!" Total RSV 1,756 doz, Richebourg 1,160, RC 657.
  • Aubert de Villaine to Adam Lechmere (Wine-Searcher, Feb 2017): "2014 is very different to 2015, which had very high phenolics. In 2014, phenolic ripeness came much later. The wines have purity, but they are going to take a long time to reach their full maturity." How long? "About 15 years." And (via Jukes): "It's a phenolic maturity that gives freshness and a minerality in the sense that it makes the wine a channel for the terroir." Bertrand (Dec 2018): "2017 is a really good vintage that will allow you to drink earlier – 2014 allows that too – we drink quite a few of those now."
  • No Cuvée Duvault-Blochet was released in 2014 (Nanson, Dec 2016). Release: Corney & Barrow, February 2017, £937.50 per 3 bottles in bond (≈ £312/bt), magnum £635; US importer Wilson Daniels.

Reviews of DRC Échezeaux 2014

  • Corney & Barrow / Adam Brett-Smith (Feb 2017): 17.5+ /20, drink 2020–2030 (typo "2010" in brochure): "Interestingly this was the very last vineyard to be harvested… In 2014 this is a very 'D'Artagnan' Échézeaux – to the 'Porthos' of Richebourg… from its profound, almost opaque ruby colour to a nose that is intensely fresh, with pure black fruits and a stony, almost austere perfume. The palate is darkly rich, very straightforward, freshly powerful with a grippy structure, a dry mid palate but a good, grunty finish. Heart on sleeve. I suspect this will become very grown up indeed over the next 10–15 years. 'Costaud' (I scribbled in the margin)." He adds: "apart from the Échézeaux this [RC] has the deepest colour of all the crus."
  • Bill Nanson (from barrel, 14 Nov 2015): "A spicy Vosne nose. Whole-cluster flavour and lots of width matched by lots of complexity. A hint of herby character, really complex and interestingly flavoured, some whole-clusters in the finishing flavour too."
  • Neal Martin, Wine Advocate (2 Feb 2017): 92, drink 2019–2035: "picked on 24, 25 and 26 September at 27.8 hectoliters per hectare. It offers an expressive bouquet, more vivacious than the 2012 Echézeaux, with scents of black plum, raspberry preserve and subtle limestone aromas… a gentle, slightly leafy quality… a cheeky pinch of black pepper… Probably one of the most approachable 2014s from the domaine, and just as likely to be drunk too young!"
  • Jancis Robinson (2 Feb 2017): 18.5/20, drink 2029–2050: colour "darker and a little denser," aromas "dark-red fruit and more wild," "Firm, compact but supple tannins… an imprint rather than drawing you along by the fingertips," "smoky oak spice" turning "earthy, more savoury" with air.
  • Stephen Tanzer, Vinous (2017): 93: "Full, dark red. Sappy scents of black raspberry, violet and licorice are higher-pitched and considerably less earthy than those of the Corton. Chewy and penetrating in the mouth, conveying strong energy and a restrained sweetness to the tactile flavors of plum, berries, spices and pepper."
  • Antonio Galloni, Vinous (12 Mar 2024): 93+: "shockingly primary. Dark, brooding and introspective, the 2014 is in no mood to show all its cards today… whole cluster savoriness and incisive tannins… needs another five years at a minimum… a very serious Echézeaux."
  • Allen Meadows, Burghound: 94 (score cited by Thatcher's Wine; text NOT FOUND). For context, Meadows on RC 2014: "not going to be one of the all-time great vintages" (94).
  • Matthew Jukes (2022): 18/20, 2025–2038: "Pure, clean, aromatic, fiercely proud and very close to being profound, this is a smart Échézeaux and its floral appeal lightens the usual animal notes found here and gives it a new complexion. Balanced, smooth and slick."
  • Adam Lechmere, Wine-Searcher (Feb 2017): "Powerful blackcurrant nose leads to instant grip on the palate with concentrated, almost-ripe blackberry and briar aromas… the palate now is dry, austere, releasing a parsimonious trickle of juice."
  • Pierre Weber / Vinum Petri: 92 (Tastingbook). CellarTracker community average 94 (via Thatcher's).
  • Drinking window consensus: approachable now (Martin, Bertrand de Villaine) but critics who tasted from bottle in 2017–2024 (Jancis, Galloni) see a closed, tannic phase until ~2027–2030. Serving in Oct 2026 at age 12: expect the "whole-cluster savoury/herby" register to be prominent — exactly the theme. No premox/TCA/bottle-variation issues reported. HIGH
  • Market (Sept 2026): US retail $2,450 (Thatcher's) to $3,275 (Tribeca, future arrival); release was ≈ £312/bt IB. Auction data NOT FOUND (sites blocked). MED

5. Stories, people, connections (DRC)

  • Aubert de Villaine (b. 1939, Rennes): joined the domaine in 1965; father Henri handed him the reins in 1971; co-gérant from 1974 with Lalou Bize-Leroy until her 1992 removal, then with Henry-Frédéric Roch (d. late 2018, aged 56); nephew Bertrand de Villaine presented as successor in 2009 and co-director now; Perrine Fenal (Lalou Bize-Leroy's daughter, on the supervisory board since 2004) replaced Roch on 23 Jan 2019 — "2019 was Perrine's first harvest" (Joanna Simon). Aubert stepped down to an advisory role in 2022. Judge at the 1976 Judgment of Paris; Decanter Man of the Year 2010; with wife Pamela runs Domaine A. & P. de Villaine in Bouzeron (drove creation of the Bouzeron AOC, 1998); director of Hyde de Villaine (HdV), Napa, founded 2000. HIGH — Wikipedia EN/FR, Wilson Daniels
  • UNESCO Climats — the Tastevin-relevant story. Aubert de Villaine presided over the Association pour l'inscription des Climats du vignoble de Bourgogne; the procedure began 2009 (FR Wikipedia; other sources say the idea dates from 2006–07), the file was deposited 2012, and the Climats were inscribed 4 July 2015 (39th session), covering the Côte de Nuits/Côte de Beaune vineyards plus the historic centres of Dijon and Beaune. HIGH on dates; MED on his exact presidency dates — Guillaume d'Angerville later took the chair. The Château du Clos de Vougeot — the Confrérie's seat and the Cistercian heart of this landscape — is the emblematic monument of the inscription; Échezeaux and Grands Échezeaux are literally the monks' vineyards outside the Clos wall.
  • Bernard Noblet, chef de cave through 2014 — "a very tall man with a slow, ironic way of expressing himself… His father worked at DRC before him." Stems as the "skeleton" of the bunch; Brett-Smith's "serene and stoic eye." Succeeded by Alexandre Bernier. HIGH/MED
  • Aubert's "monastic" word for the 2014 Richebourg (Brett-Smith: "even a little monastic (Aubert's word)"), and his Montrachet-harvest line: "Ce fut un moment de grande intensité, nous étions seuls, il n'y avait autour de nous que les corbeaux et nous… !"
  • Provenance warning the domaine attached to the 2014 release: "it is imperative that, without absolute certainty as to the authenticity and origin of the bottles they are offered, they buy wines only through our official distributor" — relevant to a committee of collectors (Kurniawan-era context).
  • Henri Jayer vs DRC, the Échezeaux duel (Herwig Janssen, 2013, on 1990s): "Henri Jayer = early picking, cold maceration, chaptalization, complete destemming, 16 to 18 months of aging… mostly from the Cruots part of Echezeaux. DRC = later picking, cold maceration, less chaptalization (none in ripe years), whole cluster, 18 months and more aging… mostly from the Poulailleres part of Echezeaux… in the top years, the stems do bring complexity and freshness to a wine." Liger-Belair's Échezeaux is 54 % Cruots and its maker is a Jayer protégé — so this flight re-stages the Jayer/DRC argument in 2014.
  • Links to Liger-Belair: (i) Louis-Michel "initially used the same horses as Domaine de la Romanée-Conti" when he began horse-ploughing in 2002 (Nanson 2004); (ii) La Romanée and Romanée-Conti are separated only by "a strangely meandering track, wide enough for a tractor"; (iii) both domaines were still pruning their top vineyards in March — "DRC, Louis-Michel Liger-Belair, and not surprisingly Domaine Leroy" (Winehog); (iv) the Liger-Belair family sold at auction in 1933 the very year DRC bought La Tâche (C&B: "La Tâche was bought in 1933"); the Liger-Belairs had pieced together "the entirety of what was La Tâche" (Nanson 2004). HIGH
  • Leflaive link: Wilson Daniels imports DRC, Domaine Leflaive and Faiveley in the same Burgundy book; C&B's 2016 DRC brochure notes DRC's Corton-Charlemagne comes via Domaine Leflaive-related arrangements ("of Domaine Leflaive, just two barrels") — the Bonneau du Martray lease (from 2017) was the vehicle; not relevant to 2014. MED
  • Confrérie/Tastevinage: DRC does not submit to the Tastevinage; any personal Confrérie rank of Aubert de Villaine NOT FOUND.

WINE B — Domaine du Comte Liger-Belair, Échezeaux Grand Cru 2014

1. The vineyard / climat

See Wine A, section 1 — same appellation. The points that matter for Liger-Belair's parcels: - Les Cruots ou Vignes Blanches (3.16–3.28 ha total): southern sector on the Vosne edge adjoining Les Suchots. Domaine text: "Cruots indicates a stony ground and Vigne Blanche an old plantation of white grapes." Soil per the domaine's geological survey: "the red-brown topsoil provides a rocky surface of approximately 10 to 20 percent… The eastern part of the parcel rests on the fragments of columnal crinoids, sometimes known as Indian beads. A fault puts them in contact with Comblanchien limestone in the central part, and a second fault is flush with Prémeaux limestone at the eastern end." Henri Jayer's Échezeaux came largely from here; he called it "the best terroir of the appellation" (Curtis 2025). HIGH - Les Champs Traversins (3.58 ha): "at the top of the hillside"; "vines planted across the others"; "The steep slope associated with the abundance of angular gravel, of average centimetre size, composed of Comblanchien limestone, with fairly pale soil, allows for identification of the ulative grezes, cryoclastic skree which formed during the periods of periglacial activity in the Plio-Quaternary era." Dujac, Jacques Prieur, A.-F. Gros, Lamarche, Jadot, Bichot also here. HIGH - Clos Saint-Denis (1.66–1.80 ha): the low southern limit along Suchots; the domaine's 0.0312 ha sliver here is "last – and least" (Winehog). DRC is the largest owner in this lieu-dit. HIGH

2. Liger-Belair's plot within Échezeaux

  • Total 0.6157 ha in Pinot Noir, in six plots across three lieux-dits: Cruots ou Vignes Blanches 0.3299 ha (two plots "just north of the center of the vineyard running from top to bottom in the climat" — Winehog), Champs Traversins 0.2546 ha, Clos Saint-Denis 0.0312 ha. HIGH — domaine website; Nanson 2015; Winehog 2020. (Jockovino 2010 gives rounded 0.4 + 0.2 ha; Decanter 2022 describes the wine as "a blend of Les Cruots and Les Champs Traversins.")
  • The domaine's own gloss: "Historically these two climats are among the best estimated within the appellation. They complement each other admirably: a thick soil, rich in Champs Traversins, stony and limestone in Cruots. Charming in its youth, this wine often 'fades' for a few years then comes back in strength, with brilliance, after five to ten years. A thousand graces on a very firm structure: iron fist, velvet glove." HIGH
  • Vine age (domaine, stated for 2017): 40 % 82 years old, 50 % 62 years old, 10 % between 22 and 42 years ⇒ in 2014 roughly 40 % planted c. 1935 (79 yo), 50 % c. 1955 (59 yo), 10 % 1975–1995. Nanson's 2015 version: "40 % of the vines were 80 years-old, 50 % were 60 years-old, and 10 % between 20 and 40." Rootstock/clonal detail NOT FOUND (the domaine's Colombière is massal; Nanson 2019: "Massale selection with multiple plantings – 'great material'"). HIGH on ages
  • Acquisition: 2006, "the long-term acquisition of the Domaine Lamadon vines" (Nanson) — the domaine's history page: "In 2006 he had the opportunity to rent 5.5 hectares from the family of one of their former share-croppers, the Lamadon of Vosne-Romanée. This included a large parcel of Echezeaux, Vosne-Romanée Premier Cru Les Suchots, Les Petits Monts and Les Brûlées, Nuits-Saint-Georges Premier Cru Aux Cras, Vosne-Romanée and Nuits-Saint Georges." Decanter (Curtis 2023) calls it a métayage agreement. First Liger-Belair Échezeaux vintage: 2006. So the 2014 was the 9th vintage. HIGH
  • Later additions (not in the 2014 bottle): a plot of Échezeaux du Dessus from 2020 (Winehog, Apr 2020) and further Échezeaux (Champs Traversins per Curtis) plus first Grands Échezeaux (0.30 ha), Malconsorts, La Croix Rameau, Aux Réas from Nathalie Pacareau (ex-Domaine Lamarche) from the 2022 harvest, 2.65 ha in all (Winehog Dec 2021). HIGH
  • Average production: 6.5 barrels ≈ 1,950 bottles (domaine); Nanson 2015 "around 1,950 bottles." 2014 exact bottle count NOT FOUND (2014 yields "30 hl/ha (roughly)" domaine-wide ⇒ ~1,850 bottles from 0.6157 ha — consistent). HIGH/MED
  • Farming: horse-ploughing since 2002 ("Initially he used the same horses as Domaine de la Romanée-Conti"); no herbicides; biodynamic on the whole domaine since 2008; Ecocert-certified organic 2011, Biodyvin biodynamic certification 2012 (domaine site; Curtis 2023; Nanson Nov 2016 "certified biodynamic since about 2012"). Treatments with light machinery, stopped after bunch closure (~2nd week July); "No anti-rot treatment is carried out on the Domaine, since it is an admission of failure"; sexual-confusion pheromones commune-wide. Harvest in 14 kg perforated crates, "all the harvesting is completed in around 1½ days only with friends" (2004). HIGH

3. Winemaking for THIS cuvée — and in 2014

WHOLE CLUSTER — every figure found

Vintage / wine Figure Source (date)
2003–c.2009, all wines "Grapes entirely destemmed and conveyed uncrushed to the fermenting vat by belt" Jockovino/Clive Coates-derived profile (Mar 2010)
Policy c. 2013 "all bunches are de-stemmed and gently crushed" Jeannie Cho Lee MW (Nov 2013)
2007 Chaumes 15 %; Aux Brûlées 100 % Allen Meadows/Burghound, as tabulated by Peter Chiu on WineBerserkers (Dec 2012)
2008 Brûlées 25 % idem
2009 Brûlées "high percentage" idem
2010 Brûlées 100 %; Chaumes "a little addition of whole clusters… since 2010 – LM suggesting that provides a 'little lift'" Meadows (idem); Nanson (Nov 2014)
Nov 2011 visit "L M indicated that he does add stems, in the form of whole clusters, usually a small percentage in general, placed in the bottom of the fermentation vat… varies according to vineyard and vintage." Craig Williams, WineBerserkers (Dec 2012)
2012 visit "One [change] is more use of whole clusters in some of the wines, the other is the use of oak." Steen Öhman, Winehog (2012), quoted by Larry Link
Dec 2012 "for ages I always told LM that his wines smell of stems – and he said 'funny, because I don't use them'… Only the Brûlées… has occasionally seen stems – it's LM's 'experimental' cuvée" Bill Nanson, WineBerserkers
2013 Suchots 15 %; Chaumes "a little"; Brûlées cuvée "essentially is a test-bed for ideas like the incorporation of whole-clusters" Nanson (Nov 2014; Aug 2015)
2015 (the year after ours) "He vinified his crus with about 20 % whole clusters, which he described as a bit more than usual." Stephen Tanzer, Vinous report on the 2015s (Nov 2016 visit), quoted by Larry Link
2015 Chaumes "About 15–20 % positive selection of whole-clusters to give a lift to this limestone 1er"; Suchots "Same amount… as Chaumes" Nanson (Nov 2016)
2016 Chaumes some; Suchots "A little whole cluster here too"; Petits Monts "never whole cluster as there's already the brightness that I'm looking for" Nanson (Nov 2017)
May 2017 William Kelley: "They are entirely destemmed"… then, corrected: "He certainly used to destem 100 %, and that is reflected on his website… The winemaking in general is / was very Jayer-iste." Gerhard P.: "over the last 9–10 vintages not all cuvees – and not always – are entirely destemmed… Louis-Michel is going to use more and more whole-clusters, but very cautiously." WineBerserkers
2018 Village Vosne: "faintly whole-cluster spiced is the impression yet this is all destemmed" Nanson (Nov 2019)
Current site text Grapes "then destemmed in whole or in part, depending on the cuvées and vintages" liger-belair.fr (2019 site)
2023 "Much of the Pinot is destemmed, but the proportion of whole clusters is slowly increasing" Charles Curtis MW, Decanter (Oct 2023)

Confidence range for Liger-Belair Échezeaux 2014: 0–15 % whole cluster, MEDIUM confidence; best single estimate: fully destemmed or with a token ≤10–15 % of whole bunches laid at the bottom of the vat. Reasoning: (a) the only cuvée-specific Échezeaux figure ever published is NOT FOUND — every documented percentage concerns Chaumes, Suchots and the experimental Brûlées; (b) Tanzer's report that the 2015 crus got "about 20 %… a bit more than usual" caps the 2014 crus below 20 %; (c) the domaine's Petits Monts is explicitly "never," and Kelley/Cho Lee/Coates all describe a 100 %-destemming house; (d) Nanson's 2014 Échezeaux note (Dec 2015) mentions no stems, whereas he flags them whenever present. The safe line at table: "Liger-Belair's 2014 Échezeaux is essentially a destemmed, Jayer-school wine; if there are any stems it is a small positive selection, and Louis-Michel was only then beginning to raise the proportion in his crus."

The paradox for the theme: tasters routinely perceive stems in Liger-Belair wines that have none — "Funny that, for ages I always told LM that his wines smell of stems – and he said 'funny, because I don't use them'" (Nanson, 2012); Don Cornutt: "His wines really have a whole cluster type of aromatic profile… Very similar to the aromatics of a Leroy wine." One explanation offered by the domaine's own regime: a full week of cold maceration below 15 °C on uncrushed berries "allows an aromatic extraction, essential to the wines of the Domaine."

The rest of the regime (domaine website "Winemaking & Ageing," plus Tanzer/Nanson)

  • "Louis-Michel Liger-Belair considers that when the grapes are on the sorting table at the entrance to the vat house, 95 % of the work is done. The remaining 5 % is not a recipe but the application of certain simple principles and a dose of intuition."
  • 14 kg perforated crates; sorting table with 8 sorters; destemmed "in whole or in part"; conveyor belts, never pumped; light sulphiting in vat; cold-soaked below 15 °C for one week; indigenous fermentation starting "in a few days"; fermentation ~10 days (up to 25 days in vat in 2015 — Tanzer); "concluded if necessary with a light chaptalization, divided in two or three days depending on the vintage" (2015: 13–13.5 % "without chaptalization" — Tanzer; 2014: natural 12.4–12.9° per Louis-Michel, so a light chaptalisation in 2014 is plausible — NOT CONFIRMED); "pumping over is carried out, as well as some punching down" — Tanzer 2016: "pumpovers early each morning but only two punchdowns at the beginning of the fermentations – 'and that was it'"; Curtis: punch-downs "greatly reduced" by 2009; Henri Jayer consulted in the 2003 heatwave. HIGH
  • Small vertical press; free-run and press blended; ~10 days settling in tank; barrelled by gravity.
  • Oak: "All the wines are aged in new barrels coming from three barrel-makers and three different forests" — i.e. 100 % new oak for every cuvée including village wines ("the whole cellar is new oak here, but some larger barrels, more and more Seguin-Moreau being used" — Nanson Nov 2017; Tanzer 2016: "all of the wines I tasted were in 100 % new barrels, which is now an exception in Burgundy"). Louis-Michel's justification (to Tanzer): "even with one-year-old barrels, my wines are too reduced; there's not enough exchange with air." Nanson 2004: 100 % new oak "because Louis-Michel prefers long aging without racking… he needs the cleanest fruit and barrels to avoid any taint." Coopers other than Seguin-Moreau NOT FOUND (2004: "two different barrels; both from the same maker, the wood coming from different forests"). HIGH on 100 % new; MED on coopers
  • No racking during élevage; no SO2 addition until the racking before bottling; malo before or after the first winter; racked by gas pressure (not pumped) into tank 10–18 months after harvest; sulphited; "2 to 3 moons" in tank under inert gas; no fining, no filtration; gravity bottling; corks oriented one by one; shipped in wooden cases with straw and silk paper. HIGH
  • 2014 specifics: Louis-Michel to Nanson (Dec 2015): "We began our harvest on September 15 with serious phenolic ripeness – 12.4–12.9° and there was super deep colour right from the beginning. The first part of aging was difficult, the malos being long and late." Domaine yields "30 hl/ha in 2014 (roughly)" vs 22 (2013), 25 (2012), 28 (2011). The 2014 wines were sulphured "first time, the day before racking" (village Vosne, Dec 2015); the 2014 La Romanée "was bottled 2 months later" than usual (Nanson, Nov 2016). Exact Échezeaux 2014 bottling date NOT FOUND (the pattern implies spring–summer 2016). Nine days earlier than DRC's Échezeaux pick (24–26 Sept). HIGH

4. The 2014 vintage at Liger-Belair — and this wine's reviews

  • Louis-Michel (Dec 2015): "I'm super happy with 2014 vs expectation, as we have lovely wines now. A little hail that affected quantity but not quality." Nanson's verdict on the range: "exemplary wines at this domaine… Last year I used the word 'exemplary' for the 2014s" (Nov 2016), and a collector on WineBerserkers: "the '14's are so super pure." The hail context: Cruots/Clos St-Denis sit on the Vosne edge where Nanson reported ~15 % losses; Échezeaux "maybe more." HIGH
  • Louis-Michel's own drinkability ledger (Nov 2017): "2005 – still closed… 2007 – I really like now… 2008 – open… 2009 – the acidity is coming back; 2010 – is great; 2011 – is also drinking great; 2012 – is shut down." The domaine's own warning on Échezeaux: "Charming in its youth, this wine often 'fades' for a few years then comes back in strength… after five to ten years" — at 12 years the 2014 should be past the dip.

Reviews of Liger-Belair Échezeaux 2014

  • Neal Martin, Wine Advocate (tasted Nov 2016, from bottle): 95: "The 2014 Echézeaux Grand Cru delivered in barrel, but more crucially, would it deliver in bottle… when you might be able to drink it? Well, the answer is yes. It has a very pure blueberry, raspberry and mint-tinged bouquet that opens nicely in the glass, almost Suchots-like in style. The palate is medium-bodied with fine tannin and a silver bead of acidity. It feels saline in the mouth with superb cohesion towards the finish. Everything is in its right place here and although I can see this closing down for some time, it should ultimately turn into an outstanding Echezeaux."
  • Allen Meadows, Burghound: 95 (score via Thatcher's; text NOT FOUND).
  • Stephen Tanzer, Vinous (2016/17): 91: "Good ruby-red. Wilder on the nose than the Reignots, offering very ripe scents of plum, dark chocolate, brown spices and game. Then big, rich and chocolatey in the mouth, a bit in the style of its 2015 sibling but quite backward and hard to judge today… I'd be more confident about its ability to reward long cellaring if it showed more of the berry intensity of the other 2014 crus I tasted at this address."
  • Bill Nanson (from barrel, Dec 2015): "Really great colour. Here is more width and amplitude, perhaps energy too. Everything is in place to be a better wine down the line, but today the Reignots has the edge, despite a hint more length here. Still, simply excellent…" (He rated the 2014 Reignots "Quite exceptional.")
  • Steen Öhman, Winehog (14 Apr 2023, from bottle): 93–94, drink from 2027: "A truly hedonistic wine – lively and enjoyable… still too young, although the nose is beautiful and complex. The palate is quite youthful still… It will improve with further cellaring as the palate adds silky complexity."
  • CellarTracker community 94 (via Thatcher's). Jancis/Jasper Morris notes NOT FOUND (paywalled).
  • Bottle variation / oxidation flag: a WineBerserkers poster (May 2017) noted the domaine's 2008 Échezeaux "has a concerning number of recent notes suggesting oxidation," and a 2010 Colombière magnum that was "very much bricked" — countered by a long-time buyer ("no concerns at all… the 1er and GCs are meant to age for decades"). Given the no-SO2-until-racking, 100 %-new-oak, unfiltered regime, check the 2014 bottle's fill and colour on the night; no such reports found for the 2014 specifically. MED
  • Market (Sept 2026): Thatcher's $2,500–$2,995/bt. Release price NOT FOUND. MED

5. Stories, people, connections (Liger-Belair)

  • General Louis Liger-Belair (Napoleonic general; "Liger is actually a Latin derivative of Loire which is where we were from originally") bought the Château de Vosne in 1815; with his adopted son/nephew Comte Louis-Charles (who married Ludovie Marey, of the négociant family "owners and wine merchants in Burgundy since 1720") he assembled La Romanée, La Tâche, La Grande Rue, large parts of Malconsorts, Chaumes, Reignots, Brûlées, Suchots, Vaucrains, Clos de Vougeot and Cras, Chambolle, Morey, Chambertin — 40 ha (Nanson) to "more than 60 hectares" (domaine) at Comte Edgar's death. The négociant house C. Marey & Comte Liger-Belair (formed 1852) later passed to Boisset; Louis-Michel recovered the name in 2014/2015 for Hospices de Beaune barrels and regional wines. HIGH
  • The 1933 auction: Comte Henri died 1924 leaving a widow and ten children; when she died in 1931 two children were minors and the law forced a sale — 31 August 1933, judicial auction at the Vosne town hall: La Tâche, Malconsorts, Brûlées went. Canon Just and Comte Michel bought back La Romanée, Reignots and Chaumes and handed them to local sharecroppers (the Foreys, later Régis Forey, for vineyard work and élevage; Bouchard Père & Fils marketed La Romanée until the 2005 vintage — "2002–2005 of La Romanée when Bouchard Père et Fils made half the wines and me, the other half"). Louis-Michel's father, General Henry Liger-Belair, did not farm. HIGH
  • Louis-Michel (b. 1970 per Club Oenologique; 1973 per Decanter — sources disagree LOW): "Ever since I was eight years old, I wanted to live here and make wine"; his father's condition: only if he became an engineer. Agricultural engineering (Toulouse), master's in wine business, oenology diploma (Dijon), officer school at Saumur; worked at Far Niente (Napa) and at biodynamic Rossignol-Trapet in Gevrey; Henri Jayer as mentor ("a clear inspiration from his old mentor Henri Jayer" — Öhman; "very Jayer-iste" — Kelley; consulted Jayer in 2003 — Curtis). Married Constance in 1999 introducing himself as "an impoverished Burgundy peasant." First vintage 2000 (1.5 ha), Reignots and La Romanée from 2002, Lamadon lease 2006, Clos des Grandes Vignes bought 2012, Clos de Vougeot (0.4 ha, 10-year fruit contract on organic vines, right of first refusal — "this parcel of land was previously owned by the Liger-Belairs in the late 1800s – Liger-Belair was one of the 5 families that bought the Clos de Vougeot, and had 8 hectares in the Clos at that time") from 2015; new cuverie behind the château (2017–19); Pacareau parcels 2022; consulting at Pommard Clos de la Commaraine from 2018. Quotes: "Making a wine is much more than winemaking. It is 250 small decisions"; "To know where you are going, you must know where you've come from"; "To make great wine you have to know when to take risks"; "All vines and all wines deserve the same care, from village appellation to grand cru." Becky Wasserman: "It was evident from Louis-Michel Liger-Belair's first vintage that he was going to be a star." Children Henry (2002), Brune (2003), Pia (2006) — Henry and Brune now at the domaine (Winehog 2025). HIGH
  • Bicentenary, 12 June 2015: 65/68-vintage La Romanée vertical (1911–2014), dinner by Pascal Barbot (L'Astrance), book Les Comtes Liger-Belair, 200 ans au service de La Romanée — the tasting at which Nanson tasted the 2006–2013 Échezeaux run ("2010: Wide, lush, complex and enveloping… Only a little oak in the finish to distract"). HIGH
  • Cousin Thibault Liger-Belair (Nuits): "their great-grandfathers were brothers, but their respective wine businesses separated their operations in the 1930s"; Thibault is an avowed whole-cluster user ("30 % for Nuits La Charmotte, 50 % for Gevrey… none for Vosne Aux Réas… 'there's no recipe'") — useful contrast if a Thibault wine is on the table. HIGH
  • Confrérie / Clos de Vougeot connection (strong): the Liger-Belairs were among the families that bought the Clos de Vougeot in 1889 (after the Ouvrard era), holding 8 ha; the Château du Clos de Vougeot became the Confrérie's seat in 1944–45; Louis-Michel has farmed 0.4 ha in the Clos since 2015 and added more in 2022. Tastevinage participation by the domaine: NOT FOUND (none expected). MED on 1889/5-families detail — from Nanson quoting Louis-Michel
  • Connections to DRC: shared horses (2002); the tractor track between La Romanée and Romanée-Conti; both late pruners; DRC bought La Tâche in 1933 as the Liger-Belairs lost it; Perrine Fenal/Leroy vs. Liger-Belair: Crush's 2010 offer compared Louis-Michel's rise to "Lalou-Bize Leroy, who created a whirlwind beginning in the 80s." And in Échezeaux itself, the two domaines are ~400 m apart across Échezeaux du Dessus: DRC on the northern Poulaillères shelf above Grands Échezeaux; Liger-Belair on the southern Cruots/Champs Traversins edge toward Suchots.

Talking points for the flight ("The Importance of Being Stemmed")

  1. Same vintage, opposite doctrines. DRC 2014: ~70–75 % whole cluster, stems netted at the bottom of the vat, picked 24–26 Sept, 27.8 hl/ha, 16–18 months in new François Frères, Noblet's last decade. Liger-Belair 2014: Jayer-school destemming (≤15 % stems at most, probably none in the Échezeaux), week-long cold soak under 15 °C, two punch-downs, 100 % new oak from three coopers, no racking, no SO2 until bottling, picked from 15 Sept at 12.4–12.9°.
  2. The Jayer/DRC Échezeaux argument, re-run. Jayer's Cruots, destemmed and chaptalised, vs. DRC's Poulaillères, whole-cluster and late-picked (Janssen 1990 comparison). Liger-Belair holds 0.33 ha of Cruots and learned at Jayer's knee.
  3. Perception vs. fact. Critics (Nanson, Cornutt, Carnes, Kelley's interlocutors) "smell stems" in Liger-Belair wines that have none — a good blind-tasting trap for the committee.
  4. Bertrand de Villaine's myth-buster: "Most people assume that the domaine only uses whole cluster fermentations, but normally there's about 25–30 % destemming" (2015). 2015 was the first ~100 % whole-cluster year "for many years."
  5. Terroir hierarchy inside Échezeaux: Poulaillères and Cruots are the two lieux-dits most often named as the best (Winehog; Curtis; Jayer) — these two bottles are, arguably, the two best addresses in the cru.
  6. Tastevin hooks: Aubert de Villaine and the 2015 UNESCO Climats inscription; the Liger-Belairs' 8 ha in the Clos de Vougeot (1889) and Louis-Michel's return to the Clos in 2015.
